, where we also summarize the fundamental results and definitions from
[
29
,
30
,
31
]
that we shall use here. Among them we point out the notion of
L
-flatness
\mathcal{F}_{L}^{\infty}({{\nu}}_{0})
, see Definition
A.1
, used in the remainder, and the Écalle-Roussarie compensator
\omega(s;\alpha),
that is a deformation of the logarithm used in the monomial scale in which the asymptotic expansion is given.

The function defined for
s>0
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
by means of

\omega(s;\alpha)\!:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{s^{-\alpha}-1}{\alpha}&\text{if $\alpha\neq 0,$}\\[2.0pt]
-\log s&\text{if $\alpha=0,$}\end{array}\right.

is called the
Écalle-Roussarie compensator
. In the sequel we shall also use the notation
\omega_{\alpha}(s)=\omega(s;\alpha)
.

\square

The asymptotic expansion for
\nu\in\Gamma_{3}
is given in Proposition
3.6
and its proof is of a different nature due to the occurrence of a saddle-node bifurcation at the polycycle.

3.1
Study of
\{D=-1/2,F\in(1/2,1)\}
and
\{F=1/2,D\in(-1,0)\}

Figure
2
shows the phase portrait in the Poincaré disc of the vector field
X_{{\nu}}
in
(\ref{sist_loud})
for
{{\nu}}
varying inside

V\!:=\left\{(D,F)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:D\in(-1,0),\,F\in(0,1)\right\}.

We take transverse sections
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
parametrized by
s\mapsto(1-s,0)
and
s\mapsto(-1/s,0)
with
s>0
, respectively, and

Figure 2:
Phase portrait in the Poincaré disc of
X_{{\nu}}
for
{{\nu}}\in V
, where for convenience the center at
(0,0)
is shifted to the left and the vertical invariant line is
\{x=1\}
.

define
T(s;{{\nu}})
to be the time that spends the solution of
X_{{\nu}}
with initial condition at
(1-s,0)\in\Sigma_{1}
to arrive at
\Sigma_{2}.
Thanks to the symmetry of
X_{{\nu}}
with respect to
\{y=0\}
, it turns out that
the period of the periodic orbit passing through
(1-s,0)\in\Sigma_{1}
is precisely
2T(s;{{\nu}})
. Consequently the emergence/disappearance of critical periodic orbits from
\Pi_{{\nu}}
corresponds to zeros of
T^{\prime}(s;{{\nu}})
bifurcating from
s=0,
more concretely to the number
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(T^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,;{{\nu}}),{{\nu}}_{\star})
as introduced in Definition
2
. A key point to study these bifurcations is that
T(s;{{\nu}})
is the Dulac time associated to the passage through a hyperbolic saddle, which is at infinity (see Figure
2
again).
Therefore we can apply
[
30
, Theorem A]
to obtain the asymptotic expansion of
T(s;{{\nu}})
at
s=0
and use then
[
31
, Theorem A]
to compute its first coefficients
T_{ij}({{\nu}})
. Next result gathers all this information, where

\Gamma(\,\cdot\,)
denotes the gamma function.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
T(s;{{\nu}})
be the Dulac time of the passage from
\Sigma_{1}
to
\Sigma_{2}
of the saddle at infinity of the vector field
X_{{\nu}}
in
(\ref{sist_loud})
for
{{\nu}}\in V
. Then the coefficients
T_{00},

T_{01},

T_{10}
and
T_{20}
in its asymptotic expansion at
s=0
are meromorphic functions on
V
that can be written as

\begin{array}[]{lcl}T_{00}({{\nu}})=\frac{\pi}{2\sqrt{F(D+1)}},&&T_{01}({{\nu}})=\rho_{1}({{\nu}})\frac{\Gamma\big(-\frac{\lambda}{2}\big)}{\Gamma\big(\frac{1-\lambda}{2}\big)},\\[10.0pt]
T_{10}({{\nu}})\textstyle=\rho_{2}({{\nu}})(2D+1)\frac{\Gamma\big(1-\frac{1}{2\lambda}\big)}{\Gamma\big(\frac{3}{2}-\frac{1}{2\lambda}\big)},&&T_{20}({{\nu}})=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{\sqrt{2F}}\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\lambda}\big)}{\Gamma\big(1-\frac{1}{\lambda}\big)}+\rho_{3}({{\nu}})(2D+1),\end{array}

where
\lambda({{\nu}})=\frac{F}{1-F}
is the hyperbolicity ratio of the saddle,

\textstyle\rho_{1}({{\nu}})=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2(1-F)}\left(\frac{F}{D+1}\right)^{\frac{\lambda+1}{2}}\left(\frac{D}{F-1}\right)^{\frac{\lambda}{2}}\text{ and }\rho_{2}({{\nu}})=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2\sqrt{F(D+1)^{3}}},

and
\rho_{3}
is an analytic function on
V\cap\left\{\frac{2}{3}<F<1\right\}.
In addition the following holds:

(a)

If
{{\nu}}_{0}\in V\cap\left\{\frac{2}{3}<F<1\right\}
then, for all
\upsilon>0
small enough,

T(s;{{\nu}})=T_{00}({{\nu}})+T_{10}({{\nu}})s+T_{20}({{\nu}})s^{2}+\mathcal{F}_{L_{0}-\upsilon}^{\infty}({{\nu}}_{0})

with
L_{0}=\min\big(3,\lambda({{\nu}}_{0})\big)
. Moreover
T_{10}(-\frac{1}{2},F)=0
and
T_{20}(-\frac{1}{2},F)>0
for all
F\in(\frac{2}{3},1)
.

(b)

If
{{\nu}}_{0}\in V\cap\left\{\frac{1}{2}<F<\frac{2}{3}\right\}
then, for all
\upsilon>0
small enough,

T(s;{{\nu}})=T_{00}({{\nu}})+T_{10}({{\nu}})s+T_{01}({{\nu}})s^{\lambda}+\mathcal{F}^{\infty}_{2-\upsilon}({{\nu}}_{0}).

Furthermore

T_{10}(-\frac{1}{2},F)=0
and
T_{01}(-\frac{1}{2},F)>0
for all
F\in(\frac{1}{2},\frac{2}{3})
.

(c)

If
{{\nu}}_{0}\in V\cap\left\{F=\frac{2}{3}\right\}
then, for all
\upsilon>0
small enough,

T(s;{{\nu}})=T_{00}({{\nu}})+T_{10}({{\nu}})s+T_{201}^{2}({{\nu}})s^{2}\omega_{2-\lambda}(s)+T_{200}^{2}({{\nu}})s^{2}+\mathcal{F}_{3-\upsilon}^{\infty}({{\nu}}_{0}),

where
T_{200}^{2}
and
T_{201}^{2}
are analytic functions in a neighbourhood of
V\cap\{F=\frac{2}{3}\}
. Moreover

T_{10}(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{2}{3})=0
and
T_{201}^{2}(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{2}{3})\neq 0
.

(d)

If
{{\nu}}_{0}\in V\cap\left\{F=\frac{1}{2}\right\}
then, for all
\upsilon>0
small enough,

T(s;{{\nu}})=T_{00}({{\nu}})+T_{101}^{1}({{\nu}})s\omega_{1-\lambda}(s)+T_{100}^{1}({{\nu}})s+\mathcal{F}_{2-\upsilon}^{\infty}({{\nu}}_{0}),

where

T_{101}^{1}({{\nu}})=-\rho_{4}({{\nu}})(F-1/2)^{2}\text{ and }T_{100}^{1}({{\nu}})=\rho_{5}({{\nu}})(D+1/2)+\rho_{6}({{\nu}})(F-1/2)

for some analytic positive functions
\rho_{i}
in a neighbourhood of
V\cap\{F=\frac{1}{2}\}

with

\rho_{5}(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})=\rho_{6}(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}).

As we already explained, the proof of this result is postponed to Appendix
A
. The monomial order in each one of these asymptotic expansions is with respect to the strict partial order
\prec_{{{\nu}}_{0}}
given in
[
29
]
. Let us recall in its regard that we write
f\prec_{{{\nu}}_{0}}g
in case that

\lim_{(s,{{\nu}})\to(0,{{\nu}}_{0})}\frac{g(s;\lambda)}{f(s;\lambda)}=0.

For the monomials under consideration this order is preserved after derivation with respect to
s
, and so it is the good flatness properties of the remainder.
Thus, as it occurs with the Taylor’s series of an analytic function, an upper bound for the number of zeros of
T^{\prime}(s;{{\nu}})
that can bifurcate from
s=0
follows by identifying the first non-vanishing coefficient in the asymptotic expansion. For the proof and a precise statement of this result, which essentially follows by using the well-known derivation-division algorithm, the reader is referred to
[
29
, Theorem C]
.

3.2
Study of
\{F=2,D\in(-2,0)\}
and
\{D=\mathcal{G}(F):F\in(1,3/2)\}

Figure
3
shows the phase portrait in the Poincaré disc of the vector field
X_{{\nu}}
in
(\ref{sist_loud})
for
{{\nu}}
varying inside

Figure 3:
Phase portrait in the Poincaré disc of
X_{{\nu}}
for
{{\nu}}\in W
with
D<-1
(left) and
D>-1
(right), where the center at
(0,0)
is shifted to the left, the vertical invariant line is
\{x=1\}
and the hyperbola
\{\frac{1}{2}y^{2}-q(x)=0\}
appears in boldface type.

W\!:=\{(D,F)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:F+D>0,\,D<0\text{ and }F>1\}.

In this case the outer boundary of the period annulus of the center at
(0,0)
is
contained in the union of the line at infinity and an invariant hyperbola
\mathcal{C}\!:=\{\frac{1}{2}y^{2}-q(x)=0\}
, where
q(x)=ax^{2}+bx+c
with

a\!:=\frac{D}{2(1-F)},\mbox{ }b\!:=\frac{D-F+1}{(1-F)(1-2F)}\mbox{ and }c\!:=\frac{F-D-1}{2F(1-F)(1-2F)}.

One can verify that if
{{\nu}}\in W
then
q
has two distinct real zeros, that we shall denote by
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
taking
p_{1}<p_{2}.

That being said, we place two transverse sections
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
parametrized by
s\mapsto(p_{1}-s,0)
and
s\mapsto(-1/s,0)
with
s>0
, respectively, and define
T(s;{{\nu}})
to be the time that takes to the solution of
X_{{\nu}}
with initial condition at
(p_{1}-s,0)\in\Sigma_{1}
to arrive at
\Sigma_{2}.
Then
T(s;{{\nu}})
is the Dulac time associated to the passage through a hyperbolic saddle at infinity, so that we can apply the results in
[
30
,
31
]
to obtain its asymptotic expansion at
s=0.
This is important for the proof of Theorem
A
because, exactly as in the previous case, the symmetry of
X_{{\nu}}
with respect to
\{y=0\}
implies that
the period of the periodic orbit passing through
(1-s,0)\in\Sigma_{1}
is
2T(s;{{\nu}})
. With regard to our next result we remark that
\frac{1-p_{2}}{1-p_{1}}<1
for all
{{\nu}}\in W
, which is relevant since the hypergeometric function

{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b;c;\,\cdot\,)
is holomorphic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus[1,+\infty)
, see Appendix
C
.
Let us also mention that
\mathrm{B}(\cdot,\cdot)
is the beta function.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
T(s;{{\nu}})
be the Dulac time of the passage from
\Sigma_{1}
to
\Sigma_{2}
of the saddle at infinity of the vector field
X_{{\nu}}
in
(\ref{sist_loud})
for
{{\nu}}\in W
. Then the coefficients
T_{00},

T_{01},

T_{10}
and
T_{20}
in its asymptotic expansion at
s=0
are meromorphic functions on
W
that can be written as

\displaystyle T_{00}({{\nu}})

\displaystyle\textstyle=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{a}(1-p_{1})}\,{}_{2}F_{1}\big(1,\frac{1}{2};\frac{3}{2};\frac{1-p_{2}}{1-p_{1}}\big),

\displaystyle T_{01}({{\nu}})

\displaystyle\textstyle=\rho_{1}({{\nu}})\mathrm{B}\big(-\lambda,\frac{1}{2}\big),

\displaystyle T_{10}({{\nu}})

\displaystyle\textstyle=\rho_{2}({{\nu}})\mathrm{B}\big(1-\frac{1}{\lambda},-\frac{1}{2}\big){}_{2}F_{1}\big(-1-\frac{1}{\lambda},-\frac{1}{2};\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\lambda};\frac{1-p_{2}}{1-p_{1}}\big)

and

\displaystyle T_{20}({{\nu}})

\displaystyle\textstyle=\rho_{3}({{\nu}})\mathrm{B}\big(1-\frac{2}{\lambda},-\frac{3}{2}\big){}_{2}F_{1}\big(-\frac{2}{\lambda}-3,-\frac{3}{2};-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{\lambda};\frac{1-p_{2}}{1-p_{1}}\big)+\rho_{4}({{\nu}})T_{10}({{\nu}}),

where
\lambda({{\nu}})=\frac{1}{2(F-1)}
is the hyperbolicity ratio of the saddle and, for
i=1,2,3,4
,
\rho_{i}
is an analytic positive function on
W
. In addition the following holds:

(a)

If
{{\nu}}_{0}\in W\cap\left\{1<F<\frac{5}{4}\right\}
then, for all
\upsilon>0
small enough,

T(s;{{\nu}})=T_{00}({{\nu}})+T_{10}({{\nu}})s+T_{20}({{\nu}})s^{2}+\mathcal{F}_{L_{0}-\upsilon}^{\infty}({{\nu}}_{0})

with
L_{0}=\min(3,\lambda({{\nu}}_{0}))
. Moreover
T_{20}({{\nu}})\neq 0
for all

{{\nu}}\in W\cap\left\{1<F<\frac{5}{4}\right\}
such that
T_{10}({{\nu}})=0.

(b)

If
{{\nu}}_{0}\in W\cap\left\{\frac{5}{4}<F<\frac{3}{2}\right\}
then, for all
\upsilon>0
small enough,

T(s;{{\nu}})=T_{00}({{\nu}})+T_{10}({{\nu}})s+T_{01}({{\nu}})s^{\lambda}+T_{20}({{\nu}})s^{2}+\mathcal{F}_{L_{0}-\upsilon}^{\infty}({{\nu}}_{0})

with
L_{0}=\lambda({{\nu}}_{0})+1,
and there exists a unique

{{\nu}}_{\star}\in W\cap\left\{\frac{5}{4}<F<\frac{3}{2}\right\}
such that
T_{10}({{\nu}}_{\star})=0
and

T_{01}({{\nu}}_{\star})=0
.
Furthermore

T_{20}({{\nu}}_{\star})<0
, the gradients of
T_{01}
and
T_{10}
at
{{\nu}}_{\star}
are linearly independent, and

{{\nu}}_{\star}=(D_{\star},\frac{4}{3})
with
D_{\star}=\mathcal{G}(\frac{4}{3})\approx-1.128
.

(c)

If
{{\nu}}_{0}\in W\cap\left\{F=\frac{5}{4}\right\}
then, for all
\upsilon>0
small enough,

T(s;{{\nu}})=T_{00}({{\nu}})+T_{10}({{\nu}})s+T_{201}^{2}({{\nu}})s^{2}\omega_{2-\lambda}(s)+T^{2}_{200}({{\nu}})s^{2}+\mathcal{F}_{3-\upsilon}^{\infty}({{\nu}}_{0}),

where
T_{200}^{2}
and
T_{201}^{2}
are analytic functions in a neighbourhood of

W\cap\{F=\frac{5}{4}\}
. Moreover

T_{10}(D,\frac{5}{4})=0
if and only if
D=-1
, and
T_{201}^{2}(-1,\frac{5}{4})\neq 0
.

(d)

If
{{\nu}}_{0}\in W\cap\left\{F=2\right\}
then, for all
\upsilon>0
small enough,

T(s;{{\nu}})=T_{00}({{\nu}})+T_{01}({{\nu}})s^{\lambda}+T^{\frac{1}{2}}_{101}({{\nu}})s\omega_{1-2\lambda}(s)+T_{100}^{\frac{1}{2}}({{\nu}})s+\mathcal{F}_{3/2-\upsilon}^{\infty}({{\nu}}_{0}),

where
T_{100}^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
T_{101}^{\frac{1}{2}}
are analytic functions in a neighbourhood of
W\cap\{F=2\}
.
Moreover
T_{01}(D,2)=0
for all
D\in(-2,0)
,
T_{101}^{\frac{1}{2}}(D,2)=0
if and only if
D=-\frac{1}{2}
, and
the gradients of
T_{01}
and
T_{101}^{\frac{1}{2}}
are linearly independent at
(-\frac{1}{2},2)
.

The proof of this result is postponed to Appendix
A
.

The asymptotic expansions in Proposition
3.3
were already given in
[
23
, Theorem 3.6]
but only to second order. In that result it is given, among others, the expression of the coefficient
T_{10}({{\nu}})
in terms of a definite improper integral. Furthermore, see
[
23
, Proposition 3.11]
, it is proved by applying the Implicit Function Theorem that the set of those
{{\nu}}\in W_{1}\!:=W\cap\{F<3/2\}
such that
T_{10}({{\nu}})=0
is the graphic of an analytic function
D=\mathcal{G}(F).

This is the function that appears in assertion
(b)
of Proposition
3.3
. Thanks to the results in Appendix
B
we can now identify the improper integral as a hypergeometric function, so that we can write

\textstyle\big\{{{\nu}}\in W_{1}:D=\mathcal{G}(F)\big\}=\big\{{{\nu}}\in W_{1}:{}_{2}F_{1}\big(-1-\frac{1}{\lambda},-\frac{1}{2};\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\lambda};\frac{1-p_{2}}{1-p_{1}}\big)=0\big\},

where
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
with
p_{1}<p_{2}
are the real roots of
q(x)=0
and
\lambda({{\nu}})=\frac{1}{2(F-1)}
.

\square

In the statement of Proposition
3.3
we refer to some positive functions
\rho_{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(W).
Let us mention that in the proof we show that

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\rho_{1}({{\nu}})=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2a}}\frac{(p_{2}-p_{1})^{\frac{1}{2(F-1)}}}{(F-1)(1-p_{1})^{\frac{F}{F-1}}}&&\rho_{2}({{\nu}})=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2a}}\frac{1}{(p_{2}-p_{1})(1-p_{1})}\\[15.0pt]
\rho_{3}({{\nu}})=\frac{3}{8\sqrt{2a}}\frac{1}{(p_{2}-p_{1})^{2}(1-p_{1})}&&\rho_{4}({{\nu}})=\frac{p_{1}-1+2F(p_{2}-p_{1})}{(p_{2}-p_{1})(p_{1}-1)}\end{array}

We do not use the explicit expressions in this paper but they may be relevant for future applications.

\square

3.3
Study of
\{F=1,D\in(-1,0)\}

Our aim in this section is to study the period function of the center at the origin of
X_{{\nu}}
for
{{\nu}}=(D,F)
with
F\approx 1
and
D\in(-1,0)
.

Figure 4:
Phase portrait of
X_{{\nu}}
for
{{\nu}}=(D,F)\in(-1,0)\times(0,+\infty)
in the Poincaré disc with
F\leqslant 1
(left) and
F>1
(right). In this case, contrary to the previous ones, the singularity at infinity for
F=1
is not a hyperbolic saddle but a saddle-node.

To this end we introduce transverse sections
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
parametrized, respectively, by means of

\sigma_{1}(s;{{\nu}})\!:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}(1-s,0)&\text{ if $F\leqslant 1,$}\\[2.0pt]
(p_{1}-s,0)&\text{ if $F>1,$}\end{array}\right.\text{ and }\sigma_{2}(s;{{\nu}})\!:=(-1/s,0),

where recall that
q(x)=a(x-p_{1})(x-p_{2})
with
p_{1}<p_{2}
for
F>1
. One can also check that
\lim_{F\to 1^{+}}p_{1}=1.
For each
{{\nu}}=(D,F)\in(-1,0)\times(0,+\infty)
we define
T(s;{{\nu}})
as the time that spends the solution of
X_{{\nu}}
starting at
\sigma_{1}(s;{{\nu}})\in\Sigma_{1}
to arrive at
\Sigma_{2}.

A key feature of this Dulac time is that the singularity for
F=1
is not a hyperbolic saddle but a saddle-node. Our next result gives the asymptotic expansion of
T(s;{{\nu}})
at
s=0
for
F\approx 1
. We point out that this is relevant for the proof of Theorem
A
because the period of the periodic orbit of
X_{{\nu}}
passing through
\sigma_{1}(s;{{\nu}})\in\Sigma_{1}
is precisely
2T(s;{{\nu}})
due to the symmetry of the vector field.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
T(s;{{\nu}})
be the Dulac time of the passage from
\Sigma_{1}
to
\Sigma_{2}
of the saddle-node unfolding at infinity
\{X_{{\nu}}\}.

Then there is an open neighbourhood
\mathscr{U}
of
(-1,0)\times\{1\}
such that

T(s;{{\nu}})=T_{0}({{\nu}})+T_{1}({{\nu}})s+T_{2}({{\nu}})s^{2}+s^{2}h(s;{{\nu}}),

where
T_{0},T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathscr{C}^{0}(\mathscr{U})
and, setting
\Theta=s\partial_{s}
,
\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}\Theta^{k}h(s;{{\nu}})=0
uniformly on compact sets of
\mathscr{U}
for
k=0,1,2
. Moreover
T_{1}(D,1)=0
if, and only if,
D=-\frac{1}{2}
. Finally
T_{2}(-1/2,1)\neq 0.

To study the saddle-node bifurcation that occurs at infinity we work in the projective plane
\mathbb{RP}^{2}
and perform the change of coordinates

(u,v)=p(x,y)\!:=\left(\frac{1-x}{y},\frac{1}{y}\right).

The meromorphic extension of
X_{{\nu}}
in these coordinates is given by

\tilde{X}_{{\nu}}\!:=p_{*}X_{{\nu}}=\frac{1}{v}\Big(uP(u,v;{{\nu}})\partial_{u}+vQ(u,v;{{\nu}})\partial_{v}\Big)

with

\displaystyle P(u,v;{{\nu}})

\displaystyle=1-F-Du^{2}+(2D+1)uv-(D+1)v^{2}

and

\displaystyle Q(u,v;{{\nu}})

\displaystyle=-F-Du^{2}+(2D+1)uv-(D+1)v^{2}.

Our first goal is to show that we can bring locally the saddle-node unfolding to a convenient normal form in order that we can apply the tools developed in
[
25
]
to study the asymptotic expansion of its Dulac map and Dulac time. With this aim,
some long but easy computations show that the local analytic change of coordinates given by

(z,w)=\Psi(u,v)\!:=\left(\frac{u}{\sqrt{g(u,v)}},\frac{v}{\sqrt{g(u,v)}}\right),

where
g(u,v)\!:={\frac{\left(2D+1\right)}{\left(2F-1\right)D}}uv-{\frac{\left(D+1\right)}{2FD}}{v}^{2}-\frac{1}{2D},
brings the vector field
\tilde{X}_{{\nu}}
to

\bar{X}_{{\nu}}\!:=\frac{1}{w\bar{U}(z,w;{{\nu}})}\bigl(z(z^{2}-2(F-1))\partial_{z}-w(2F-z^{2})\partial_{w}\bigr),

with
\bar{U}(z,w;{{\nu}})\!:=\left({\frac{\left(2D+1\right)}{2(2F-1)}}zw-{\frac{\left(D+1\right)}{4F}}{w}^{2}-\frac{D}{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
.
A technical assumption in order to apply the results from
[
25
]
is that for each
{{\nu}}
the Taylor’s series of
(z,w)\mapsto\bar{U}(z,w;{{\nu}})
at
(0,0)
is absolutely convergent for all
(z,w)\in[-1,1]^{2}
. This is not fulfilled unless we perform a rescaling which is only well defined provided that
{{\nu}}
varies inside a compact subset of
(-1,0)\times(0,+\infty)
and this forces us to work locally. For this reason, as a first step in the proof, we will show a local version of the statement. More concretely, that for each
{{\nu}}_{\star}=(D_{\star},1)
with
D_{\star}\in(-1,0)
there exists an open ball
B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}}=\{{{\nu}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\|{{\nu}}-{{\nu}}_{\star}\|<\delta\}

such that

T(s;{{\nu}})=T_{0}^{{{\nu}}_{\star}}({{\nu}})+T_{1}^{{{\nu}}_{\star}}({{\nu}})s+T_{2}^{{{\nu}}_{\star}}({{\nu}})s^{2}+s^{2}h^{{{\nu}}_{\star}}(s;{{\nu}}),

with
T_{i}^{{{\nu}}_{\star}}
continuous functions on
B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}}
and
\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}\Theta^{k}h^{{{\nu}}_{\star}}(s;\nu)=0
uniformly on
B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}}
for
k=0,1,2
. To begin with we take
\delta>0
small enough so that the closure of
B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}}
is inside
(-1,0)\times(\frac{1}{2},+\infty)
and define

r\!:=\inf\left\{\left|\frac{(D+1)}{DF}w^{2}-\frac{2(2D+1)}{D(2F-1)}zw\right|^{-1/2}:|z|\leqslant 1,|w|\leqslant 1\text{ and }\|{{\nu}}-{{\nu}}_{\star}\|\leqslant\delta\right\},

(6)

which is strictly positive.
The pull-back of
\bar{X}_{{\nu}}
by the rescaling
\rho(z,w)\!:=(rz,rw)
can now be written as in
[
25
, Eq. 13]
because
one can easily verify that

\rho^{*}\bar{X}_{{\nu}}=\frac{1}{wU(z,w;{{\nu}})}\left(z(z^{2}-\varepsilon)\partial_{z}-w(2F/r^{2}-z^{2})\partial_{w}\right)\text{ with $\varepsilon\!:=2(F-1)/r^{2}$}

and where the Taylor’s series of

U(z,w;{{\nu}})\!:=\frac{\bar{U}(rz,rw;{{\nu}})}{r}=\frac{-D}{2r}\left(1+r^{2}\left(\frac{(D+1)}{2FD}w^{2}-\frac{(2D+1)}{D(2F-1)}zw\right)\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}

at
(z,w)=(0,0)
is absolutely convergent for all
(z,w)\in[-1,1]^{2}
and
{{\nu}}\in B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}}
since, on account of
(\ref{p3eq7})
,

r^{2}\left|\frac{(D+1)}{2FD}w^{2}-\frac{(2D+1)}{D(2F-1)}zw\right|\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\text{ for all $(z,w,{{\nu}})\in[-1,1]^{2}\times B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}}.$}

In these new rescaled coordinates, that we still denote by
(z,w)
for simplicity, the period annulus is inside the quadrant
\{w\geqslant 0,z\geqslant\vartheta_{\varepsilon}\}
where

\vartheta_{\varepsilon}\!:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\sqrt{\varepsilon}&\text{ if $\varepsilon\geqslant 0$},\\
0&\text{ if $\varepsilon<0$}.\end{array}\right.

Setting
\Psi_{\star}\!:=\rho^{-1}\circ\Psi,

we take two auxiliary transverse sections
\Sigma_{1}^{n}\!:=\Psi_{\star}^{-1}(\{w=1\})
and
\Sigma_{2}^{n}\!:=\Psi_{\star}^{-1}(\{z=1\})
parameterized by
\sigma_{1}^{n}(s;{{\nu}})\!:=\Psi_{\star}^{-1}(s+\vartheta_{\varepsilon},1)
and
\sigma_{2}^{n}(s;{{\nu}})\!:=\Psi_{\star}^{-1}(1,s)
, respectively (see Figure
4
). We define
\mathcal{T}(s;{{\nu}})
and
\mathscr{D}(s;{{\nu}})
to be the Dulac time and Dulac map of
\tilde{X}_{{\nu}}
from
\Sigma_{1}^{n}
to
\Sigma_{2}^{n},
respectively. We remark that, by construction,
\mathcal{T}(s;{{\nu}})
is the time that the solution of
\rho^{*}\bar{X}_{{\nu}}
starting at the point
(s+\vartheta_{\varepsilon},1)
spends to arrive at
\{z=1\}
and that the intersection point is precisely
(z,w)=(1,\mathscr{D}(s;{{\nu}}))
. In this regard, since
w=\mathscr{D}(z;{{\nu}})
is a trajectory of the vector field
z(z^{2}-\varepsilon)\partial_{z}+w(2F/r^{2}-z^{2})\partial_{w}
, see
[
25
, p. 6417]
for details, the application of
(b)
in Corollary A of
[
25
]
with
\{\mu=2,\ell=k=2,\lambda=2F/r^{2}\}
shows that

\mathscr{D}(s;{{\nu}})=s^{2}\mathcal{I}(B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}}),

(7)

by shrinking
\delta>0
if necessary. Here, and in what follows,
\mathcal{I}(B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}})
stands for some function
h(s;{{\nu}})
verifying that
\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}\Theta^{k}h(s;{{\nu}})=0
uniformly on
{{\nu}}\in B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}}
for
k=0,1,2
.
Furthermore, by applying Corollary B in the same paper with
\{\mu=2,\ell=k=2\}
and shrinking
\delta>0
again we can assert that

\mathcal{T}(s;{{\nu}})=b_{0}({{\nu}})+b_{1}({{\nu}})s+b_{2}({{\nu}})s^{2}+s^{2}\mathcal{I}(B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}})

(8)

with
b_{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{0}(B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}})
for
i=0,1,2.

Working in the original
(x,y)
coordinates, we consider next the transition times
T_{1}(\,\cdot\,;{{\nu}})
and
T_{2}(\,\cdot\,;{{\nu}})
of
X_{{\nu}}
from
\Sigma_{1}
to
p^{-1}(\Sigma_{1}^{n})
and from
p^{-1}(\Sigma_{2}^{n})
to
\Sigma_{2}
, respectively. We define moreover
R(\,\cdot\,;{{\nu}})
to be the transition map from
\Sigma_{1}
to
p^{-1}(\Sigma_{1}^{n})
. Accordingly

T(s)=T_{1}(s)+\big(\mathcal{T}\circ R\big)(s)+\big(T_{2}\circ\mathscr{D}\circ R\big)(s),

(9)

where we omit the dependence on
{{\nu}}
for the sake of shortness. By
[
22
, Lemma 3.2]
, we have that
T_{2}(s;{{\nu}})
an analytic function at
\{0\}\times B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}}
with
T_{2}(0;{{\nu}})=0.
Observe at this point that, setting

\xi_{{\nu}}\!:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0&\text{ if $F\leqslant 1,$}\\
1-p_{1}&\text{ if $F>1,$}\end{array}\right.

we can write the parametrization of
\Sigma_{1}
as
\sigma_{1}(s;{{\nu}})=(1-\xi_{{\nu}}-s,0).
We claim that there exist two functions
f(\hat{s};{{\nu}})
and
g(\hat{s};{{\nu}})
, analytic at
\{0\}\times B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}},
such that

T_{1}(s;{{\nu}})=f(s+\xi_{{\nu}};{{\nu}})\text{ and }R(s;{{\nu}})=g(s+\xi_{{\nu}};{{\nu}})-\vartheta_{\varepsilon}.

(10)

To show this let us consider two additional transverse sections
\hat{\Sigma}_{1}
and
\hat{\Sigma}_{1}^{n}
parameterized respectively by
\hat{\sigma}_{1}(\hat{s};{{\nu}})\!:=(1-\hat{s},0)
and
\hat{\sigma}_{1}^{n}(\hat{s};{{\nu}})\!:=(\Psi_{\star}\circ p)^{-1}(\hat{s},1),
which clearly are analytic at
\{0\}\times B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}}
. Moreover it is clear that they are related with
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{1}^{n}
through
\sigma_{1}(s;{{\nu}})=\hat{\sigma}_{1}(s+\xi_{{\nu}};{{\nu}})
and
\sigma_{1}^{n}(s;{{\nu}})=\hat{\sigma}_{1}^{n}(s+\vartheta_{\varepsilon};{{\nu}}).
That being said, the claim follows by noting that if we choose
f(\hat{s};{{\nu}})
and
g(\hat{s};{{\nu}})
to be, respectively, the transition time and transition map of
X_{{\nu}}
from
\hat{\Sigma}_{1}
to
\hat{\Sigma}_{1}^{n}
, which are clearly analytic at
\{0\}\times B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}},
then the equalities in
(\ref{p3eq1})
hold. Note moreover that
g(\xi_{{\nu}};{{\nu}})=\vartheta_{\varepsilon}
since
R(0;{{\nu}})=0.
On account of the claim, by considering the second order Taylor’s development of
f(\hat{s};{{\nu}})
and
g(\hat{s};{{\nu}})
at
\hat{s}=\xi_{{\nu}}
, respectively, we get

T_{1}(s;{{\nu}})=a_{0}({{\nu}})+a_{1}({{\nu}})s+a_{2}({{\nu}})s^{2}+s^{2}\mathcal{I}(B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}})\text{ and }R(s;{{\nu}})=c_{1}({{\nu}})s+c_{2}({{\nu}})s^{2}+s^{2}\mathcal{I}(B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}})

with
a_{i},c_{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{0}(B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}})
and where we also use that
{{\nu}}\mapsto\xi_{{\nu}}
is a continuous function. The combination of the second expression above with
(\ref{p3eq2})
and
(\ref{p3eq3})
yields

\big(\mathscr{D}\circ R\big)(s)=s^{2}\mathcal{I}(B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}})\text{ and }\big(\mathcal{T}\circ R\big)(s)=\hat{b}_{0}({{\nu}})+\hat{b}_{1}({{\nu}})s+\hat{b}_{2}({{\nu}})s^{2}+s^{2}\mathcal{I}(B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}}),

respectively, with
\hat{b}_{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{0}(B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}}).
Summing up, since
\big(T_{2}\circ\mathscr{D}\circ R\big)(s)=s^{2}\mathcal{I}(B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}})
due to
T_{2}(0;{{\nu}})=0,
from
(\ref{p3eq4})
we can assert that

T(s;{{\nu}})=T_{0}^{{{\nu}}_{\star}}({{\nu}})+T_{1}^{{{\nu}}_{\star}}({{\nu}})s+T_{2}^{{{\nu}}_{\star}}({{\nu}})s^{2}+s^{2}h^{{{\nu}}_{\star}}(s;{{\nu}})

(11)

for some functions
T^{{{\nu}}_{\star}}_{i}
that are continuous on
B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}}
and some

h^{{{\nu}}_{\star}}\in\mathcal{I}(B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}})
. This concludes the proof of the local version of the statement, in which we remark that the coefficients
T_{i}^{{{\nu}}_{\star}}({{\nu}})
and the remainder
s^{2}h^{{{\nu}}_{\star}}(s;{{\nu}})
depend by construction on
{{\nu}}_{\star}.

Our next step will be to globalize them and to this end we define

\mathscr{U}\!:=\bigcup_{{{\nu}}_{\star}\in(-1,0)\times\{1\}}B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}}

which is clearly an open neighbourhood of
(-1,0)\times\{1\}
. Let us consider now any

{{\nu}}_{1},{{\nu}}_{2}\in(-1,0)\times\{1\}
such that
B_{{{\nu}}_{1}}\cap B_{{{\nu}}_{2}}\neq 0.
Then, from
(\ref{p3eq5})
, we get that

T_{0}^{{{\nu}}_{1}}({{\nu}})-T_{0}^{{{\nu}}_{2}}({{\nu}})+\big(T_{1}^{{{\nu}}_{1}}({{\nu}})-T_{1}^{{{\nu}}_{2}}({{\nu}})\big)s+\big(T_{2}^{{{\nu}}_{1}}({{\nu}})-T_{2}^{{{\nu}}_{2}}({{\nu}})\big)s^{2}+s^{2}\big(h^{{{\nu}}_{1}}(s;{{\nu}})-h^{{{\nu}}_{2}}(s;{{\nu}})\big)=0

for all
s>0
small enough and
{{\nu}}\in B_{{{\nu}}_{1}}\cap B_{{{\nu}}_{2}}
. Since
h^{{{\nu}}_{1}}-h^{{{\nu}}_{2}}\in\mathcal{I}(B_{{{\nu}}_{1}}\cap B_{{{\nu}}_{2}}),
taking the limit
s\to 0^{+}
on both sides of the above equality we deduce that

T_{0}^{{{\nu}}_{1}}=T_{0}^{{{\nu}}_{2}}
on
B_{{{\nu}}_{1}}\cap B_{{{\nu}}_{2}}
. Similarly, but taking the first and second derivatives with respect to
s
, respectively, we get that
T_{1}^{{{\nu}}_{1}}=T_{1}^{{{\nu}}_{2}}
and
T_{2}^{{{\nu}}_{1}}=T_{2}^{{{\nu}}_{2}}
on
B_{{{\nu}}_{1}}\cap B_{{{\nu}}_{2}}
. Hence, for
i=0,1,2,
the local functions
T_{i}^{{{\nu}}_{\star}}\in\mathscr{C}^{0}(B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}})
for
{{\nu}}_{\star}\in(-1,0)\times\{1\}
glue together into a well defined continuous function
T_{i}
on
\mathscr{U}
. Exactly the same argument shows that the local functions

h^{{{\nu}}_{\star}}\in\mathcal{I}(B_{{{\nu}}_{\star}})
for
{{\nu}}_{\star}\in(-1,0)\times\{1\}
glue together into a well defined function
h(s;{{\nu}})
satisfying that
\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}\Theta^{k}h(s;\nu)=0
uniformly on compact sets of
\mathscr{U}
for
k=0,1,2
. To show this last assertion it suffices to take a finite subcover
B_{{{\nu}}_{1}}\cup\ldots\cup B_{{{\nu}}_{n}}
of the given compact subset of
\mathscr{U}
and use that
h|_{B_{{{\nu}}_{i}}}\in\mathcal{I}(B_{{{\nu}}_{i}})
for
i=1,2,\ldots,n.

So far we have proved the first assertion in the statement. Let us turn to the proof of the second one. To this end the key point is that for those
{{\nu}}_{0}\in\mathscr{U}\cap\{F<1\}
we can also apply
(a)
in Proposition
3.2

to obtain that

T(s;{{\nu}})=T_{00}({{\nu}})+T_{10}({{\nu}})s+T_{20}({{\nu}})s^{2}+\mathcal{F}_{3-\upsilon}^{\infty}({{\nu}}_{0}),

(12)

where, setting
\lambda({{\nu}})=\frac{F}{1-F}
,

\textstyle T_{10}({{\nu}})=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}(2D+1)}{2\sqrt{F(1+D)^{3}}}\frac{\Gamma\left(1-\frac{1}{2\lambda}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{3}{2}-\frac{1}{2\lambda}\right)}\text{ and }T_{20}(-\frac{1}{2},F)=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{\sqrt{2F}}\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\lambda}\big)}{\Gamma\big(1-\frac{1}{\lambda}\big)}.

Hence, since
T_{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{0}(\mathscr{U})
, from
(\ref{p3eq5})
and
(\ref{p3eq6})
we can assert that

T_{1}(D,1)=\lim_{F\to 1^{-}}T_{10}(D,F)=\frac{2D+1}{(1+D)^{3/2}},

where we also use that
\lim_{F\to 1^{-}}\frac{\Gamma\left(1-\frac{1}{2\lambda}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{3}{2}-\frac{1}{2\lambda}\right)}=\frac{\Gamma(1)}{\Gamma(\frac{3}{2})}=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}
. Consequently, as desired,
T_{1}(D,1)=0
if and only if
D=-\frac{1}{2}
. The same argument shows that

T_{2}(-1/2,1)=\lim_{F\to 1^{-}}T_{20}(-1/2,F)=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{1}{2}\big)}{\Gamma\big(1\big)}\neq 0,

and this completes the proof of the result.

4
Distinguished cases

This section is devoted to study three specific parameters. Recall that among the quadratic centers there are four nonlinear isochrones, see
(\ref{isochronous})
. Chicone and Jacobs show in
[
6
, Theorem 3.1]
that the criticality of the period function at the inner boundary (i.e., the center) of
\mathscr{P}
is exactly 1 for each one of the nonlinear isochrones. In this section we prove that for two of them, namely
\nu=(-\frac{1}{2},2)
and
\nu=(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}),

the criticality at the outer boundary (i.e., the polycycle) is also 1, see Propositions
4.2
and
4.3
, respectively.
In the same vein it is also well-known that the criticality at the inner boundary of any quadratic center is at most two, see
[
6
, Theorem 3.2]
. This maximum criticality is achieved in three parameter values, the so-called Loud points, which following the notation in
[
6
]
are given by
\nu=L_{i}
with

\textstyle L_{1}\!:=\left(-\frac{3}{2},\frac{5}{2}\right),\;L_{2}\!:=\left(\frac{-11+\sqrt{105}}{20},\frac{15-\sqrt{105}}{20}\right)\text{ and }L_{3}\!:=\left(\frac{-11-\sqrt{105}}{20},\frac{15+\sqrt{105}}{20}\right).

(13)

As we already explained in the introduction, we conjecture that the criticality at the outer boundary of any quadratic center is at most two, and that there are only three parameter values where this maximum criticality is attained. In this paper we identify and prove the validity of the conjecture for two of these parameters. We investigate one of them in this section, see Proposition
4.4
. The other one was already studied in
[
24
]
and we postpone its treatment until the proof of Theorem
A
.

The following is a sort of division theorem within the class of flat functions that will be used to study the criticality at the outer boundary for the above-mentioned isochrones. In its statement, and in what follows, we use the notation
0_{n}=(0,0,\ldots,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
for the sake of shortness.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let us fix
K\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
,
L\geqslant 0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}.
If
f(s;\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n})\in\mathcal{F}^{K}_{L}(0_{n})
verifies that

f(s;\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{k-1},0,\ldots,0)\equiv 0\text{, for some $k\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}$,}

then there exist
f_{k},\ldots,f_{n}\in\mathcal{F}_{L}^{K-1}(0_{n})
such that
f=\sum_{i=k}^{n}\mu_{i}f_{i}
.

We proceed by induction on
n\in\mathbb{N}.
For the base case
n=1
we take
f(s;\mu_{1})\in\mathcal{F}_{L}^{K}(0_{1})
with
f(s;0)\equiv 0
and define
f_{1}(s;\mu_{1})\!:=\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{2}f(s;\mu_{1}t)dt,
so that
f=\mu_{1}f_{1}.
To show that
f_{1}\in\mathcal{F}_{L}^{K-1}(0_{1})
we use that, by hypothesis (see Definition
A.1
), for every
\nu=(\nu_{0},\nu_{1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{\geq 0}
with
|\nu|=\nu_{0}+\nu_{1}\leqslant K-1
there exist a neighbourhood
V\subset\mathbb{R}
of
0
and
C,s_{0}>0
such that
|\partial^{\nu_{0}}_{s}\partial^{\nu_{1}+1}_{\mu_{1}}f(s;\mu_{1})|\leqslant Cs^{L-\nu_{0}}
for every
\mu_{1}\in V
and
s\in(0,s_{0})
. On account of this and applying the Dominated Convergence Theorem
[
37
, Theorem 11.30]
,

|\partial^{\nu}f_{1}(s;\mu_{1})|\leqslant\int_{0}^{1}\left|\partial^{\nu}(\partial_{2}f(s;\mu_{1}t))\right|dt\leqslant\int_{0}^{1}|\partial_{s}^{\nu_{0}}\partial_{2}^{\nu_{1}+1}f(s;\mu_{1}t)|t^{\nu_{1}}dt\leqslant\frac{C}{\nu_{1}+1}s^{L-\nu_{0}}

for every
\mu_{1}\in V
and
s\in(0,s_{0}).
Hence
f_{1}\in\mathcal{F}_{L}^{K-1}(0_{1})
. To prove the inductive step we suppose that
n>1
and consider
f(s;\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n})\in\mathcal{F}^{K}_{L}(0_{n})
verifying that

f(s;\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{k-1},0,\ldots,0)\equiv 0
for some
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
. It is clear that we can write

\displaystyle f(s;\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n-1},\mu_{n})-f(s;\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n-1},0)=\mu_{n}f_{n}(s;\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n})

(14)

with

\displaystyle f_{n}(s;\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n})\!:=\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{n+1}f(s;\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n-1},\mu_{n}t)dt.

Similarly as for the base case, taking
f\in\mathcal{F}_{L}^{K}(0_{n})
into account, one can easily show that
f_{n}\in\mathcal{F}_{L}^{K-1}(0_{n})
. Since
f(s;\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n-1},0)|_{\mu_{k}=\ldots=\mu_{n-1}=0}\equiv 0,
by the inductive hypothesis there exist

\displaystyle f_{k}(s;\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n-1}),\ldots,f_{n-1}(s;\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n-1})\in\mathcal{F}_{L}^{K-1}(0_{n-1})

such that

\displaystyle f(s;\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n-1},0)=\sum_{i=k}^{n-1}\mu_{i}f_{i}(s;\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n-1}).

Due to
\mathcal{F}_{L}^{K-1}(0_{n-1})\subset\mathcal{F}_{L}^{K-1}(0_{n}),
see Definition
A.1
, the combination of this identity with
(\ref{l1eq1})
shows that
f=\sum_{i=k}^{n}\mu_{i}f_{i}
with
f_{k},\ldots,f_{n}\in\mathcal{F}_{L}^{K-1}(0_{n})
as desired. This shows the inductive step and concludes the proof of the result.

We state next our first result about the bifurcation of critical periodic orbits from the outer boundary of an isochronous center. With regard to its proof let us advance that, after a convenient division in the space of coefficients, we proceed as in the proofs of Bautin
[
4
, §3]
and Chicone and Jacobs
[
6
, Theorem 2.2]
for the analogous results about limit cycles and critical periods, respectively, bifurcating from the center. Here we tackle the bifurcation from the polycycle, which is more challenging because, contrary to the center, the period function cannot be analytically extended there. To overcome this difficulty it is crucial the fact that the flatness of the remainder in the asymptotic expansion is preserved after the derivation with respect to the parameters.

Proposition 4.2
.

If
{{\nu}}_{0}=(-\frac{1}{2},2)
then
\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{0}},X_{{{\nu}}_{0}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)=1
.

We show first the upper bound
\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{0}},X_{{{\nu}}_{0}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)\leqslant 1
, which constitutes the difficult part of the proof. To this end, following the notation introduced in Section
3.2
, we define
P(s;{{\nu}})
to be the period of the periodic orbit of
X_{{\nu}}
passing through the point
(p_{1}-s,0)
. Thanks to the reversibility of
X_{{\nu}}
with respect to
\{y=0\}
it turns out that
P(s;{{\nu}})=2T(s;{{\nu}})
where
T(\,\cdot\,;{{\nu}})
is the Dulac time that we consider in Proposition
3.3
. Thus, by applying
(d)
in that result
and setting
\lambda({{\nu}})=\frac{1}{2(F-1)}
, we can assert that

T(s;{{\nu}})=T_{00}({{\nu}})+T_{01}({{\nu}})s^{\lambda}+T^{\frac{1}{2}}_{101}({{\nu}})s\omega_{1-2\lambda}(s)+T_{100}^{\frac{1}{2}}({{\nu}})s+r_{1}(s;{{\nu}}),

where
r_{1}\in\mathcal{F}_{3/2-\upsilon}^{\infty}({{\nu}}_{0})
for all
\upsilon>0
small enough, the coefficients are analytic in a neighbourhood of
{{\nu}}_{0}=(-\frac{1}{2},2)
and, moreover, the gradients of
T_{01}
and
T_{101}^{\frac{1}{2}}
are linearly independent at
{{\nu}}_{0}
. Since one can verify that
\partial_{s}s\omega_{\alpha}(s)=(1-\alpha)\omega_{\alpha}(s)-1
, the derivation of the above equality yields

s^{1-\lambda}T^{\prime}(s;{{\nu}})=\lambda T_{01}({{\nu}})+2\lambda T_{101}^{\frac{1}{2}}({{\nu}})s^{1-\lambda}\omega_{1-2\lambda}(s)+\big(T_{100}^{\frac{1}{2}}({{\nu}})-T_{101}^{\frac{1}{2}}({{\nu}})\big)s^{1-\lambda}+r_{2}(s;{{\nu}})

where, by using Lemmas A.3 and A.4 in
[
29
]
, the remainder
r_{2}\!:=s^{1-\lambda}\partial_{s}r_{1}
belongs to
\mathcal{F}_{1-\upsilon}^{\infty}({{\nu}}_{0})
because
\partial_{s}r_{1}\in\mathcal{F}_{1/2-\upsilon}^{\infty}({{\nu}}_{0})
and, on the other hand,
s^{1-\lambda}\in\mathcal{F}_{1/2-\upsilon}^{\infty}({{\nu}}_{0})
due to
\lambda({{\nu}}_{0})=1/2.
Note furthermore that
\hat{{\nu}}=\Psi({{\nu}})\!:=\big(\lambda({{\nu}})T_{01}({{\nu}}),2\lambda({{\nu}})T_{101}^{\frac{1}{2}}({{\nu}})\big)
is local analytic change of coordinates at
{{\nu}}_{0}=(-\frac{1}{2},2)
such that
\Psi({{\nu}}_{0})=(0,0)
. We can thus write

\mathscr{R}_{1}(s;\hat{{\nu}})\!:=\left.s^{1-\lambda({{\nu}})}T^{\prime}(s;{{\nu}})\right|_{{{\nu}}=\Psi^{-1}(\hat{{\nu}})}=\hat{{\nu}}_{1}+\hat{{\nu}}_{2}s^{1-\hat{\lambda}}\omega_{1-2\hat{\lambda}}(s)+a(\hat{{\nu}})s^{1-\hat{\lambda}}+h(s;\hat{{\nu}}),

(15)

where we set
\hat{\lambda}(\hat{{\nu}})\!:=\lambda(\Psi^{-1}(\hat{{\nu}}))
for shortness and define

a(\hat{{\nu}})\!:=\big(T_{100}^{\frac{1}{2}}-T_{101}^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)(\Psi^{-1}(\hat{{\nu}}))\text{ and }h(s;\hat{{\nu}})\!:=r_{2}(s;\Psi^{-1}(\hat{{\nu}}))\in\mathcal{F}^{\infty}_{1-\upsilon}(0_{2}).

Recall at this point that the center at the origin of
X_{{{\nu}}_{0}}
is isochronous, so that
T^{\prime}(s;{{\nu}}_{0})\equiv 0.
Consequently, due to
\Psi({{\nu}}_{0})=(0,0)
,

a(0,0)=0\text{ and }h(s;0,0)\equiv 0.

By the Weierstrass Division Theorem (see for instance
[
11
, Theorem 1.8]
), the first equality implies that
a(\hat{{\nu}})=\hat{{\nu}}_{1}a_{1}(\hat{{\nu}})+\hat{{\nu}}_{2}a_{2}(\hat{{\nu}})
with
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
analytic functions at
(0,0).
On the other hand, by Lemma
4.1
,
h(s;\hat{{\nu}})=\hat{{\nu}}_{1}h_{1}(s;\hat{{\nu}})+\hat{{\nu}}_{2}h_{2}(s;\hat{{\nu}})
with
h_{i}\in\mathcal{F}^{\infty}_{1-\nu}(0_{2}).
Therefore, from
(\ref{p32eq1})
,

\mathscr{R}_{1}(s;\hat{{\nu}})=\hat{{\nu}}_{1}\Big(1+a_{1}(\hat{{\nu}})s^{1-\hat{\lambda}}+h_{1}(s;\hat{{\nu}})\Big)+\hat{{\nu}}_{2}\Big(s^{1-\hat{\lambda}}\omega_{1-2\hat{\lambda}}(s)+a_{2}(\hat{{\nu}})s^{1-\hat{\lambda}}+h_{2}(s;\hat{{\nu}})\Big)

Since
h_{i}\in\mathcal{F}^{\infty}_{1-\upsilon}(0_{2}),

h_{i}(s;\hat{{\nu}})
tends to zero uniformly for
\hat{{\nu}}\approx(0,0)
as
s\to 0^{+}
(see Definition
A.1
). Due to
\lambda({{\nu}}_{0})=1/2,
this is also the case of
s^{1-\hat{\lambda}}
and
s^{1-\hat{\lambda}}\omega_{1-2\hat{\lambda}}(s)
by
(c)
of Lemma A.4 in
[
29
]
. Hence there exists a neighbourhood
U
of
(0,0)
such that
\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}(1+a_{1}(\hat{{\nu}})s^{1-\hat{\lambda}}+h_{1}(s;\hat{{\nu}}))=1
uniformly on
U
. Accordingly, the function

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}_{2}(s;\hat{{\nu}})\!:=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\mathscr{R}_{1}(s;\hat{{\nu}})}{1+a_{1}(\hat{{\nu}})s^{1-\hat{\lambda}}+h_{1}(s;\hat{{\nu}})}=\hat{{\nu}}_{1}+\hat{{\nu}}_{2}\ell(s;\hat{{\nu}})

(16)

with

\displaystyle\ell(s;{{\nu}})\!:=

\displaystyle\,\frac{s^{1-\hat{\lambda}}\omega_{1-2\hat{\lambda}}(s)+a_{2}(\hat{{\nu}})s^{1-\hat{\lambda}}+h_{2}(s;\hat{{\nu}})}{1+a_{1}(\hat{{\nu}})s^{1-\hat{\lambda}}+h_{1}(s;\hat{{\nu}})}

belongs to the class
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{s>0}(U)
, see Definition
A.1
.

We claim that, by shrinking
U
, there exists
s_{0}>0
such that
\mathscr{R}_{2}(s;\hat{{\nu}})
has at most one zero on
(0,s_{0})
, counted with multiplicities, for all
\hat{{\nu}}=(\hat{{\nu}}_{1},\hat{{\nu}}_{2})\in U\setminus\{(0,0)\}.
Indeed, to show this note first that if
\hat{{\nu}}_{2}=0
then
\mathscr{R}_{2}(s;\hat{{\nu}})=\hat{{\nu}}_{1}\neq 0,
so that there is nothing to be proved in this case.
Let us study consequently the case
\hat{{\nu}}_{2}\neq 0
. To this end we observe that
\mathscr{R}_{2}^{\prime}(s;\hat{{\nu}})=\hat{{\nu}}_{2}\ell^{\prime}(s;\hat{{\nu}})
where, using a more compact notation,

\displaystyle\ell^{\prime}(s;\hat{{\nu}})

\displaystyle=\partial_{s}\left(\frac{s^{1-\hat{\lambda}}\omega_{1-2\hat{\lambda}}+a_{2}s^{1-\hat{\lambda}}+\mathcal{F}^{\infty}_{1-\upsilon}}{1+a_{1}s^{1-\hat{\lambda}}+\mathcal{F}^{\infty}_{1-\upsilon}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt=\frac{\omega_{1-2\hat{\lambda}}}{s^{\hat{\lambda}}}\left(\frac{\hat{\lambda}+\frac{a_{2}-a_{2}\hat{\lambda}-1}{\omega_{1-2\hat{\lambda}}}+\mathcal{F}^{\infty}_{\frac{1}{2}-\upsilon^{\prime}}}{1+a_{1}s^{1-\hat{\lambda}}+\mathcal{F}^{\infty}_{1-\upsilon}}-\frac{\big(s^{1-\hat{\lambda}}+a_{2}\frac{s^{1-\hat{\lambda}}}{\omega_{1-2\hat{\lambda}}}+\mathcal{F}^{\infty}_{1-\upsilon^{\prime}}\big)\big((1-\hat{\lambda})a_{1}+\mathcal{F}^{\infty}_{\frac{1}{2}-\upsilon^{\prime}}\big)}{(1+a_{1}s^{1-\hat{\lambda}}+\mathcal{F}^{\infty}_{1-\upsilon})^{2}}\right).

Here we use the identity
\partial_{s}s^{b}\omega_{\alpha}(s)=s^{b-1}((b-\alpha)\omega_{\alpha}(s)-1)
and that, by Lemmas A.3 and A.4 in
[
29
]
, we have
1/\omega_{1-2\hat{\lambda}}\in\mathcal{F}^{\infty}_{-\upsilon}(0_{2})
and
s^{-\hat{\lambda}}\in\mathcal{F}_{-1/2-\upsilon}^{\infty}(0_{2})
for all
\upsilon>0
small enough due to
\hat{\lambda}(0,0)=\frac{1}{2}
and, moreover, that the inclusion
\mathcal{F}_{L}^{\infty}\mathcal{F}_{L^{\prime}}^{\infty}\subset\mathcal{F}_{L+L^{\prime}}^{\infty}
holds. We also remark that, by
(a)
of Lemma A.4 in
[
29
]
,

\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{\omega_{1-2\hat{\lambda}}(s)}=\frac{|1-2\hat{\lambda}|-(1-2\hat{\lambda})}{2}\text{ uniformly for $\hat{{\nu}}\approx(0,0)$.}

On account of this, from the above expression of
\ell^{\prime}
we obtain that

\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}\frac{s^{\hat{\lambda}}\ell^{\prime}(s;\hat{{\nu}})}{\omega_{1-2\hat{\lambda}}(s)}=b(\hat{{\nu}})\text{ uniformly for $\hat{{\nu}}\approx(0,0)$,}

where
b(\hat{{\nu}})\!:=\hat{\lambda}+\frac{1}{2}(a_{2}-a_{2}\hat{\lambda}-1)(|1-2\hat{\lambda}|-(1-2\hat{\lambda}))
. Since
\hat{\lambda}(0,0)=1/2
, it is clear that
b(\hat{{\nu}})
is a non-vanishing continuous function in a neighbourhood of
(0,0)
. Accordingly, due to
\mathscr{R}^{\prime}_{2}(s;\hat{{\nu}})=\hat{{\nu}}_{2}\ell^{\prime}(s;\hat{{\nu}})
, we can assert that

\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}\frac{s^{\hat{\lambda}}\mathscr{R}^{\prime}_{2}(s;\hat{{\nu}})}{\omega_{1-2\hat{\lambda}}(s)}=\hat{{\nu}}_{2}b(\hat{{\nu}})\text{ uniformly for $\hat{{\nu}}\approx(0,0)$.}

Since
\omega_{\alpha}(s)
only vanishes at
s=1,
by shrinking
U
if necessary, we can assert the existence of some
s_{0}\in(0,1)
such that
\mathscr{R}_{2}^{\prime}(s;\hat{{\nu}})\neq 0
for all
s\in(0,s_{0})
and
\hat{{\nu}}=(\hat{{\nu}}_{1},\hat{{\nu}}_{2})\in U
with
\hat{{\nu}}_{2}\neq 0
. Therefore, by Rolle’s Theorem,
\mathscr{R}_{2}(s;\hat{{\nu}})
has at most one zero on
(0,s_{0})
counted with multiplicities. This shows the validity of the claim for the case
{{\nu}}_{2}\neq 0.

Recall finally that the period function
P(s;{{\nu}})
is twice the Dulac time
T(s;{{\nu}})
.
Thus, taking the claim into account, from
(\ref{p32eq1})
and
(\ref{p32eq2})
it turns out that
V\!:=\Psi^{-1}(U)
is an open neighbourhood of
{{\nu}}_{0}=(-\frac{1}{2},2)
verifying that
P^{\prime}(s;{{\nu}})
has at most one isolated zero on
(0,s_{0})
, counted with multiplicities, for all
{{\nu}}\in V.
(To be more precise, the claim applies for the punctured neighbourhood
V\setminus\{{{\nu}}_{0}\}
and, on the other hand,
P^{\prime}(s;{{\nu}}_{0})\equiv 0,
so that it has not any isolated zero.) Hence, see Definition
2
,
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(P^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,;{{\nu}}),{{\nu}}_{0})\leqslant 1
. Therefore the upper bound
\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{0}},X_{{{\nu}}_{0}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)\leqslant 1
follows from assertion
(2a)
in Lemma
2.4

since, using the notation in that result,
P(s;\nu)=\hat{P}(\sigma(s;\nu);\nu)
with
\sigma(s;\nu)=(p_{1}-s,0)
for
s\in[0,\delta)
. Thus it only remains to show that this upper bound is achieved. To this end we recall that, by
[
23
, Theorem A]
,
{{\nu}}_{0}=(-\frac{1}{2},2)
is a local bifurcation value of the period function at the outer boundary, see Definition
2
. Then, since the period annulus of the centers under consideration varies continuously, see Remark
2
, by applying
(a)
in Lemma
2.16
we get that
\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{1}},X_{\nu_{1}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)\geqslant 1
. This completes the proof of the result.

The following is our second result about the criticality of the quadratic isochrones.

Proposition 4.3
.

If
{{\nu}}_{0}=(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
then
\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{0}},X_{{{\nu}}_{0}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)=1
.

We prove
\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{0}},X_{{{\nu}}_{0}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)\leqslant 1
first, which is the most complicated part of the proof. To this end, for each
s\in(0,1)
we denote by
P(s;{{\nu}})
the period of the periodic orbit of
X_{{\nu}}
passing thought the point
(1-s,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},
see Figure
2
. Then, on account of the reversibility of the vector field with respect to
\{y=0\}
, it follows that
P(s;{{\nu}})=2T(s;{{\nu}})
, where
T(\,\cdot\,;{{\nu}})
is the Dulac time introduced before Proposition
3.2
. Thanks to that result we have thus the asymptotic expansion of
P(s;{{\nu}})
near the polycycle, which corresponds to
s=0.
On the other hand, it is well known that the period function can be analytically extended to the center (which corresponds to
s=1
with this parametrization) because it is non-degenerated. The coefficients of the Taylor’s series of
P^{\prime}(s;{{\nu}})
at
s=1
belong to the polynomial ring
\mathbb{R}[D,F]
. Chicone and Jacobs show (see Lemma 3.1 and Theorem 3.9 in
[
6
]
) that these coefficients are in the ideal generated by

\displaystyle p_{2}(D,F)=

\displaystyle\,10D^{2}+10DF-D+4F^{2}-5F+1

and

\displaystyle p_{4}(D,F)=

\displaystyle\,1540D^{4}+4040D^{3}F+1180D^{3}+4692D^{2}F^{2}+1992D^{2}F+453D^{2}

\displaystyle+2768DF^{3}+228DF^{2}+318DF-2D+784F^{4}-616F^{3}-63F^{2}-154F+49

over the local ring
\mathbb{R}\{D,F\}_{{{\nu}}_{i}}
of convergent power series at
{{\nu}}_{i}
localized at any of the of the four quadratic isochrones
{{\nu}}_{0}\!:=(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}),

{{\nu}}_{1}\!:=(0,1)

{{\nu}}_{2}\!:=(0,\frac{1}{4})
and
{{\nu}}_{3}\!=(-\frac{1}{2},2).
With regard to the first one, we claim that the ideal
\mathfrak{B}\!:=(p_{2},p_{4})
is equal to
\left(D+F,(2F-1)^{2}\right)
over the local ring
\mathbb{R}\{D,F\}_{{{\nu}}_{0}}
. Indeed, to prove this we use that

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}p_{2}\\
p_{4}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}q_{11}&q_{12}\\
q_{21}&q_{22}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}(2F-1)^{2}\\
D+F\end{array}\right)

(17)

with
q_{11}=1,

q_{12}=10D-1
,
q_{21}=52{F}^{2}+44F+49
and

q_{22}=576{F}^{3}+(2192D-584){F}^{2}+(2500{D}^{2}+812D-135)F+1540{D}^{3}+1180{D}^{2}+453D-2.

(The idea to obtain this is that the zero of
p_{2i}|_{D=-F}
at
F=1/2
has multiplicity two.)
From
(\ref{p35eq1})
we get that
p_{2i}\in\left(D+F,(2F-1)^{2}\right)
over the polynomial ring
\mathbb{R}[D,F].
Conversely, since one can verify that the determinant
q_{11}q_{22}-q_{21}q_{12}
is different from zero at
{{\nu}}_{0}=(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}),
by inverting the matrix in
(\ref{p35eq1})
it follows that
(2F-1)^{2}\in\mathfrak{B}
and
D+F\in\mathfrak{B}
over the local ring
\mathbb{R}\{D,F\}_{{{\nu}}_{0}}
. This proves the validity of the claim. Consequently, thanks to the result of Chicone and Jacobs mentioned above, we have the following equality between ideals over the local ring
\mathbb{R}\{D,F\}_{{{\nu}}_{0}}:

\mathfrak{B}=\big(D+F,(2F-1)^{2}\big)=\big(P^{(i)}(1;{{\nu}}),i\in\mathbb{N}\big).

Now the crucial point is that the ideal
\big(P^{(i)}(s_{0};{{\nu}}),i\in\mathbb{N}\big)

does not depend on the point
s_{0}\in(0,1].
Indeed, this follows verbatim the argument that R. Roussarie gives in
[
35
, pp. 76–78]
or
[
36
, §4.3.1]
to justify the same property about the ideal of the displacement map, the so-called Bautin ideal.
Here we also use that, such as the displacement map, the period function
P(s;{{\nu}})
extends analytically to the non-degenerate center (i.e.,
s=1
). Accordingly,

\mathfrak{B}=\big(D+F,(2F-1)^{2}\big)=\big(P^{(i)}(s_{0};{{\nu}}),i\in\mathbb{N}\big)\text{ for all $s_{0}\in(0,1].$}

(18)

We turn next to the study of the period function near the polycycle (i.e.,
s=0).
In this regard by applying
(d)
in Proposition
3.2
we can assert that, for all
\upsilon>0
small enough,

P(s;{{\nu}})=2T_{00}({{\nu}})+2T_{101}^{1}({{\nu}})s\omega_{1-\lambda}(s)+2T_{100}^{1}({{\nu}})s+\mathcal{F}_{2-\upsilon}^{\infty}({{\nu}}_{0}),

where
\lambda({{\nu}})=\frac{F}{1-F}
and

T_{101}^{1}({{\nu}})=-\rho_{4}({{\nu}})(F-1/2)^{2}\text{ and }T_{100}^{1}({{\nu}})=\rho_{5}({{\nu}})(D+1/2)+\rho_{6}({{\nu}})(F-1/2)

(19)

for some analytic positive functions
\rho_{4},

\rho_{5}
and
\rho_{6}
in a neighbourhood of
{{\nu}}_{0}=(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
. Consequently, on account of the identity
\partial_{s}s\omega_{\alpha}(s)=(1-\alpha)\omega_{\alpha}(s)-1
and assertion
(f)
of Lemma A.3 in
[
29
]
,

P^{\prime}(s;{{\nu}})=2\lambda T_{101}^{1}({{\nu}})\omega_{1-\lambda}(s)+2\big(T_{100}^{1}-T_{101}^{1}\big)({{\nu}})+\mathcal{F}_{1-\upsilon}^{\infty}({{\nu}}_{0}).

Furthermore, from
(\ref{p35eq2})
it follows that

\hat{{\nu}}=\Psi({{\nu}})\!:=\left((F-1/2)\sqrt{2\lambda\rho_{4}({{\nu}})},2\big(T_{100}^{1}-T_{101}^{1}\big)({{\nu}})\right)

is an analytic local change of coordinates in a neighbourhood of
{{\nu}}={{\nu}}_{0}
because one can verify that its Jacobian at
{{\nu}}_{0}=(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
is equal to
-2\rho_{5}({{\nu}}_{0})\sqrt{2\rho_{4}({{\nu}}_{0})}\neq 0.
Setting
\hat{{\nu}}=(\hat{{\nu}}_{1},\hat{{\nu}}_{2})
, observe that then

P^{\prime}(s;\Psi^{-1}(\hat{{\nu}}))=-\hat{{\nu}}_{1}^{2}\omega_{1-\hat{\lambda}}(s)+\hat{{\nu}}_{2}+f(s;\hat{{\nu}}),

(20)

where
f\in\mathcal{F}_{1-\upsilon}^{\infty}(0_{2})
and we denote
\hat{\lambda}\!:=\lambda(\Psi^{-1}(\hat{{\nu}}))
for shortness.

We claim that
\mathfrak{B}=(\hat{{\nu}}_{1}^{2},\hat{{\nu}}_{2})
over the local ring
\mathbb{R}\{D,F\}_{{{\nu}}_{0}}
. To show this we note that

\hat{{\nu}}_{2}\big|_{D=-F}=(2F-1)\big(\rho_{6}-\rho_{5}\big)(-F,F)+2(F-1/2)^{2}\rho_{4}(-F,F).

Since
\rho_{5}({{\nu}}_{0})=\rho_{6}({{\nu}}_{0})
by
(d)
in Proposition
3.2
, it follows that

\big(\rho_{6}-\rho_{5}\big)(-F,F)=(F-1/2)r_{1}(F)
for some analytic function
r_{1}
at
F=1/2.
Consequently
\hat{{\nu}}_{2}|_{D=-F}=(F-1/2)^{2}r_{2}(F)
with
r_{2}
analytic at
F=1/2.
Taking this into account, the Weierstrass Division Theorem (see
[
11
, Theorem 1.8]
) shows that

\hat{{\nu}}_{2}=(D+F)q(\hat{{\nu}})+(F-1/2)^{2}r_{2}(F)

for some analytic function
q
at
\hat{{\nu}}=(0,0)
which, from
(\ref{p35eq2})
, verifies
q(0,0)=2\rho_{5}({{\nu}}_{0})\neq 0.
Hence we can write

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\hat{{\nu}}_{1}^{2}\\
\hat{{\nu}}_{2}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&2\lambda\rho_{4}({{\nu}})\\
q(\Psi({{\nu}}))&r_{2}(F)\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}D+F\\
(F-1/2)^{2}\end{array}\right),

where the matrix has an analytic inverse at
{{\nu}}={{\nu}}_{0}.
Taking
(\ref{p35eq4})
into account this shows that
\mathfrak{B}=(\hat{{\nu}}_{1}^{2},\hat{{\nu}}_{2})
over the local ring
\mathbb{R}\{D,F\}_{{{\nu}}_{0}}
, as desired.

Recall at this point that the center of
X_{{{\nu}}_{0}}
is isochronous. Hence
P^{\prime}(s;{{\nu}}_{0})\equiv 0.
Thus, taking
\Psi({{\nu}}_{0})=(0,0)
into account, from
(\ref{p35eq5})
we get that
f(s;0,0)\equiv 0.
Having this in mind we write the remainder in
(\ref{p35eq5})
as

f(s;\hat{{\nu}})=f_{1}(s;\hat{{\nu}})+f_{2}(s;\hat{{\nu}}_{1})

with
f_{1}(s;\hat{{\nu}})\!:=f(s;\hat{{\nu}}_{1},\hat{{\nu}}_{2})-f(s;\hat{{\nu}}_{1},0)
and
f_{2}(s;\hat{{\nu}}_{1})\!:=f(s;\hat{{\nu}}_{1},0)
. Since
f_{1}(s;\hat{{\nu}}_{1},0)\equiv 0
, the application of Lemma
4.1
shows the existence of
g_{1}\in\mathcal{F}_{1-\upsilon}^{\infty}(0_{2})
such that
f_{1}(s;\hat{{\nu}})=\hat{{\nu}}_{2}g_{1}(s;\hat{{\nu}})
. Due to
f_{2}(s;0)\equiv 0
and again by Lemma
4.1
,
f_{2}(s;\hat{{\nu}}_{1})=\hat{{\nu}}_{1}g_{2}(s;\hat{{\nu}}_{1})
with
g_{2}\in\mathcal{F}_{1-\upsilon}^{\infty}(0_{2})
. We also have
g_{2}(s;0)\equiv 0
because, otherwise, it would exist
s_{0}>0
such that
g_{2}(s_{0};\hat{{\nu}}_{1})\neq 0
for all
\hat{{\nu}}_{1}\approx 0
. In this case, taking the claim into account together with
(\ref{p35eq4})
and
(\ref{p35eq5})
,

P^{\prime}(s_{0};\Psi^{-1}(\hat{{\nu}}))=-\hat{{\nu}}_{1}^{2}\omega_{1-\hat{\lambda}}(s_{0})+\hat{{\nu}}_{2}+\hat{{\nu}}_{2}g_{1}(s_{0};\hat{{\nu}})+\hat{{\nu}}_{1}g_{2}(s_{0};\hat{{\nu}}_{1})\in\mathfrak{B}=(\hat{{\nu}}_{1}^{2},\hat{{\nu}}_{2}).

From here, since each
g_{i}(s_{0};\hat{{\nu}})
is analytic at
\hat{{\nu}}=(0,0)
and
g_{2}(s_{0};\hat{{\nu}}_{1})\neq 0
for
\hat{{\nu}}_{1}\approx 0
, we would get that
\hat{{\nu}}_{1}\in(\hat{{\nu}}_{1}^{2},\hat{{\nu}}_{2})
over the local ring
\mathbb{R}\{D,F\}_{{{\nu}}_{0}}
, which is clearly a contradiction. Concerning the analyticity of
g_{i}(s_{0};\hat{{\nu}})
, let us remark that it follows by applying the Weierstrass Division Theorem thanks to the analyticity of
f(s_{0};\hat{{\nu}})
at
\hat{{\nu}}=(0,0),
which in its turn follows from
(\ref{p35eq5})
noting that:

•

P^{\prime}(s_{0};{{\nu}})
is analytic at
{{\nu}}={{\nu}}_{0}
because
\{X_{{\nu}}\}_{{{\nu}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}
is an analytic family of the vector fields and hence, by Lemma
2.1
,
(s,{{\nu}})\mapsto P(s;{{\nu}})=\hat{P}((1-s,0);{{\nu}})
is analytic on
(0,1)\times\mathbb{R}^{2},

•

the change of coordinates
\hat{{\nu}}=\Psi({{\nu}})
is analytic at
{{\nu}}={{\nu}}_{0},
and

•

\omega_{\alpha}(s_{0})
is analytic at
\alpha=0
because we can write it as

\omega_{\alpha}(s_{0})=F(\alpha\ln s_{0})\alpha
with
F(x)=\frac{e^{-x}-1}{x}.

Hence
g_{2}(s;0)\equiv 0
and, by Lemma
4.1
once again,
f_{2}(s;\hat{{\nu}}_{1})=\hat{{\nu}}_{1}^{2}g_{3}(s;\hat{{\nu}}_{1})
with
g_{3}\in\mathcal{F}_{1-\upsilon}^{\infty}(0_{2})
. Summing-up all this information about the remainder, from
(\ref{p35eq5})
we get that

P^{\prime}(s;\Psi^{-1}(\hat{{\nu}}))=-\hat{{\nu}}_{1}^{2}\big(\omega_{1-\hat{\lambda}}(s)+\mathcal{F}_{1-\upsilon}^{\infty}(0_{2})\big)+\hat{{\nu}}_{2}\big(1+\mathcal{F}_{1-\upsilon}^{\infty}(0_{2})\big).

We are now in position to complete the proof by showing that there exist
s_{0}>0
and an open neighbourhood
U
of
\hat{{\nu}}=(0,0)
such that

G(s;\hat{{\nu}})\!:=\frac{P^{\prime}(s;\Psi^{-1}(\hat{{\nu}}))}{\omega_{1-\hat{\lambda}}(s)+\mathcal{F}_{1-\upsilon}^{\infty}(0_{2})}=-\hat{{\nu}}_{1}^{2}+\hat{{\nu}}_{2}\frac{1+\mathcal{F}_{1-\upsilon}^{\infty}(0_{2})}{\omega_{1-\hat{\lambda}}(s)+\mathcal{F}_{1-\upsilon}^{\infty}(0_{2})}

has at most one zero on
(0,s_{0})
, counted with multiplicities, for all
\hat{{\nu}}=(\hat{{\nu}}_{1},\hat{{\nu}}_{2})\in U\setminus\{(0,0)\}.
This is clear in case that
\hat{{\nu}}_{2}=0.
To tackle the case
\hat{{\nu}}_{2}\neq 0
we compute the derivative with respect to
s
to obtain that

\displaystyle G^{\prime}(s;\hat{{\nu}})

\displaystyle=\hat{{\nu}}_{2}\partial_{s}\left(\frac{1+\mathcal{F}_{1-\upsilon}^{\infty}}{\omega_{1-\hat{\lambda}}(s)+\mathcal{F}_{1-\upsilon}^{\infty}}\right)=\hat{{\nu}}_{2}\partial_{s}\left(\frac{1+\mathcal{F}_{1-\upsilon}^{\infty}}{\omega_{1-\hat{\lambda}}(s)(1+\mathcal{F}_{1-2\upsilon}^{\infty})}\right)

\displaystyle=\hat{{\nu}}_{2}\partial_{s}\left(\frac{1+\mathcal{F}_{1-2\upsilon}^{\infty}}{\omega_{1-\hat{\lambda}}(s)}\right)=\frac{\hat{{\nu}}_{2}}{s^{2-\hat{\lambda}}\omega^{2}_{1-\hat{\lambda}}(s)}(1+\mathcal{F}_{1-2\upsilon}^{\infty})+\frac{\hat{{\nu}}_{2}}{\omega_{1-\hat{\lambda}}(s)}\mathcal{F}_{-2\upsilon}^{\infty}

\displaystyle=\frac{\hat{{\nu}}_{2}}{s^{2-\hat{\lambda}}\omega^{2}_{1-\hat{\lambda}}(s)}\big(1+\mathcal{F}_{1-2\upsilon}^{\infty}+s^{2-\hat{\lambda}}\omega_{1-\hat{\lambda}}(s)\mathcal{F}_{-2\upsilon}^{\infty}\big)=\frac{\hat{{\nu}}_{2}}{s^{2-\hat{\lambda}}\omega^{2}_{1-\hat{\lambda}}(s)}\big(1+\mathcal{F}_{1-3\upsilon}^{\infty}\big).

Here, in the second equality we apply first assertion
(c)
of Lemma A.4 in
[
29
]
to get that
1/\omega_{1-\hat{\lambda}}(s)\in\mathcal{F}^{\infty}_{-\upsilon}
for all
\upsilon>0
small enough, due to
\hat{\lambda}(0,0)=1
, and use next that
\mathcal{F}_{-\upsilon}^{\infty}\mathcal{F}_{1-\upsilon}^{\infty}\subset\mathcal{F}_{1-2\upsilon}^{\infty}
from
(g)
of Lemma A.3 in
[
29
]
. In the third equality,
on account of
\frac{1}{1+s}-1\in\mathcal{F}_{1}^{\infty}
and by
(h)
of Lemma A.3 in
[
29
]
, we use first the inclusion
\frac{1}{1+\mathcal{F}^{\infty}_{1-2\upsilon}}\subset 1+\mathcal{F}^{\infty}_{1-2\upsilon}
. Then, by using
(d)
and
(g)
of Lemma A.3 in
[
29
]
, we expand the numerator to get that
(1+\mathcal{F}_{1-\upsilon}^{\infty})(1+\mathcal{F}_{1-2\upsilon}^{\infty})\subset 1+\mathcal{F}_{1-2\upsilon}^{\infty}
. Next, in the fourth equality we use that
\partial_{s}\omega_{\alpha}(s)=s^{-\alpha-1}
and assertion
(f)
of Lemma A.3 in
[
29
]
to deduce that
\partial_{s}\mathcal{F}_{1-2\upsilon}^{\infty}\subset\mathcal{F}_{-2\upsilon}^{\infty}
. Finally in the last equality we apply
(c)
of Lemma A.4 in
[
29
]
to get that
s^{2-\hat{\lambda}}\omega_{1-\hat{\lambda}}(s)\in\mathcal{F}^{\infty}_{1-\upsilon}
and we use again that
\mathcal{F}_{1-\upsilon}^{\infty}\mathcal{F}_{-2\upsilon}^{\infty}\subset\mathcal{F}_{1-3\upsilon}^{\infty}
.
On account of Definition
A.1
we can assert the existence of some
s_{0}\in(0,1)
and a neighbourhood
U
of
(0,0)
such that
G^{\prime}(s;\hat{{\nu}})\neq 0
for all
s\in(0,s_{0})
and
\hat{{\nu}}\in U
with
\hat{{\nu}}_{2}\neq 0.
Consequently
P^{\prime}(s;\Psi^{-1}(\hat{{\nu}}))
has at most one isolated zero on
(0,s_{0}),
counted with multiplicities, for all
\hat{{\nu}}\in U\setminus\{(0,0)\}.
Thus, on account of Definition
2
and the fact that
\Psi({{\nu}}_{0})=(0,0)
, we get
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(P^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,;{{\nu}}),{{\nu}}_{0})\leqslant 1
. Finally the upper bound
\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{0}},X_{{{\nu}}_{0}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)\leqslant 1
follows from assertion
(2a)
in Lemma
2.4

since, using the notation in that result,
P(s;\nu)=\hat{P}(\sigma(s;\nu);\nu)
with
\sigma(s;\nu)=(1-s,0)
for
s\in[0,\delta)
. Therefore it only remains to show that this upper bound is attained. To this end we recall that, by
[
23
, Theorem A]
,
{{\nu}}_{0}=(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
is a local bifurcation value of the period function at the outer boundary, see Definition
2
. Then, since the period annulus of the centers under consideration varies continuously, see Remark
2
, by applying
(a)
in Lemma
2.16
we get that
\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{1}},X_{\nu_{1}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)\geqslant 1
. This finishes the proof of the result.

As we explain at the beginning of this section, the maximum criticality of the period function at the inner boundary is 2 and it is achieved at the three Loud points
\nu=L_{i}
, see
(\ref{Li})
. We refer the interested reader to the paper of Chicone and Jacobs
[
6
]
for a proof of this result. In a joint paper with P. Mardešić, see
[
23
, Theorem 4.3]
, we prove that at each
\nu=L_{i}
there exists a germ of analytic curve that consists of local bifurcation values of the period function at the interior, see Definition
2
. Since the period function extends analytically to the center, this follows readily by applying the Weierstrass Preparation Theorem.
In our next result we identify a parameter
\nu=\nu_{\star}
for which the criticality at the outer boundary is 2. Furthermore we prove that at
\nu=\nu_{\star}
there exists a
\mathscr{C}^{1}
germ of curve of local bifurcation values of the period function at the interior. Hence, roughly speaking, this parameter is the mirror image at the outer boundary of one of the Loud points, see Figure
5
and Remark
4
. In the statement, following the notation introduced at the beginning of Section
3.2
, for each
s\in(0,p_{1})
and
{{\nu}}\approx{{\nu}}_{\star}
we denote by
P(s;{{\nu}})
the period of the periodic orbit of
X_{{\nu}}
passing through the point
(p_{1}-s,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We also remark that
T_{10}
and
T_{01}
are the coefficients given in Proposition
3.3
, which vanish at

{{\nu}}_{\star}=(\mathcal{G}(4/3),4/3)
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let us consider
{{\nu}}_{\star}=(\mathcal{G}(4/3),4/3)
. Then the following holds:

(a)

\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{\star}},X_{{{\nu}}_{\star}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)=2
.

(b)

There exist an open neighbourhood
U
of
{{\nu}}_{\star}
and
s_{0}>0
such that the set

\Delta\!:=\{{{\nu}}\in U;\text{ there exists $s\in(0,s_{0})$ such that }P^{\prime}(s;{{\nu}})=P^{\prime\prime}(s;{{\nu}})=0\}

satisfies the following conditions:

(b1)

Each
{{\nu}}\in\Delta
is a local bifurcation value of the period function at the interior,

(b2)

there exist
\varepsilon>0
and a

\mathscr{C}^{1}
injective curve
\delta:(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\to U
with
\delta(0)={{\nu}}_{\star}
,
\delta((0,\varepsilon))=\Delta
and such that
\delta^{\prime}(0)\neq(0,0)
is tangent to
\{{{\nu}}\in U;\;T_{10}({{\nu}})=0\}
,

(b3)

for each
{{\nu}}\in\Delta
there exists a unique
s_{{\nu}}\in(0,s_{0})
such that
P^{\prime}(s_{{\nu}};{{\nu}})=P^{\prime\prime}(s_{{\nu}};{{\nu}})=0
and, moreover,
\lim_{{{\nu}}\to{{\nu}}_{\star}}s_{{\nu}}=0^{+}
,

(b4)

\Delta\subset\{{{\nu}}\in U;\text{ $T_{10}({{\nu}})<0$ and $T_{01}({{\nu}})>0$}\}
, and

(b5)

for any
{{\nu}}_{0}\in\Delta
and any neighbourhood
V
of
{{\nu}}_{0}
there exist
\bar{{\nu}}\in V
and different
s_{1},s_{2}\in(0,s_{0})
such that
P^{\prime}(s_{1};\bar{{\nu}})=P^{\prime}(s_{2};\bar{{\nu}})=0
.

We observe first of all that
\sigma(s;\nu)\!:=(p_{1}-s,0)
is a parametrization of the outer boundary of the period annulus verifying the hypothesis in Lemma
2.4
. This will enable us to relate
\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{\star}},X_{{{\nu}}_{\star}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)
with
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(P^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,;{{\nu}}),{{\nu}}_{\star}).
That being said, thanks to the reversibility of the vector field with respect to
\{y=0\}
, we note that
P(s;{{\nu}})=2T(s;{{\nu}})
, where
T(\,\cdot\,;{{\nu}})
is the Dulac time considered in Proposition
3.3
. From point
(b)
in that result we can assert that, for all
\upsilon>0
small enough,

P(s;{{\nu}})=2T_{00}({{\nu}})+2T_{10}({{\nu}})s+2T_{01}({{\nu}})s^{\lambda}+2T_{20}({{\nu}})s^{2}+\mathcal{F}_{5/2-\upsilon}^{\infty}({{\nu}}_{\star}),

where
\lambda({{\nu}})=\frac{1}{2(F-1)},

T_{10}({{\nu}}_{\star})=T_{01}({{\nu}}_{\star})=0
,
T_{20}({{\nu}}_{\star})<0
and the gradients
\nabla T_{10}({{\nu}}_{\star})
and
\nabla T_{01}({{\nu}}_{\star})
are linearly independent. Due to
T_{20}({{\nu}}_{\star})\neq 0
, by applying
[
30
, Theorem C]
we get that
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(P^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,;{{\nu}}),{{\nu}}_{\star})\leqslant 2
.
(For readers convenience, let us explain that
[
30
, Theorem C]
is a general result addressed to the Dulac time which,
by using the well-known derivation-division algorithm, gives a bound for
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(T^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,;\nu),\nu_{0})
in terms of the position of the first non-vanishing coefficient in the asymptotic expansion of
T(s;\nu)
at
s=0
.) Consequently, by assertion
(2a)
in Lemma
2.4
,
\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{\star}},X_{{{\nu}}_{\star}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)\leqslant 2
. In addition, since

F_{1}(s;{{\nu}})\!:=P^{\prime}(s;{{\nu}})=2T_{10}({{\nu}})+2\lambda T_{01}({{\nu}})s^{\lambda-1}+4T_{20}({{\nu}})s+\mathcal{F}_{3/2-\upsilon}^{\infty}({{\nu}}_{\star})

(21)

and the gradients
\nabla T_{10}({{\nu}}_{\star})
and
\nabla T_{01}({{\nu}}_{\star})
are linearly independent, by
[
30
, Proposition 4.2]
it turns out that
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(P^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,;{{\nu}}),{{\nu}}_{\star})\geqslant 2
. As a matter of fact, from the proof of that result, this lower bound is achieved by means of two different sequences of zeros of
P^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,;{{\nu}})
and, therefore, by assertion
(2b)
in Lemma
2.4
,
\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{\star}},X_{{{\nu}}_{\star}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)\geqslant 2
. Accordingly
\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{\star}},X_{{{\nu}}_{\star}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)=2

and this proves
(a).

Let us turn next to the proof of the assertions in
(b).
For this purpose, from
(\ref{F1})

and by applying Lemmas A.3 and A.4 in
[
29
]
, we get

F_{2}(s;{{\nu}})\!:=s^{2-\lambda}P^{\prime\prime}(s;{{\nu}})=2\lambda(\lambda-1)T_{01}({{\nu}})+4T_{20}({{\nu}})s^{2-\lambda}+\mathcal{F}_{1-\upsilon}^{\infty}({{\nu}}_{\star}).

(22)

Setting
U_{\varepsilon}\!:=\{{{\nu}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\|{{\nu}}-{{\nu}}_{\star}\|<\varepsilon\}
, the map

F\!:=(F_{1},F_{2})
is well-defined for
(s,{{\nu}})\in(0,\varepsilon)\times U_{\varepsilon}
taking
\varepsilon>0
small enough. Since
T_{10}({{\nu}}_{\star})=T_{01}({{\nu}}_{\star})=0
,
T_{20}({{\nu}}_{\star})\neq 0
and the gradients
\nabla T_{10}({{\nu}}_{\star})
and
\nabla T_{01}({{\nu}}_{\star})
are linearly independent, we can assume by reducing
\varepsilon>0
if necessary that
\hat{{\nu}}=\Psi({{\nu}})
, defined by means of

\Psi({{\nu}})\!:=\left(\frac{T_{10}({{\nu}})}{2T_{20}({{\nu}})},\frac{\lambda({{\nu}})T_{01}({{\nu}})}{2T_{20}({{\nu}})}\right),

(23)

is an analytic change of coordinates from
U_{\varepsilon}
to the neighbourhood
\hat{U}_{\hat{\varepsilon}}\!:=(-\hat{\varepsilon},\hat{\varepsilon})^{2}
of
(0,0)=\Psi({{\nu}}_{\star}).

Recall that our aim is to study the solutions of the system of equations
\{P^{\prime}=0,P^{\prime\prime}=0\}
which, on account of
(\ref{F1})
and
(\ref{F2})
, is equivalent to
\{F_{1}=0,F_{2}=0\}.
In order to study the latter we first lift
\Psi
to an analytic change of
variables
{\Phi}
given by

(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}})=\Phi(s,{{\nu}})\!:=(s^{2-\lambda({{\nu}})},\Psi({{\nu}})),

which (after diminishing
\varepsilon
and
\hat{\varepsilon}
if necessary) is defined from
\mathscr{U}_{\varepsilon}\!:=(0,\varepsilon)\times U_{\varepsilon}
to
\hat{\mathscr{U}}_{\hat{\varepsilon}}\!:=(0,\hat{\varepsilon})\times\hat{U}_{\hat{\varepsilon}},
and then we consider the map
\hat{F}:\hat{\mathscr{U}}_{\hat{\varepsilon}}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
defined by
\hat{F}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}})=(\hat{F}_{1}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}),\hat{F}_{2}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}))
with

\hat{F}_{1}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}})\!:=\frac{F_{1}(\Phi^{-1}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}))}{4T_{20}(\Psi^{-1}(\hat{{\nu}}))}\text{ and }\hat{F}_{2}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}})\!:=\frac{F_{2}(\Phi^{-1}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}))}{4T_{20}(\Psi^{-1}(\hat{{\nu}}))}.

By assertions
(h)
and
(c)
of Lemmas A.3 and A.4 in
[
29
]
, respectively, it follows that

\hat{F}_{1}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}})=\hat{{\nu}}_{1}+\hat{{\nu}}_{2}\hat{s}^{\frac{\hat{\lambda}-1}{2-\hat{\lambda}}}+f_{1}(\hat{s};\hat{{\nu}})\text{ and }\hat{F}_{2}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}})=(\hat{\lambda}-1)\hat{{\nu}}_{2}+\hat{s}+f_{2}(\hat{s};\hat{{\nu}}),

where
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{F}^{\infty}_{2-\upsilon}(0_{2})
for some
\upsilon>0
small enough and we set

\hat{\lambda}(\hat{{\nu}})\!:=\lambda(\Psi^{-1}(\hat{{\nu}}))
for shortness. Here we also use that
\mathcal{F}^{\infty}_{3-\upsilon}(0_{2})\subset\mathcal{F}^{\infty}_{2-\upsilon}(0_{2})
and
s=\hat{s}^{1/(2-\hat{\lambda})}\in\mathcal{F}_{2-\upsilon}^{\infty}(0_{2})
due to
\lambda({{\nu}}_{\star})=3/2
. Observe on the other hand that, via the diffeomorphism
\Phi,
the system
\{P^{\prime}(s;{{\nu}})=0,P^{\prime\prime}(s;{{\nu}})=0\}
on
\mathscr{U}_{\varepsilon}
is equivalent to the system
\{\hat{F}_{1}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}})=0,\hat{F}_{2}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}})=0\}
on
\hat{\mathscr{U}}_{\hat{\varepsilon}}
. With regard to the latter note that, by
[
29
, Lemma A.1]
, the remainders
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
extend to
\mathscr{C}^{1}
functions in a neighbourhood of
(0,0,0)
satisfying that
\nabla f_{1}(0,0,0)=\nabla f_{2}(0,0,0)=(0,0,0)
. Observe in particular that
\hat{F}_{2}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}})
extends to a
\mathscr{C}^{1}
function in a neighbourhood of
(0,0,0)
. Hence, taking
\hat{\lambda}(0,0)=3/2
into account, by the Implicit Function Theorem there exists a
\mathscr{C}^{1}
function
h(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})
in a neighbourhood of
(0,0)
such that, by shrinking
\hat{\varepsilon}>0
if necessary,

\text{$\hat{F}_{2}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}})=0$ with $(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}})\in\hat{\mathscr{U}}_{\hat{\varepsilon}}$}\Leftrightarrow\hat{{\nu}}_{2}=h(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1}).

Furthermore
h
satisfies
h(0,\hat{{\nu}}_{1})\equiv 0
and
\nabla h(0,0)=(-2,0)
. Our next task is to substitute
\hat{{\nu}}_{2}=h(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})
in
\hat{F}_{1}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1},\hat{{\nu}}_{2})=0
and analyze the resulting equation. To this end we extend
\hat{F}_{1}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1},\hat{{\nu}}_{2})|_{\hat{{\nu}}_{2}=h(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})}
on a neighbourhood of
(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})=(0,0)
by means of

(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})\mapsto\hat{{\nu}}_{1}+h(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})|\hat{s}|^{{e}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})}+\hat{f}_{1}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1}),

where
{e}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})\!:=\frac{\hat{\lambda}-1}{2-\hat{\lambda}}\big|_{\hat{\lambda}=\hat{\lambda}(\hat{{\nu}}_{1},h(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1}))}
and
\hat{f}_{1}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})=f_{1}(\hat{s};\hat{{\nu}}_{1},h(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1}))
are clearly
\mathscr{C}^{1}
in a neighbourhood of
(0,0)
. We claim that the function
g(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})\!:=h(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})|\hat{s}|^{{e}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})}
is
\mathscr{C}^{1}
in a neighbourhood of
(0,0)
as well and that its gradient vanishes at
(0,0)
. To show this notice first that
g(0,\hat{{\nu}}_{1})=0
and, consequently,
\partial_{\hat{{\nu}}_{1}}g(0,\hat{{\nu}}_{1})=0.
Moreover, using that

h(0,\hat{{\nu}}_{1})=0
, we get

\partial_{\hat{s}}g(0,\hat{{\nu}}_{1})=\lim\limits_{\hat{s}\to 0}\frac{h(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})|\hat{s}|^{{e}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})}}{\hat{s}}=\lim\limits_{\hat{s}\to 0}\frac{h(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})-h(0,\hat{{\nu}}_{1})}{\hat{s}}\lim\limits_{\hat{s}\to 0}|\hat{s}|^{{e}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})}=\partial_{\hat{s}}h(0,\hat{{\nu}}_{1})\cdot 0=0

because
h
is
\mathscr{C}^{1}
and
{e}(0,0)=1
implies
{e}(0,\hat{{\nu}}_{1})>0
for
\hat{{\nu}}_{1}\approx 0.
Similarly, if
\hat{s}\neq 0
then

\displaystyle\partial_{\hat{s}}g(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})

\displaystyle=(\partial_{\hat{s}}h(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1}))|\hat{s}|^{{e}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})}+h(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})|\hat{s}|^{{e}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})}\left(\log|\hat{s}|\partial_{\hat{s}}{e}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})+\frac{{e}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})}{\hat{s}}\right)

\displaystyle=(\partial_{\hat{s}}h(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1}))|\hat{s}|^{{e}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})}+\frac{h(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})-h(0,\hat{{\nu}}_{1})}{\hat{s}}|\hat{s}|^{{e}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})}\Big(\hat{s}\log|\hat{s}|\partial_{\hat{s}}{e}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})+{e}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})\Big)

and

\displaystyle\partial_{\hat{{\nu}}_{1}}g(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})

\displaystyle=(\partial_{\hat{{\nu}}_{1}}h(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1}))|\hat{s}|^{{e}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})}+h(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})|\hat{s}|^{{e}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})}\log|\hat{s}|\partial_{\hat{{\nu}}_{1}}{e}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})

tend to zero as
\hat{s}\to 0
uniformly on
\hat{{\nu}}_{1}\approx 0
. This clearly implies that
g
is
\mathscr{C}^{1}
in a neighbourhood of
(0,0)
and
\nabla g(0,0)=(0,0)
, so that the claim is true. Thus, by applying the Implicit Function Theorem to the “extended equation”

\hat{{\nu}}_{1}+h(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})|\hat{s}|^{{e}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})}+\hat{f}_{1}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})=0

and reducing
\hat{\varepsilon}>0
once again, we obtain a
\mathscr{C}^{1}
function
\ell(\hat{s})
on
(-\hat{\varepsilon},\hat{\varepsilon})
such that
\hat{F}_{1}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1},h(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1}))=0
with
(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}}_{1})\in(0,\hat{\varepsilon})\times(-\hat{\varepsilon},\hat{\varepsilon})
if, and only if,
\hat{{\nu}}_{1}=\ell(\hat{s})
. Moreover
\ell(0)=0
and
\ell^{\prime}(0)=0
. Accordingly, after shrinking
\hat{\varepsilon}>0
once again if necessary, we can assert that

\text{$\hat{F}_{1}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}})=\hat{F}_{2}(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}})=0$ with $(\hat{s},\hat{{\nu}})\in\hat{\mathscr{U}}_{\hat{\varepsilon}}$}\Leftrightarrow\hat{{\nu}}=(\hat{{\nu}}_{1},\hat{{\nu}}_{2})=\big(\ell(\hat{s}),h(\hat{s},\ell(\hat{s})\big).

At this point, since we reduced the original
\hat{\varepsilon}>0
,
we also diminish
\varepsilon>0
so that
\Phi(s,{{\nu}})=(s^{2-\lambda({{\nu}})},\Psi({{\nu}}))
is still a diffeomorphism from
\mathscr{U}_{\varepsilon}
into
\hat{\mathscr{U}}_{\hat{\varepsilon}}
. Then, from
(\ref{F1})
and
(\ref{F2})
, the following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

{{\nu}}_{0}\in\Delta\!:=\{{{\nu}}\in U_{\varepsilon};\text{ there exists $s\in(0,\varepsilon)$ such that $P^{\prime}(s;{{\nu}})=P^{\prime\prime}(s;{{\nu}})=0$}\}
,

(2)

\Psi({{\nu}}_{0})=\big(\ell(\hat{s}_{0}),h(\hat{s}_{0},\ell(\hat{s}_{0}))\big)
for some
\hat{s}_{0}\in(0,\hat{\varepsilon})
,

(3)

{{\nu}}_{0}=\delta(t_{0})
for some
t_{0}\in(0,\hat{\varepsilon})
, where

\delta(t)\!:=\Psi^{-1}\big(\ell(t),h(t,\ell(t))\big)
.

It is clear from these equivalences that
{\delta}\!:{(-\hat{\varepsilon},\hat{\varepsilon})}\longrightarrow{U_{\varepsilon}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{1}
parametrized curve with
\delta(0)={{\nu}}_{\star}
satisfying that
\delta\big((0,\hat{\varepsilon})\big)=\Delta
. One can easily verify, taking
\ell^{\prime}(0)=0
and
\partial_{1}h(0,0)\neq 0
into account, together with the definition of
\Psi
in
(\ref{Psi})
, that
\delta^{\prime}(0)
is a non-zero vector tangent to
\{{{\nu}}\in U_{\varepsilon};\;T_{10}({{\nu}})=0\}.
In particular, on account of
\delta^{\prime}(0)\neq(0,0)
and by reducing
\hat{\varepsilon}>0,
we have that
\delta
is one-to-one. This proves the assertion
(b2)
in the statement.
Due to
\mathcal{Z}_{0}(P^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,;{{\nu}}),{{\nu}}_{\star})\leqslant 2
, and after shrinking
\varepsilon>0
if necessary, note also that the zeros of
P^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,;{{\nu}})
on
(0,\varepsilon)
can have at most multiplicity two. Therefore, since the interior of
\Delta=\delta\big((0,\hat{\varepsilon})\big)
is empty (as a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
), by applying Lemma
2.15
we can assert that each
{{\nu}}_{0}\in\Delta
is a local bifurcation value of the period function at the interior, which shows the validity of
(b1)
in the statement.
With regard to the assertions in
(b3)
, we note that the uniqueness of
s_{{\nu}}
and
\lim_{{{\nu}}\to{{\nu}}_{\star}}s_{{\nu}}=0^{+}
follow from the point
(2)
above using that
\hat{s}\mapsto h(\hat{s},\ell(\hat{s}))
is invertible at
\hat{s}=0
and that, by definition,
\hat{s}=s^{2-\lambda({{\nu}})}.

On the other hand, since
P^{\prime\prime}(s_{{\nu}};{{\nu}})=0
for all
{{\nu}}\in\Delta
, from
(\ref{F2})
we get that
T_{01}({{\nu}})T_{20}({{\nu}})<0
for all
{{\nu}}\in\Delta
. Here we also use that
\lim_{{{\nu}}\to{{\nu}}_{\star}}s_{{\nu}}=0^{+}
to take advantage of the properties of the remainder and the fact that
\lambda({{\nu}}_{\star})=3/2.
By arguing similarly, on account of
P^{\prime}(s_{{\nu}};{{\nu}})=0
for all
{{\nu}}\in\Delta
, from
(\ref{F1})
it follows that
T_{01}({{\nu}})T_{10}({{\nu}})<0
for all
{{\nu}}\in\Delta
. Taking this into account the assertion in
(b4)
is a consequence of
T_{20}({{\nu}}_{\star})<0,
see
(b)
in Proposition
3.3
.
Finally, in order to prove
(b5)
, let us consider
{{\nu}}\in\Delta
and note that from
(\ref{F1})
we obtain

\lim_{{{\nu}}\to{{\nu}}_{\star}}\left.\big(\partial_{{{\nu}}_{1}}P^{\prime}(s;{{\nu}}),\partial_{{{\nu}}_{2}}P^{\prime}(s;{{\nu}})\big)\right|_{s=s_{{\nu}}}=2\nabla T_{10}({{\nu}}_{\star}),

where we use that the flatness of the remainder
\mathcal{F}_{3/2-\upsilon}^{\infty}({{\nu}}_{\star})
is preserved after derivation with respect to parameters, see Definition
A.1
. Similarly, in this case from
(\ref{F2})
and using also
P^{\prime\prime}(s_{{\nu}};{{\nu}})\equiv 0
, we get

\lim_{{{\nu}}\to{{\nu}}_{\star}}\left.s^{2-\lambda({{\nu}})}\big(\partial_{{{\nu}}_{1}}P^{\prime\prime}(s;{{\nu}}),\partial_{{{\nu}}_{2}}P^{\prime\prime}(s;{{\nu}})\big)\right|_{s=s_{{\nu}}}=\frac{3}{2}\nabla T_{01}({{\nu}}_{\star}).

Thus, since the vectors
\nabla T_{10}({{\nu}}_{\star})
and
\nabla T_{01}({{\nu}}_{\star})
are linearly independent, so they are

\nabla P^{\prime}(s;{{\nu}})|_{s=s_{{\nu}}}
and
\nabla P^{\prime\prime}(s;{{\nu}})|_{s=s_{{\nu}}}
for all
{{\nu}}\in U_{\varepsilon}
(after shrinking
\varepsilon>0
if necessary). That being said, we fix any
{{\nu}}_{0}\in\Delta
and compute the second order Taylor’s expansion of
P^{\prime}(s;{{\nu}})
at
s=s_{{{\nu}}_{0}},

P^{\prime}(s;{{\nu}})=P^{\prime}(s_{{{\nu}}_{0}};{{\nu}})+P^{\prime\prime}(s_{{{\nu}}_{0}};{{\nu}})(s-s_{{{\nu}}_{0}})+\mbox{\rm o}(s-s_{{{\nu}}_{0}}).

Then, due to
P^{\prime}(s;{{\nu}}_{0})\not\equiv 0,

P^{\prime}(s_{{{\nu}}_{0}};{{\nu}}_{0})=P^{\prime\prime}(s_{{{\nu}}_{0}};{{\nu}}_{0})=0
and the fact that the gradients
\nabla P^{\prime}(s_{{{\nu}}_{0}};{{\nu}})
and
\nabla P^{\prime\prime}(s_{{{\nu}}_{0}};{{\nu}})
are linearly independent at
{{\nu}}={{\nu}}_{0},
the application of
[
30
, Proposition 4.2]
shows that for each open neighbourhood
V
of
{{\nu}}_{0}
there exist
\bar{{\nu}}\in V
and two
s_{1},s_{2}\in(0,\varepsilon)
such that
P^{\prime}(s_{1};\bar{{\nu}})=P^{\prime}(s_{2};\bar{{\nu}})=0.
This proves the validity of the assertion in
(b5)
and completes the proof of the result.
