Figure 5:
Arrangement of the three types of local bifurcation curve (inner boundary, interior and outer boundary) near the parameter values
\nu=L_{3}
and
\nu=\nu_{\star}
, see
(\ref{Li})
and Proposition
4.4
, respectively. We refer the reader to Remark
4
for a detailed explication.

Let us finish this section contextualizing the results in Proposition
4.4
. In Figure
5
we display the ellipse
\Gamma_{C}
that consists of local bifurcation values of the period function at the inner boundary (i.e., the center) of
\mathscr{P}
. It corresponds, see
[
6
, Lemma 3.1]
, to the vanishing of the first period constant

p_{2}({{\nu}})=10D^{2}+10DF-D+4F^{2}-5F+1.

Moreover the curve
\Gamma_{B}
consists of local bifurcation values at the outer boundary (i.e., the polycycle) of
\mathscr{P}
, see
[
23
, Theorem A]
. It is made of the arc
\{D=\mathcal{G}(F):F\in(1,\frac{3}{2})\}
joining
(-\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2})
and
(-\frac{1}{2},1)
together with several straight segments. According to Proposition
4.4
and
[
23
, Theorem 4.3]
, respectively, the germs of curve
\Delta
and
\delta_{3}
are inside the set of local bifurcation values of the period function at the interior of
\mathscr{P}
. At this moment we do not have any analytical tool to fully characterize this set. We conjecture that
\Delta
and
\delta_{3}
connect with each other to delimit a region of parameters for which the corresponding center has exactly two critical periodic orbits. With regard to this conjecture
it is proved in
[
23
, Theorem 5.2]
that the center of any parameter inside one of the two light gray regions has at least two critical periodic orbits. The boundary of these regions is inside
\Gamma_{C},

\Gamma_{B}
and
\Gamma_{0}.
For completeness let us explain that the curve
\Gamma_{0}
consists of those parameters such that the period function tends to
2\pi
as the periodic orbits tend to the outer boundary.

\square

5
Proof of Theorem
A

The statement covers all the parameters
\nu_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
outside the vertical segments

\ell_{0}\!:=\{D=-1,F\in[0,1]\}\cup\{D=0,F\in[0,\frac{1}{4})\}.
For simplicity in the exposition, instead of proving the five assertions in the statement separately, we split
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\ell_{0}
depending on the result and tool applied to study the corresponding criticality. For reader’s convenience we enumerate the different cases that we obtain in this way.

1.

Let us consider first of all the set
\ell_{1}\!:=\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus(\Gamma_{B}\cup\Gamma_{U})
,
where recall (see Figure
1
) that
\Gamma_{U}
is the
union of the dotted straight lines, whatever its colour is,
and
\Gamma_{B}
is the Jordan curve in boldface type. Then, by
[
23
, Theorem A]
,
we know that any
\nu_{0}\in\ell_{1}
is a local regular value of the period function at
the outer boundary, see
(c)
in Definition
2
. On account of this, by
(b)
in Lemma
2.16
we get that

\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{0}},X_{{{\nu}}_{0}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)=0
. Here we also use that the period annuli of the
Loud’s centers vary continuously, see Remark
2
, and that the outer boundary of

\mathscr{P}_{\nu}
for
\nu\notin\Gamma_{B}\cup\Gamma_{U}
is a hyperbolic polycycle,
see for instance
[
23
, §3.1]
.

2.

The criticality at

\ell_{2}\!:=\{D=-\frac{1}{2},F\in(\frac{1}{2},1)\}\cup\{F=\frac{1}{2},D\in(-\frac{1}{2},0)\}

and
\ell_{3}\!:=\{F=\frac{1}{2},D\in(-1,-\frac{1}{2})\}
follows
from the results in Section
3.1
. In this case
\sigma(s;\nu)=(1-s,0)

is a parametrization of the outer boundary of the
period annulus verifying the hypothesis

(a)
,
(b)
and
(c)
in Lemma
2.4
. Moreover denoting by
P(s;\nu)
the period of the periodic orbit of
X_{\nu}

passing through
\sigma(s;\nu)
, we have that
P(s;\nu)=2T(s;\nu)
, where
T
is the Dulac map considered
in Proposition
3.2
. By applying this result we know that the first non-vanishing coefficient
in the asymptotic expansion of
P(s;{{\nu}})
at
s=0
is the third one for all
{{\nu}}\in\ell_{2}.
Therefore

[
30
, Theorem C]
implies that

\mathcal{Z}_{0}(P^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,;\nu),\nu_{0})\leqslant 1
for all
\nu_{0}\in\ell_{2}.

On account
of this, by assertion
(2a)
in Lemma
2.4
it follows that

\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{0}},X_{\nu_{0}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)\leqslant 1
for all
\nu_{0}\in\ell_{2}.

On the other hand, due to

\ell_{2}\subset\Gamma_{B}
, we know by
[
23
, Theorem A]
that these parameters are local
bifurcation values of the period function at the outer boundary. Thus, since the period annuli of
the Loud’s centers vary continuously (see Remark
2
), by applying
(a)
in Lemma
2.16
we get that

\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{0}},X_{\nu_{0}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)\geqslant 1
for all
\nu_{0}\in\ell_{2}.

Hence
\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{0}},X_{\nu_{0}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)=1
for all
\nu_{0}\in\ell_{2}.

We turn now to the criticality in the segment
\ell_{3}.
So let us fix any

\nu_{0}=(D_{0},\frac{1}{2})
with
D_{0}\in(-1,-\frac{1}{2})
and note that then,
by
(d)
in Proposition
3.2
,

T^{\prime}(s;{{\nu}})=-\rho_{4}({{\nu}})(F-1/2)^{2}\big(\lambda\omega_{1-\lambda}(s)-1\big)+\rho_{5}({{\nu}})(D+1/2)+\rho_{6}({{\nu}})(F-1/2)+\mathscr{R}(s;\nu),

where
\mathscr{R}\in\mathcal{F}_{1-\upsilon}^{\infty}({{\nu}}_{0})
for all
\upsilon>0
small enough. To obtain
the derivative of the Dulac time,
we use that
\partial_{s}(s\omega_{\alpha}(s))=(1-\alpha)\omega_{\alpha}(s)-1
and that,
by
(f)
in Lemma A.3 in
[
29
]
,

\partial_{s}\mathcal{F}_{2-\upsilon}^{\infty}({{\nu}}_{0})\subset\mathcal{F}_{1-\upsilon}^{\infty}({{\nu}}_{0}).
From this equality,
since
\lambda\omega_{1-\lambda}(s)-1
tends to
+\infty
as
(s,\nu)\to(0,\nu_{0})
due
to
\lambda(\nu_{0})=1
(see Definition
3
),
\mathscr{R}(s;\nu)
tends to 0 as
(s,\nu)\to(0,\nu_{0})
,

\rho_{i}(\nu_{0})>0
and
D_{0}+\frac{1}{2}<0
, we can
assert the existence of an open neighbourhood
V
of
\nu_{0}
and
\varepsilon>0
such that

P^{\prime}(s;\nu)=2T^{\prime}(s;\nu)<0
for all
\nu\in V
and
s\in(0,\varepsilon).
Consequently

\mathcal{Z}_{0}(P^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,;\nu),\nu_{0})=0
and so, by applying
(2c)
in Lemma
2.4
, we conclude that

\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{0}},X_{{{\nu}}_{0}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)=0.

3.

We turn next to study the horizontal segments

\ell_{4}\!:=\big\{F=2,D\in(-2,0)\setminus\{-\frac{1}{2}\}\big\}

and the curve

\ell_{5}\!:=\big\{D=\mathcal{G}(F):F\in(1,\frac{3}{2})\big\}.
Here we set

\nu_{\star}\!:=(\mathcal{G}(4/3),4/3)
because this parameter yields to a distinguished case.

We begin by noting (see the first paragraph in Section
3.2
) that
\sigma(s;\nu)=(p_{1}-s,0)

is a parametrization of the
outer boundary of the period annulus verifying the assumptions in Lemma
2.4
and that if we denote the period of
the periodic orbit of
X_{\nu}

passing through
\sigma(s;\nu)
by
P(s;\nu)
then
P(s;\nu)=2T(s;\nu)
,
where
T
is the Dulac map considered
in Proposition
3.3
. Thus, by applying first that result and then
[
30
, Theorem C]
we obtain that

\mathcal{Z}_{0}(P^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,;\nu),\nu_{0})\leqslant 1
for all
\nu_{0}\in\ell_{4}\cup\ell_{5}\setminus\{\nu_{\star}\}
.

Moreover
[
23
, Theorem A]
shows that these parameters are local
bifurcation values of the period function at the outer boundary because

\ell_{4}\cup\ell_{5}\subset\Gamma_{B}
. Since the period annuli of
the Loud’s centers vary continuously (see Remark
2
), by applying
(a)
in Lemma
2.16
we have

\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{0}},X_{\nu_{0}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)\geqslant 1
for all

\nu_{0}\in\ell_{4}\cup\ell_{5}\setminus\{\nu_{\star}\}.

Therefore
\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{0}},X_{\nu_{0}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)=1
for all

\nu_{0}\in\ell_{4}\cup\ell_{5}\setminus\{\nu_{\star}\}.

On the other hand we have that

\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{\star}},X_{\nu_{\star}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)=2
by assertion
(a)
in Proposition
4.4
.
Finally the fact that there is a curve of local
bifurcation values of the period function at the interior of
\mathscr{P}
arriving at
\nu=\nu_{\star}

tangent to
\Gamma_{B}
follows from assertion
(b)
in the same result.

4.

Next we analyze the parameters in the segment
\ell_{6}\!:=\{F=1,D\in(-1,0)\}
, that corresponds to a case in
which there is a saddle-node singularity at the outer boundary of the period annulus.
This is treated in Section
3.3
, where we introduce the map

\sigma(s;{{\nu}})\!:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}(1-s,0)&\text{ if $F\leqslant 1,$}\\[2.0pt]
(p_{1}-s,0)&\text{ if $F>1,$}\end{array}\right.

that provides a parametrization of the outer boundary of the period annulus verifying the assumptions
in Lemma
2.4
. In addition if we denote by
P(s;\nu)
the period of the periodic orbit of
X_{\nu}
passing
through
\sigma(s;\nu)
then
P(s;\nu)=2T(s;\nu)
, where
T
is the Dulac map considered in Proposition
3.6
.
From that result we get the existence of an open neighbourhood
\mathscr{U}
of
\ell_{6}=(-1,0)\times\{1\}

such that

P^{\prime}(s;{{\nu}})=2T_{1}({{\nu}})+4T_{2}({{\nu}})s+s\hat{h}(s;{{\nu}})

with
T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathscr{C}^{0}(\mathscr{U})
and where
\hat{h}(s;{{\nu}})
and
s\partial_{s}\hat{h}(s;{{\nu}})

tend to zero as
s\to 0^{+}

uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathscr{U}
. We know
moreover that
T_{1}(\nu)=0
if, and only if,
\nu=\nu_{\star}\!:=(-\frac{1}{2},1)

and that
T_{2}(\nu_{\star})\neq 0.

If we take any
\nu_{0}\in\ell_{6}\setminus\{\nu_{\star}\}
then, thanks to the good properties of the remainder,
we get that
\lim_{(s,\nu)\to(0,\nu_{0})}P^{\prime}(s;\nu)=2T_{1}(\nu_{0})\neq 0
and this easily implies

\mathcal{Z}_{0}(P^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,;\nu),\nu_{0})=0.
Hence, by applying assertion
(2c)
in Lemma
2.4
,

\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{0}},X_{\nu_{0}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)=0
for all
\nu_{0}\in\ell_{6}\setminus\{\nu_{\star}\}
.

In order to study the criticality of
\nu_{\star}
we observe that

\lim_{(s,\nu)\to(0,\nu_{\star})}P^{\prime\prime}(s;\nu)=4T_{2}(\nu_{\star})\neq 0
and, consequently,

\mathcal{Z}_{0}(P^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,;\nu),\nu_{0})\leqslant 1
by Rolle’s Theorem. Therefore, by assertion
(2a)
in Lemma
2.4
,

\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{\star}},X_{\nu_{\star}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)\leqslant 1.
On the other hand, the application
of
[
23
, Theorem A]
together with
(a)
in Lemma
2.16
shows that

\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{\star}},X_{\nu_{\star}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)\geqslant 1
due to
\nu_{\star}\in\Gamma_{B}.

Hence
\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{\star}},X_{\nu_{\star}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)=1
.

5.

We proceed with the study of the segment
\ell_{7}\!:=\{F=0,D\in(-1,0)\}

which, as in the previous case, corresponds to
period annuli having a saddle-node singularity at the outer boundary. In order to compute the
criticality of any
\nu_{0}\in\ell_{7}
we apply the results obtained in
[
27
]
. In that paper it is proved that
for each
\nu_{0}\in\ell_{7}
there exist
\delta>0
, an open neighbourhood
V

of
\nu_{0}
and a continuous function

{\sigma}\!:{[0,\delta)\times V}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{RP}^{2}}
verifying the hypothesis in Lemma
2.4
. Moreover, denoting
the period of the periodic orbit of
X_{\nu}
passing through
\sigma(s;\nu)
by
P(s;\nu)
, the proof of

[
27
, Theorem B]
shows that
P^{\prime}(s;\nu)
tends to
-\infty
as
(s,\nu)\to(0,\nu_{0}).
Consequently

\mathcal{Z}_{0}(P^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,;\nu),\nu_{0})=0
and hence, by applying
(c)
in Lemma
2.4
, we get that

\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{0}},X_{\nu_{0}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)=0
.

6.

We analyze next the parameters inside the segment
\ell_{8}\!:=\big\{D=0,F\in[\frac{1}{4},2]\big\}.
So let us
fix any
\nu_{0}=(0,F_{0})
with
F_{0}\in[\frac{1}{4},2].
By
[
23
, Theorem A]
we can assert
that if
F_{0}\in[\frac{1}{2},2]
then
\nu_{0}
is a local bifurcation value of the period function at the outer
boundary. On the other hand, if
F_{0}\in[\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2}]
then we can conclude the same by applying

[
28
, Theorem B]
. Hence, since the period annuli of the
Loud’s centers vary continuously, see Remark
2
, assertion
(a)
in Lemma
2.16
shows that

\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{0}},X_{\nu_{0}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)\geqslant 1
for all
\nu_{0}\in\ell_{8}.

7.

From the results in
[
19
,
39
]
it follows that

\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{0}},X_{{{\nu}}_{0}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)=0
for any parameter
\nu_{0}
inside the
set

\ell_{9}\!:=\{D=0,F\notin[0,2]\}\cup\{D=-1,F<0\}\cup\{D+F=0,F<0\}
.
Indeed, in those papers the authors determine
a region
M
in the parameter plane for which the corresponding centers have a globally monotonic period
function. Taking this into account the assertion follows easily from the fact that

\ell_{9}
is contained in the interior of
M
, see Definition
1
.

8.

We consider now the half-line
\ell_{10}\!:=\{D+F=0,F>1\},

so let us take a parameter
\nu_{0}=(-F_{0},F_{0})
with
F_{0}>1.

In this case the assertions with regard to its criticality
follow from the results in
[
24
]
.
It is proved there that there exists a function
\xi=\xi(\nu)
in a neighbourhood
U
of
\nu_{0}

such that
\sigma(s;\nu)=\big(0,\xi(\nu)(1-s)\big)
is a
\mathscr{C}^{0}
map on

[0,\delta)\times U
verifying the hypothesis

(a)
,
(b)
and
(c)
in Lemma
2.4
. Moreover if we denote by
P(s;\nu)
the period of
the periodic orbit of
X_{\nu}

passing through
\sigma(s;\nu)
then
[
24
, Theorem B]
shows that

•

\mathcal{Z}_{0}(P^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,;\nu),\nu_{0})=0
if
F_{0}\notin[3/2,2]
,

•

\mathcal{Z}_{0}(P^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,;\nu),\nu_{0})=1
if
F_{0}\in[3/2,2)
and

•

\mathcal{Z}_{0}(P^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,;\nu),\nu_{0})=2
if
F_{0}=2
.

In the first case

\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{0}},X_{{{\nu}}_{0}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)=0
by
(2c)
in Lemma
2.4
, whereas
in the second case the combination of
(2a)
and
(2b)

implies
\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{0}},X_{{{\nu}}_{0}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)=1.

In the third case, by applying
(2a)

we get
\mathrm{Crit}\bigl((\Pi_{{{\nu}}_{0}},X_{{{\nu}}_{0}}),X_{{{\nu}}}\bigr)\leqslant 2.

To show that this upper bound is attained we also apply
(2b)
in Lemma
2.4
but to this end we must
check the assumption that for each open neighbourhood
V
of
\nu_{0}=(-2,2)
and
\delta>0
there exist
distinct
s_{1},s_{2}\in(0,\delta)
and
\hat{\nu}\in V
such that
P^{\prime}(s_{i};\hat{\nu})=0
for
i=1,2.
To verify this
we note first, see
[
24
, §4]
, that we can write

P^{\prime}(s;\nu)=\delta_{1}(\nu)f_{1}(s;\nu)+\delta_{2}(\nu)f_{2}(s;\nu)+f_{3}(s;\nu)

where the coefficients
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
are independent at
\nu_{0}
in the sense of

[
30
, Definition 4.1]
and, for
i=1,2,

\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}\frac{f_{i+1}(s;\nu)}{f_{i}(s;\nu)}=0
.
On account of this and
P^{\prime}(s;\nu_{0})\not\equiv 0,
the fact that the mentioned assumption is verified
follows from the proof of
[
30
, Proposition 4.2]
. Related with this let us also mention,
see again
[
24
, §4]
, that the ordered set

(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})
is an extended complete Chebyshev system
on
(0,\varepsilon)

for
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small (see
[
13
]
for a definition).

On the other hand, by assertion
(a)
in
[
24
, Theorem C]
, there exist a neighbourhood
U

of
\nu_{0}=(-2,2)
,
s_{0}>0
and a injective
\mathscr{C}^{0}
curve
{\rho}\!:{(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)}\longrightarrow{U}
satisfying

\rho(0)=(-2,2)
and

\rho((0,\varepsilon))=\Delta\!:=\{\nu\in U;\text{ there exists $s\in(0,s_{0})$ such that $P^{\prime}(s;\nu)=P^{\prime\prime}(s;\nu)=0$}\}.

Furthermore, assertion
(b)
in that result shows that the curve
\Delta
has an exponentially flat contact
with the straight line
\{F=2\}
at
\nu_{0}=(-2,2)
, see Figure
6
.
Since the interior of
\Delta
is clearly empty and
the Chebyshev property explained above prevents the zeros of

P^{\prime}(\,\cdot\,;\nu)
to have multiplicity greater than 2, the application of Lemma
2.15
shows
that
\Delta
consists of local bifurcation values of the period function at the interior.

9.

Finally the fact that the criticality at the outer boundary of the isochrones

\nu_{1}\!:=(-\frac{1}{2},2)
and
\nu_{2}\!:=(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
is 1 follows from Propositions
4.2

and
4.3
, respectively.

Since
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\ell_{0}=\left(\cup_{i=1}^{10}\ell_{i}\right)\cup\{\nu_{1}\}\cup\{\nu_{2}\},
this concludes the proof of the result.

Appendix A
Coefficient formulas

A.1
Previous results about the Dulac time

This appendix is entirely devoted to the proof of Propositions
3.2
and
3.3
in Section
3
. For the parameter values under consideration in both results, and thanks to the symmetry of the vector field
X_{\nu}
in
(\ref{sist_loud})
, it turns out that the period function is twice the Dulac time associated to the passage through a hyperbolic saddle at infinity. The asymptotic expansion of this type of passage is the subject of our recent papers
[
29
,
30
,
31
]
and in order to prove the results in Section
3
we strongly rely on the tools developed there. For this reason we first summarize for reader’s convenience the definitions and results from those papers that are indispensable here. We recap the results in three theorems. In short, Theorem
A.3
will provide us with the monomial scale needed in each asymptotic expansion, which only depends on the hyperbolicity ratio of the saddle, whereas Theorem
A.4
will give the explicit expression of their coefficients in terms of a sort of Mellin transform that is introduced in Theorem
A.5
.

In order to facilitate the application of the above-mentioned results we particularize them to fit in the context needed to prove Propositions
3.2
and
3.3
. Thus, following the notation that we use in
[
30
]
, let us consider the parameter
{{\hat{\mu}}}\!:=(\lambda,\mu)\in\hat{W}\!:=(0,+\infty)\times W
, where
W
is an open set of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, and the family of vector fields
\{X_{{{\hat{\mu}}}}\}_{{{\hat{\mu}}}\in\hat{W}}
with

X_{{\hat{\mu}}}({x_{1}},{x_{2}})\!:=\frac{1}{x_{2}}\Big({x_{1}}P_{1}({x_{1}},{x_{2}};{{\hat{\mu}}})\partial_{x_{1}}+{x_{2}}P_{2}({x_{1}},{x_{2}};{{\hat{\mu}}})\partial_{x_{2}}\Big),

(24)

where

•

P_{1}
and
P_{2}
belong to
\mathscr{C}^{\omega}(\mathscr{U}\!\times\!\hat{W})
for some open set
\mathscr{U}
of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
containing the origin,

•

P_{1}({x_{1}},0;{{\hat{\mu}}})>0
and
P_{2}(0,{x_{2}};{{\hat{\mu}}})<0
for all
({x_{1}},0),(0,{x_{2}})\in\mathscr{U}
and
{{\hat{\mu}}}\in\hat{W},

•

\lambda=-\frac{P_{2}(0,0;{{\hat{\mu}}})}{P_{1}(0,0;{{\hat{\mu}}})}
.

Moreover, for
i=1,2,
let
{\sigma_{i}}\!:{(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\times\hat{W}}\longrightarrow{\Sigma_{i}}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\omega}
transverse section to
X_{{{\hat{\mu}}}}
at
x_{i}=0
defined by

\sigma_{i}(s;{{\hat{\mu}}})=\bigl(\sigma_{i1}(s;{{\hat{\mu}}}),\sigma_{i2}(s;{{\hat{\mu}}})\bigr)

such that
\sigma_{1}(0,{{\hat{\mu}}})\in\{(0,x_{2});x_{2}>0\}
and
\sigma_{2}(0,{{\hat{\mu}}})\in\{(x_{1},0);x_{1}>0\}

for all
{{\hat{\mu}}}\in\hat{W}.
Then Theorem
A.3
is concerned with the time
T(s;{{\hat{\mu}}})
that spends the solution of
X_{{\hat{\mu}}}
passing through the point
\sigma_{1}(s;{{\hat{\mu}}})\in\Sigma_{1}
to arrive at
\Sigma_{2},
see Figure
7
. More concretely it shows that
T(s;{{\hat{\mu}}})
has an asymptotic expansion at
s=0
with the remainder having good flatness properties with respect to the parameters. We specify these properties in the following two definitions.

Figure 7:
Definition of
T(\,\cdot\,;{{\hat{\mu}}})
, where
\varphi(t,p;{{\hat{\mu}}})
is the solution of
X_{{\hat{\mu}}}
passing through the point
p\in\mathscr{U}
at time
t=0.

Given
K\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\cup\{\infty\}
and an open subset
U\subset\hat{W}\subset\mathbb{R}^{N+1},
we say that a function
\psi(s;{{\hat{\mu}}})
belongs to the class
\mathscr{C}^{K}_{s>0}(U)
if there
exists an open neighbourhood
\Omega
of

\{(s,{{\hat{\mu}}})\in\mathbb{R}^{N+2};s=0,{{\hat{\mu}}}\in U\}=\{0\}\times U

in
\mathbb{R}^{N+2}
such that
(s,{{\hat{\mu}}})\mapsto\psi(s;{{\hat{\mu}}})
is
\mathscr{C}^{K}
on
\Omega\cap\big((0,+\infty)\times U\big)
.

\square

Consider
K\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\cup\{\infty\}
and an open subset
U\subset\hat{W}\subset\mathbb{R}^{N+1}.
Given
L\in\mathbb{R}
and
{{\hat{\mu}}}_{0}\in U
, we say that
\psi(s;{{\hat{\mu}}})\in\mathscr{C}^{K}_{s>0}(U)
is
(L,K)
-flat with respect to
s
at
{{\hat{\mu}}}_{0}
, and we write
\psi\in\mathcal{F}_{L}^{K}({{\hat{\mu}}}_{0})
, if for each
\nu=(\nu_{0},\ldots,\nu_{N+1})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{N+2}
with
|\nu|=\nu_{0}+\cdots+\nu_{N+1}\leqslant K
there exist a neighbourhood
V
of
{{\hat{\mu}}}_{0}
and
C,s_{0}>0
such that

\left|\frac{\partial^{|\nu|}\psi(s;{{\hat{\mu}}})}{\partial s^{\nu_{0}}\partial{{\hat{\mu}}}_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\cdots\partial{{\hat{\mu}}}_{N+1}^{\nu_{N+1}}}\right|\leqslant Cs^{L-\nu_{0}}\text{ for all $s\in(0,s_{0})$ and ${{\hat{\mu}}}\in V$.}

If
W
is a (not necessarily open) subset of
U
then define
\mathcal{F}_{L}^{K}(W)\!:=\bigcap_{{{\hat{\mu}}}_{0}\in W}\mathcal{F}_{L}^{K}({{\hat{\mu}}}_{0}).

\square

Next result merges the statements of Theorem 1.6, Theorem 4.3 and Corollary B in
[
31
]
. Following the notation in that paper, we particularise them to the case
(n_{1},n_{2})=(0,1)
for simplicity. Moreover, for the sake of shortness, we only include those items that will be used in the present paper.

Theorem A.3
.

Let
T(s;{{\hat{\mu}}})
be the Dulac time of the hyperbolic saddle
(\ref{X})
from
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
. Then, setting
D_{00}=\emptyset
,
D_{10}=\frac{1}{\mathbb{N}}
,
D_{01}=\mathbb{N}
,
D_{20}=\frac{2}{\mathbb{N}}
and
D_{02}=\frac{\mathbb{N}}{2},
for each
(i,j)\in\{(0,0),(1,0),(0,1),(2,0),(0,2)\}
there exists a meromorphic function
T_{ij}({{\hat{\mu}}})
on
\hat{W}=(0,+\infty)\times W
, having poles only along
D_{ij}\times W
, such that the following assertions hold:

(1)

If
\lambda_{0}\in(1,2)
then
T(s;{{\hat{\mu}}})=T_{00}({{\hat{\mu}}})+T_{10}({{\hat{\mu}}})s+T_{01}({{\hat{\mu}}})s^{\lambda}+T_{20}({{\hat{\mu}}})s^{2}+\mathcal{F}_{L}^{\infty}(\{\lambda_{0}\}\times W)
for any
L\in\big[2,\lambda_{0}+1\big)
.

(2)

If
\lambda_{0}>2
then
T(s;{{\hat{\mu}}})=T_{00}({{\hat{\mu}}})+T_{10}({{\hat{\mu}}})s+T_{20}({{\hat{\mu}}})s^{2}+\mathcal{F}_{L}^{\infty}(\{\lambda_{0}\}\times W)
for any
L\in\big[2,\min(3,\lambda_{0})\big)
.

(3)

If
\lambda_{0}=\frac{1}{2}
then
T(s;{{\hat{\mu}}})=T_{00}({{\hat{\mu}}})+T_{01}({{\hat{\mu}}})s^{\lambda}+s\boldsymbol{T}_{10}^{\lambda_{0}}(\omega;{{\hat{\mu}}})+\mathcal{F}_{L}^{\infty}(\{\lambda_{0}\}\times W)
for any
L\in[1,\frac{3}{2})
, where
\omega=\omega(s;\alpha)
,
\alpha=1-2\lambda
and
\boldsymbol{T}_{10}^{\lambda_{0}}(w;{{\hat{\mu}}})\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\hat{U})[w]
for some open neighbourhood
\hat{U}
of
\{\lambda_{0}\}\times W
. Moreover

\boldsymbol{T}_{10}^{\lambda_{0}}(\omega;{{\hat{\mu}}})=T_{10}({{\hat{\mu}}})+T_{02}({{\hat{\mu}}})(1+\alpha\omega)\,\text{ for $\lambda\neq\lambda_{0}$}.

(4)

If
\lambda_{0}=1
then

T(s;{{\hat{\mu}}})=T_{00}({{\hat{\mu}}})+s\boldsymbol{T}_{10}^{\lambda_{0}}(\omega;{{\hat{\mu}}})+\mathcal{F}_{L}^{\infty}(\{\lambda_{0}\}\times W)
for any
L\in(1,2)
, where
\omega=\omega(s;\alpha)
,
\alpha=1-\lambda
and
\boldsymbol{T}_{10}^{\lambda_{0}}(w;{{\hat{\mu}}})\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\hat{U})[w]
for some open neighbourhood
\hat{U}
of
\{\lambda_{0}\}\times W
. Moreover

\boldsymbol{T}_{10}^{\lambda_{0}}(\omega;{{\hat{\mu}}})=T_{10}({{\hat{\mu}}})+T_{01}({{\hat{\mu}}})(1+\alpha\omega)\,\text{ for $\lambda\neq\lambda_{0}$}.

(5)

If
\lambda_{0}=2
then
T(s;{{\hat{\mu}}})=T_{00}({{\hat{\mu}}})+T_{10}({{\hat{\mu}}})s+s^{2}\boldsymbol{T}_{20}^{\lambda_{0}}(\omega;{{\hat{\mu}}})+\mathcal{F}_{L}^{\infty}(\{\lambda_{0}\}\times W)
for any
L\in\big[2,3\big)
, where
\omega=\omega(s;\alpha)
,
\alpha=2-\lambda
and
\boldsymbol{T}_{20}^{\lambda_{0}}(w;{{\hat{\mu}}})\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\hat{U})[w]
for some open neighbourhood
\hat{U}
of
\{\lambda_{0}\}\times W
. Moreover

\boldsymbol{T}_{20}^{\lambda_{0}}(\omega;{{\hat{\mu}}})=T_{20}({{\hat{\mu}}})+T_{01}({{\hat{\mu}}})\left(1+\alpha\omega\right)\,\text{ for $\lambda\neq\lambda_{0}$}.

We focus next on the expression of the coefficients
T_{ij}
and the result that we state below in this regard follows from assertion
(c)
in
[
31
, Theorem A]
particularized to
(n_{1},n_{2})=(0,1)
. In its statement we use the following functions:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle L_{1}(u)\!:=\exp\int_{0}^{u}\left(\frac{P_{1}(0,z)}{P_{2}(0,z)}+\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\frac{dz}{z}&\displaystyle L_{2}(u)\!:=\exp\int_{0}^{u}\left(\frac{P_{2}(z,0)}{P_{1}(z,0)}+{\lambda}\right)\frac{dz}{z}\\[15.0pt]
\displaystyle A_{1}(u)\!:=\frac{1}{P_{2}(0,u)}&\displaystyle A_{2}(u)\!:=\frac{L_{2}(u)}{P_{1}(u,0)}\\[15.0pt]
\displaystyle M_{1}(u)\!:=L_{1}(u)\partial_{1}\!\left(\frac{P_{1}}{P_{2}}\right)(0,u)&\displaystyle B_{1}(u)\!:=L_{1}(u)\partial_{1}P_{2}^{-1}(0,u)\\[15.0pt]
\displaystyle C_{1}(u)\!:=L_{1}^{2}(u)\partial_{1}^{2}P_{2}^{-1}(0,u)&\displaystyle\hskip-51.21504pt+2L_{1}(u)\hat{M}_{1}(1/\lambda,u)\partial_{1}P_{2}^{-1}(0,u)\end{array}

(25)

Here, given
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and a real valued function
f(x)
that is
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}
in an open interval containing
x=0
,
\hat{f}(\alpha,x)
is a sort of incomplete Mellin transform (see Theorem
A.5
below). Moreover, for the sake of shortness, in the following statement we use the compact notation
\sigma_{ijk}
for the
k
th derivative at
s=0
of the
j
th component of
\sigma_{i}(s;{{\hat{\mu}}})
, i.e.,

\sigma_{ijk}({{\hat{\mu}}})\!:=\partial^{k}_{s}\sigma_{ij}(0;{{\hat{\mu}}}).

Also with regard to the statement, note that
D_{ij}
refers to the discrete sets introduced in Theorem
A.3
.

Theorem A.4
.

For each
(i,j)\in\{(0,0),(1,0),(0,1),(2,0)\}
, the following expression of
T_{ij}({{\hat{\mu}}})
is valid provided that
\lambda\notin D_{ij}
:

\displaystyle T_{00}({{\hat{\mu}}})\!=

\displaystyle-\sigma_{120}\hat{A}_{1}(-1,\sigma_{120}),

\displaystyle T_{01}({{\hat{\mu}}})\!=

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{120}\sigma_{111}^{\lambda}}{\sigma_{210}^{\lambda}L_{1}^{\lambda}(\sigma_{120})}\hat{A}_{2}(\lambda,\sigma_{210}),

\displaystyle T_{10}({{\hat{\mu}}})\!=

\displaystyle-\frac{\sigma_{121}}{P_{2}(0,\sigma_{120})}-\frac{\sigma_{120}\sigma_{111}}{L_{1}(\sigma_{120})}\hat{B}_{1}\big(1/\lambda-1,\sigma_{120}\big),

and

\displaystyle T_{20}({{\hat{\mu}}})\!=

\displaystyle-\frac{\sigma_{120}\sigma_{122}}{2\sigma_{120}P_{2}(0,\sigma_{120})}-\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{121}^{2}\partial_{2}P_{2}^{-1}(0,\sigma_{120})-\sigma_{121}\sigma_{111}\partial_{1}P_{2}^{-1}(0,\sigma_{120})

\displaystyle-\frac{\sigma_{120}\sigma_{111}^{2}}{2L_{1}^{2}(\sigma_{120})}\hat{C}_{1}(2/\lambda-1,\sigma_{120})-S_{1}\frac{\sigma_{120}\sigma_{111}}{L_{1}(\sigma_{120})}\hat{B}_{1}(1/\lambda-1,\sigma_{120}),

where

\displaystyle S_{1}\!=

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{112}}{2\sigma_{111}}-\frac{\sigma_{121}}{\sigma_{120}}\left(\frac{P_{1}}{P_{2}}\right)\!(0,\sigma_{120})-\frac{\sigma_{111}}{L_{1}(\sigma_{120})}\hat{M}_{1}(1/\lambda,\sigma_{120}).

As we already explained, the following result (that merges Theorem B.1 and Corollary B.3 in
[
31
]
) is the third ingredient needed in the proof of Propositions
3.2
and
3.3
.

Theorem A.5
.

Let us consider an open interval
I
of
\mathbb{R}
containing
x=0
and an open subset
U
of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
.

(a)

Given
f(x;\nu)\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(I\times U)
, there exits a unique
\hat{f}(\alpha,x;\nu)\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}((\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\times I\times U)
such that

x\partial_{x}\hat{f}({\alpha},x;\nu)-\alpha\hat{f}({\alpha},x;\nu)=f(x;\nu).

(b)

If
x\in I\setminus\{0\}
then
\partial_{x}(\hat{f}({\alpha},x;\nu)|x|^{-\alpha})=f(x;\nu)\frac{|x|^{-\alpha}}{x}
and, taking any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
with
k>\alpha
,

\hat{f}(\alpha,x;\nu)=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\frac{\partial_{x}^{i}f(0;\nu)}{i!(i-\alpha)}x^{i}+|x|^{\alpha}\int_{0}^{x}\!\left(f(s;\nu)-T_{0}^{k-1}f(s;\nu)\right)|s|^{-\alpha}\frac{ds}{s},

where
T_{0}^{k}f(x;\nu)=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\frac{1}{i!}\partial_{x}^{i}f(0;\nu)x^{i}
is the
k
-th degree Taylor polynomial of
f(x;\nu)
at
x=0
.

(c)

If
f(x;\nu)
is analytic on
I\times U
then
\hat{f}(\alpha,x;\nu)
is analytic on
(\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\times I\times U
. Finally,
for each
(\alpha_{0},x_{0},\nu_{0})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\times I\times U
the function

(\alpha,x,\nu)\mapsto(\alpha_{0}-\alpha)\hat{f}(\alpha,x;\nu)
extends analytically to
(\alpha_{0},x_{0},\nu_{0})
.

(d)

If
f(x;\nu)=x^{n}g(x;\nu)
with
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(I\times U)
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
then

\hat{f}(\alpha,x;\nu)=x^{n}\hat{g}(\alpha-n,x;\nu).

The following simple observation will be useful in order to study the coefficients of the asymptotic expansions that we shall deal with.

If
\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}x^{\lambda_{i}}+\psi(x)=0
for all
x\in(0,\varepsilon),
where
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
with
\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}<\cdots<\lambda_{m}
,
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{m}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\psi(x)=\mathrm{o}(x^{\lambda_{m}})
then
a_{1}=a_{2}=\cdots=a_{m}=0
.

\square

We are now in position to begin the proof of the two first results in Section
3
.

A.2
Proof of Proposition
3.2

We follow the approach in
[
22
, §5]
to take advantage of the general setting developed in
[
30
]
. To this end we will work on an extended parameter space
\bar{{\nu}}\in\bar{V}
that we specify as follows. Firstly, introducing an auxiliary parameter
\eta\approx 0,
we consider two local transverse sections
\Sigma_{1}^{\eta}
and
\Sigma_{2}^{\eta}
parametrized respectively by
s\mapsto(1-s,\eta)
and
s\mapsto(-\frac{1}{s},\frac{\eta}{s})
, for
s>0
, cf. Figure
2
.
Secondly, taking any
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
such the straight line
y=\alpha x+\beta
does not intersect any solution of
X_{{\nu}}
while traveling from
\Sigma_{1}^{\eta}
to
\Sigma_{2}^{\eta}.
One can readily see that a sufficient condition for this to hold is that

\alpha+\beta<\eta\text{ and }\eta>-\alpha.

Then, setting
\bar{{\nu}}\!:=(D,F,\alpha,\beta,\eta)
, we will work on the extended parameter space

\bar{V}\!:=\left\{\bar{{\nu}}\in\mathbb{R}^{5}:D\in(-1,0),F\in(0,1),\alpha+\beta<\eta,-\alpha<\eta\right\}.

Taking this into account, we consider the projective change of coordinates, see Figure
8
,

Figure 8:
Projective coordinate change in the proof of Proposition
3.2
.

(x_{1},x_{2})=\left(\frac{1-x}{y-\alpha x-\beta},\frac{1}{y-\alpha x-\beta}\right).

One can verify that in these coordinates the parametrizations of
\Sigma_{1}^{\eta}
and
\Sigma_{2}^{\eta}
become

\sigma_{1}(s)\!:=\left(\frac{s}{\eta+\alpha s-\alpha-\beta},\frac{1}{\eta+\alpha s-\alpha-\beta}\right)\text{ and }\sigma_{2}(s)\!:=\left(\frac{1+s}{\alpha+\eta-\beta s},\frac{s}{\alpha+\eta-\beta s}\right),

(26)

respectively, whereas the vector field
(\ref{sist_loud})
is brought to

\bar{X}_{\bar{{\nu}}}\!:=\frac{1}{x_{2}}\big(x_{1}P_{1}(x_{1},x_{2};\bar{{\nu}})\partial_{x_{1}}+x_{2}P_{2}(x_{1},x_{2};\bar{{\nu}})\partial_{x_{2}}\big)

(27)

with

\displaystyle P_{1}(x_{1},x_{2};\bar{{\nu}})

\displaystyle=(1-\alpha x_{1})^{2}+(\alpha+\beta)x_{2}-x_{2}^{2}+(1-\alpha^{2}-\alpha\beta)x_{1}x_{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-F(1+\alpha(x_{2}-x_{1})+\beta x_{2})^{2}-D(x_{1}-x_{2})^{2}

and

\displaystyle P_{2}(x_{1},x_{2};\bar{{\nu}})

\displaystyle=\alpha^{2}x_{1}^{2}-\alpha x_{1}-x_{2}^{2}+(1-\alpha^{2}-\alpha\beta)x_{1}x_{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-F(1+\alpha(x_{2}-x_{1})+\beta x_{2})^{2}-D(x_{1}-x_{2})^{2}.

The reason why we introduce the auxiliary parameters
\alpha,

\beta
and
\eta
is because the computations are much easier taking the projective change of coordinates that sends
y=0
to infinity (i.e., with
\alpha=\beta=0)
, which is not compatible with the placement of the original transverse sections (i.e., with
\eta=0).
Since the parameters
\bar{{\nu}}
with
\alpha=\beta=\eta=0
are in the boundary of the admissible set
\bar{V}
, we will work in the interior and then make a limit argument. By introducing these auxiliary parameters we end up in a setting where the assumptions to apply the results in Section
A.1
are fulfilled. Observe in particular that
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are analytic on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\bar{V}
. Following the notation introduced there, note that the hyperbolicity ratio of the saddle

\lambda=-\frac{P_{2}(0,0)}{P_{1}(0,0)}=\frac{F}{1-F}

depends on
\mu=\bar{{\nu}}
. We denote the Dulac time of
\bar{X}_{\bar{{\nu}}}
from
\Sigma_{1}^{\eta}
to
\Sigma_{2}^{\eta}
by
\bar{T}(s;\bar{{\nu}})
. Note that, by construction, it does not depend on
\alpha
and
\beta
as long as
\alpha+\beta<\eta
and
\eta>-\alpha
holds. Moreover, and this is the key point for our purposes, the Dulac time
T(s;{{\nu}})
in the statement is precisely
\bar{T}(s;\bar{{\nu}})
for
\eta=0.

Let us fix any
\bar{{\nu}}_{0}=(D_{0},F_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0},\eta_{0})\in\bar{V}
with
F_{0}\in[\frac{1}{2},1)
. Observe that
\lambda(\bar{\nu}_{0})>2
if
F_{0}\in(\frac{2}{3},1),

\lambda(\bar{\nu}_{0})\in(1,2)
if
F_{0}\in(\frac{1}{2},\frac{2}{3})
,
\lambda(\bar{\nu}_{0})=2
if
F_{0}=\frac{2}{3}
and
\lambda(\bar{\nu}_{0})=1
if
F_{0}=\frac{1}{2}
. Consequently, by applying (2), (1), (5) and (4) in Theorem
A.3
, respectively, we get that

(a^{\prime})

\bar{T}(s;\bar{{\nu}})=\bar{T}_{00}(\bar{{\nu}})+\bar{T}_{10}(\bar{{\nu}})s+\bar{T}_{20}(\bar{{\nu}})s^{2}+\mathcal{F}_{L_{0}-\upsilon}^{\infty}(\bar{{\nu}}_{0})
if
F_{0}\in(\frac{2}{3},1),
where
L_{0}=\min(3,\lambda(\bar{{\nu}}_{0})),

(b^{\prime})

\bar{T}(s;\bar{{\nu}})=\bar{T}_{00}(\bar{{\nu}})+\bar{T}_{10}(\bar{{\nu}})s+\bar{T}_{01}(\bar{{\nu}})s^{\lambda}+\mathcal{F}^{\infty}_{2-\upsilon}(\bar{{\nu}}_{0})
if
F_{0}\in(\frac{1}{2},\frac{2}{3})
,

(c^{\prime})

\bar{T}(s;\bar{{\nu}})=\bar{T}_{00}(\bar{{\nu}})+\bar{T}_{10}(\bar{{\nu}})s+\bar{T}_{201}^{2}(\bar{{\nu}})s^{2}\omega_{2-\lambda}(s)+\bar{T}_{200}^{2}(\bar{{\nu}})s^{2}+\mathcal{F}_{3-\upsilon}^{\infty}(\bar{{\nu}}_{0})
if
F_{0}=\frac{3}{2}
, where

\bar{T}_{201}^{2}(\bar{{\nu}})
and
\bar{T}_{200}^{2}(\bar{{\nu}})
are smooth in a neighbourhood of
\{\bar{{\nu}}\in\bar{V}:\lambda(\bar{{\nu}})=2\}
and, moreover,

\bar{T}_{201}^{2}(\bar{{\nu}})=(2-\lambda)\bar{T}_{01}(\bar{{\nu}})\text{ and }\bar{T}_{200}^{2}(\bar{{\nu}})=\bar{T}_{20}(\bar{{\nu}})+\bar{T}_{01}(\bar{{\nu}})\text{ for $\lambda(\bar{{\nu}})\neq 2,$}

(d^{\prime})

\bar{T}(s;\bar{{\nu}})=\bar{T}_{00}(\bar{{\nu}})+\bar{T}_{101}^{1}(\bar{{\nu}})s\omega_{1-\lambda}(s)+\bar{T}_{100}^{1}(\bar{{\nu}})s+\mathcal{F}_{2-\upsilon}^{\infty}(\bar{{\nu}}_{0})
if
F_{0}=\frac{1}{2}
, where

\bar{T}_{101}^{1}(\bar{{\nu}})
and
\bar{T}_{100}^{1}(\bar{{\nu}})
are smooth in a neighbourhood of
\{\bar{\nu}\in\bar{V}:\lambda(\bar{\nu})=1\}
and, moreover,

\bar{T}_{101}^{1}(\bar{{\nu}})=(1-\lambda)\bar{T}_{01}(\bar{{\nu}})\text{ and }\bar{T}_{100}^{1}(\bar{{\nu}})=\bar{T}_{10}(\bar{{\nu}})+\bar{T}_{01}(\bar{{\nu}})\text{ for $\lambda(\bar{{\nu}})\neq 1.$}

Here
\upsilon
is a small enough positive number depending on
\bar{{\nu}}_{0}.
Furthermore the coefficients
\bar{T}_{ij}(\bar{{\nu}})
are meromorphic functions on
\bar{V}
with poles only at those
F\in(0,1)
such that
\lambda(\bar{{\nu}})=\frac{F}{1-F}\in D_{ij},
where
D_{00}=\emptyset
,
D_{10}=\frac{1}{\mathbb{N}}
,
D_{01}=\mathbb{N}
and
D_{20}=\frac{2}{\mathbb{N}}
.

We claim that the coefficients
\bar{T}_{ij}(\bar{{\nu}})
do not depend on
\alpha
and
\beta
. Indeed, to see this recall that the Dulac time
\bar{T}(s;\bar{{\nu}})
does not depend on
\alpha
and
\beta
provided that
\alpha+\beta<\eta
and
\eta>-\alpha
, which is verified for
\bar{{\nu}}\in\bar{V}.
Hence
\partial_{\alpha}\bar{T}(s;\bar{{\nu}})\equiv 0
and
\partial_{\beta}\bar{T}(s;\bar{{\nu}})\equiv 0.
Thus from
(b^{\prime})
we get that, for each fixed
\bar{{\nu}}_{\star}\in\bar{V}\cap\{\frac{1}{2}<F<\frac{2}{3}\}
,

\partial_{\alpha}\bar{T}_{00}(\bar{{\nu}}_{\star})+\partial_{\alpha}\bar{T}_{10}(\bar{{\nu}}_{\star})s+\partial_{\alpha}\bar{T}_{01}(\bar{{\nu}}_{\star})s^{\lambda(\bar{{\nu}}_{\star})}+\mbox{\rm o}(s^{2-2\upsilon})=0\text{ for all $s>0$,}

where we use that the flatness order of the remainder is preserved when derived with respect to parameters (see Definition
A.1
). Then, since
1<\lambda<2
for
F\in(\frac{1}{2},\frac{2}{3})
and we can choose
\upsilon>0
arbitrary small (depending on
\bar{{\nu}}_{\star})
, by taking Remark
A.1
into account we can assert that

\partial_{\alpha}\bar{T}_{00}(\bar{{\nu}}_{\star})=0\text{, }\partial_{\alpha}\bar{T}_{10}(\bar{{\nu}}_{\star})=0\text{ and }\partial_{\alpha}\bar{T}_{01}(\bar{{\nu}}_{\star})=0.

Since
\bar{V}\cap\{\frac{1}{2}<F<\frac{2}{3}\}
is open and the coefficients are meromorphic on
\bar{V}
, by Lemma
B.1
it follows that
\partial_{\alpha}\bar{T}_{00}
,
\partial_{\alpha}\bar{T}_{10}
and
\partial_{\alpha}\bar{T}_{01}
are identically zero. The claim for
\partial_{\beta}
and the other coefficients follows verbatim.

On account of the claim and the fact that
\bar{T}(s;\bar{{\nu}})|_{\eta=0}=T(s;{{\nu}})
, the assertions in
(a)
–
(d)
with regard to the asymptotic expansion of
T(s;{{\nu}})
follow from
(a^{\prime})
–
(d^{\prime})
, respectively, by setting

T_{ij}({{\nu}})\!:=\left.\bar{T}_{ij}(\bar{{\nu}})\right|_{\eta=0}.

Next we proceed with the computation of the expression of each coefficient. To this end observe that

T_{ij}({{\nu}})=\left.\bar{T}_{ij}(D,F,\alpha,\beta,\eta)\right|_{\eta=0}=\lim_{\eta\to 0^{+}}\bar{T}_{ij}(D,F,\alpha,\beta,\eta)=\lim\limits_{\eta\to 0^{+}}\bar{T}_{ij}(D,F,0,0,\eta),

(28)

where the second follows by the continuity of
\bar{{\nu}}\mapsto\bar{T}_{ij}(\bar{{\nu}})
on
\bar{V}
and the last one on account of the previous claim. In view of this the plan is to compute
\bar{T}_{ij}(D,F,0,0,\eta)
with
\eta>0
by applying Theorem
A.4
and then make
\eta\to 0.
With this aim it is first necessary to obtain the functions in
(\ref{def_fun})
. In doing so, and setting

\delta_{1}\!=\frac{1}{2F},

\kappa_{1}\!=\frac{D+1}{F},

\delta_{2}\!=\frac{1}{2(1-F)}
and
\kappa_{2}\!=\frac{D}{F-1},

for shortness, one can verify that

L_{i}(u)=h(u;\delta_{i},\kappa_{i})\text{ for $i=1,2$, where $h(u;\delta,\kappa)\!:=(1+\kappa u^{2})^{\delta}$.}

We stress that
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are defined in
(\ref{def_fun})
in terms of the functions
P_{1}(x_{1},x_{2};\bar{{\nu}})
and
P_{1}(x_{1},x_{2};\bar{{\nu}})
given in
(\ref{t1eq0})
and that, as we already explained, we take
\alpha=\beta=0
here and in what follows. Similarly, setting

\gamma_{1}=-\frac{2D+1}{F^{2}}

for the sake of shortness again, some computations show that
M_{1}(u)=\gamma_{1}uh(u;\delta_{1}-2,\kappa_{1}),

A_{1}(u)=-2\delta_{1}h(u;-1,\kappa_{1}),\;B_{1}(u)=M_{1}(u)\text{ and }\left.C_{1}(u)\right|_{D=-\frac{1}{2}}=-4\delta_{1}^{2}h(u;2\delta_{1}-2,\delta_{1}).

(29)

Moreover
A_{2}(u)=2\delta_{2}h(u;\delta_{2}-1,\kappa_{2}).
With regard to the parametrization of the transverse sections in the expression of the coefficients, see
(\ref{t1eq3})
, using the compact notation
\sigma_{ijk}(\bar{{\nu}})\!:=\partial^{k}_{s}\sigma_{ij}(0;\bar{{\nu}})
we get that

\sigma_{112}=\sigma_{121}=\sigma_{122}=0\text{ and }\sigma_{111}=\sigma_{120}=\sigma_{210}=\sigma_{221}=1/\eta.

(30)

We are now in position to apply Theorem
A.4
to obtain the coefficients
\bar{T}_{ij}(\bar{{\nu}})
. (We omit the computation leading to
T_{00}({{\nu}})
because it is given in
[
22
, Proposition 5.2]
.) In doing so we obtain that

\bar{T}_{01}({{\nu}},0,0,\eta)=\frac{\sigma_{120}\sigma_{111}^{\lambda}}{\sigma_{210}^{\lambda}L_{1}^{\lambda}(\sigma_{120})}\hat{A}_{2}(\lambda,\sigma_{210})=2\delta_{2}\frac{\eta^{\lambda}}{(\eta^{2}+\kappa_{1})^{\delta_{2}}}\hat{h}(\lambda,1/\eta;\delta_{2}-1,\kappa_{2}).

Therefore

\displaystyle T_{01}({{\nu}})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\eta\to 0^{+}}\bar{T}_{01}({{\nu}},0,0,\eta)=\frac{2\delta_{2}}{\kappa_{1}^{\delta_{2}}}\frac{\kappa_{2}^{\frac{\lambda}{2}}}{2}\mathrm{B}\left(-\frac{\lambda}{2},-\delta_{2}+1+\frac{\lambda}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta_{2}}{\sqrt{\kappa_{2}}}\left(\frac{\kappa_{2}}{\kappa_{1}}\right)^{\delta_{2}}\mathrm{B}\left(-\frac{\lambda}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2(1-F)}\left(\frac{F}{D+1}\right)^{\frac{\lambda+1}{2}}\left(\frac{D}{F-1}\right)^{\frac{\lambda}{2}}\frac{\Gamma(-\frac{\lambda}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{1-\lambda}{2})},

where the first equality follows from
(\ref{t1eq1})
, the second one by
(a)
in Proposition
B.3
(provided that
\lambda\notin\mathbb{N}
), and the last one by using
(\ref{eqB0})
and that
\Gamma(\frac{1}{2})=\sqrt{\pi}.

Since
\rho_{1}({{\nu}})=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2(1-F)}\big(\frac{F}{D+1}\big)^{\frac{\lambda+1}{2}}\big(\frac{D}{F-1}\big)^{\frac{\lambda}{2}}
is an analytic positive function on
V
, this proves the equality in the statement because we know that
T_{01}({{\nu}})
is meromorphic on
V
with poles only at those
F\in(0,1)
such that
\lambda({{\nu}})\in\mathbb{N}.

Let us turn next to the computation of
T_{10}
. With this aim we note that

\displaystyle\bar{T}_{10}({{\nu}},0,0,\eta)

\displaystyle=-\sigma_{120}\left(\frac{\sigma_{121}}{\sigma_{120}P_{2}(0,\sigma_{120})}+\frac{\sigma_{111}}{L_{1}(\sigma_{120})}\hat{B}_{1}\big(1/\lambda-1,\sigma_{120}\big)\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\eta^{-2}}{L_{1}(\eta^{-1})}\hat{B}_{1}(1/\lambda-1,\eta^{-1})=-\gamma_{1}\frac{\eta^{-3+2\delta_{1}}}{(\eta^{2}+\kappa_{1})^{\delta_{1}}}\hat{h}(1/\lambda-2,\eta^{-1};\delta_{1}-2,\kappa_{1}).

Here the first equality follows by Theorem
A.4
, the second one from
(\ref{t1eq2})
and the last one by applying
(d)
in Theorem
A.5
to the function
B_{1}(u)=\gamma_{1}uh(u;\delta_{1}-2,\kappa_{1})
, see
(\ref{t1eq4})
. Since
-3+2\delta_{1}=\frac{1}{\lambda}-2
, by applying
(a)
in Proposition
B.3
we get

T_{10}({{\nu}})=\lim_{\eta\to 0^{+}}T_{10}({{\nu}},0,0,\eta)=-\frac{\gamma_{1}\kappa_{1}^{\frac{1}{2\lambda}-1}}{2\kappa_{1}^{\delta_{1}}}\mathrm{B}\left(1-\frac{1}{2\lambda},\frac{1}{2}\right)=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}(2D+1)}{2\sqrt{F(1+D)^{3}}}\frac{\Gamma(1-\frac{1}{2\lambda})}{\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-\frac{1}{2\lambda})}

and, due to
\rho_{2}({{\nu}})=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2\sqrt{F(1+D)^{3}}}
, this proves the validity of the expression for
T_{10}
given in the statement. Let us finally compute the coefficient
T_{20}.
In this case, on account of
(\ref{t1eq2})
, by Theorem
A.4
we get

\bar{T}_{20}({{\nu}},0,0,\eta)=-\eta^{-1}\left(\frac{\eta^{-2}}{2L_{1}^{2}(\eta^{-1})}\hat{C}_{1}(2/\lambda-1,\eta^{-1})+\frac{\eta^{-1}S_{1}}{L_{1}(\eta^{-1})}\hat{B}_{1}(1/\lambda-1,\eta^{-1})\right).

Thus, since
\gamma_{1}=0
for
D=-\frac{1}{2},
from
(\ref{t1eq4})
it turns out that

\bar{T}_{20}(-1/2,F,0,0,\eta)=\frac{2\delta_{1}^{2}\eta^{-3+4\delta_{1}}}{(\eta^{2}+\kappa_{1})^{2\delta_{1}}}\hat{h}(2/\lambda-1,\eta^{-1};2\delta_{1}-2,\delta_{1})\Big|_{D=-\frac{1}{2}}

Hence, due to
-3+4\delta_{1}=\frac{2}{\lambda}-1
, by applying
(a)
in Proposition
B.3
once again,

T_{20}(-1/2,F)=\lim_{\eta\to 0^{+}}\bar{T}_{20}(-1/2,F,0,0,\eta)=\frac{\kappa_{1}^{\frac{1}{\lambda}-\frac{1}{2}}}{4F^{2}\kappa_{1}^{2\delta_{1}}}\Big|_{D=-\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{B}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\lambda},\frac{1}{2}\right)=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{\sqrt{2F}}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\lambda})}{\Gamma(1-\frac{1}{\lambda})}.

Since
T_{20}({{\nu}})
is a meromorphic function having poles only at those
{{\nu}}_{0}\in V
such that
\lambda({{\nu}}_{0})\in D_{20}=\frac{2}{\mathbb{N}}
and, on the other hand,
\lambda({{\nu}})>2
for all
{{\nu}}\in V\cap\{\frac{2}{3}<F<1\}
, by applying the Weierstrass Division Theorem (see for instance
[
11
, Theorem 1.8]
), we can assert the existence of an analytic function
\rho_{3}
on
V\cap\{\frac{2}{3}<F<1\}
such that

T_{20}({{\nu}})=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{\sqrt{2F}}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\lambda})}{\Gamma(1-\frac{1}{\lambda})}+\rho_{3}({{\nu}})(2D+1).

It only remains to prove the assertions with regard to the properties of the coefficients in the respective asymptotic expansions. Being the ones in
(a)
and
(b)
an easy consequence of well-known properties of the gamma function (see for instance
[
1
]
), we proceed with the other two:

(c)

Let us take any
{{\nu}}_{0}\in V\cap\{F=\frac{2}{3}\}
and note that, from
(c^{\prime})
, the functions
T_{201}^{2}({{\nu}})\!:=\left.\bar{T}_{201}^{2}(\bar{{\nu}})\right|_{\eta=0}
and
T_{200}^{2}({{\nu}})\!:=\left.\bar{T}_{200}^{2}(\bar{{\nu}})\right|_{\eta=0}
are smooth in a neighbourhood of
\{{{\nu}}\in V:\lambda({{\nu}})=2\}=V\cap\{F=\frac{2}{3}\}
and

T_{201}^{2}({{\nu}})=\big(2-\lambda({{\nu}})\big)T_{01}({{\nu}})\text{ and }T_{200}^{2}({{\nu}})=T_{20}({{\nu}})+T_{01}({{\nu}})\text{ for $\lambda({{\nu}})\neq 2.$}

Recall that
T_{01}({{\nu}})
and
T_{20}({{\nu}})
are meromorphic with a pole
at those
{{\nu}}
such that
\lambda({{\nu}})=2\in D_{01}\cap D_{20}
.
What is more, by Propositions 3.2 and 3.6 in
[
31
]
, respectively, we know that in both cases the pole is simple. Consequently by the Weierstrass Division Theorem (or, more directly, by
[
31
, Lemma 2.8]
) it follows that
T_{201}^{2}({{\nu}})
and
T_{200}^{2}({{\nu}})
are analytic in a neighbourhood of
V\cap\{F=\frac{2}{3}\}
. On the other hand, from the already proved part of the statement, if
{{\nu}}_{\star}=(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{2}{3})
then
T_{10}({{\nu}}_{\star})=0
and

T_{201}^{2}({{\nu}}_{\star})=\lim_{{{\nu}}\to{{\nu}}_{\star}}(2-\lambda)T_{01}({{\nu}})=\frac{\rho_{1}({{\nu}}_{\star})}{\Gamma(-\frac{1}{2})}\lim_{{{\nu}}\to{{\nu}}_{\star}}\Gamma(-\lambda/2)(2-\lambda)=-2\frac{\rho_{1}({{\nu}}_{\star})}{\Gamma(-\frac{1}{2})}\neq 0

because
\lim_{x\to-1}(x+1)\Gamma(x)=-1
(see
[
1
, §6]
for instance).

(d)

Consider finally any
{{\nu}}_{0}\in V\cap\{F=\frac{1}{2}\}
. Then, from assertion

(d^{\prime})
,
T_{101}^{1}({{\nu}})\!:=\left.\bar{T}_{101}^{1}(\bar{{\nu}})\right|_{\eta=0}
and
T_{100}^{1}({{\nu}})\!:=\left.\bar{T}_{100}^{1}(\bar{{\nu}})\right|_{\eta=0}
are smooth functions in a neighbourhood of
\{{{\nu}}\in V:\lambda({{\nu}})=1\}=V\cap\{F=\frac{1}{2}\}
and, in addition,

T_{101}^{1}({{\nu}})=\big(1-\lambda({{\nu}})\big)T_{01}({{\nu}})\text{ and }T_{100}^{1}({{\nu}})=T_{10}({{\nu}})+T_{01}({{\nu}})\text{ for $\lambda({{\nu}})\neq 1.$}

Since
T_{10}({{\nu}})
and
T_{01}({{\nu}})
are meromorphic with a pole at those
{{\nu}}
such that
\lambda({{\nu}})=1\in D_{10}\cap D_{01},
the above equality shows exactly as before that
T_{101}^{1}({{\nu}})
and
T_{100}^{1}({{\nu}})
are analytic in a neighbourhood of
V\cap\{F=\frac{1}{2}\}
. Moreover, from the expression for
T_{01}
in the statement that we already proved and using that
1-\lambda=2\frac{F-1/2}{F-1}
, we can write

T_{101}^{1}({{\nu}})=(1-\lambda)T_{01}({{\nu}})=-\rho_{4}({{\nu}})(F-1/2)^{2}\text{ with $\rho_{4}({{\nu}})\!:=\frac{\rho_{1}({{\nu}})}{(F-1)^{2}}\frac{4\Gamma(-\frac{\lambda}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{1-\lambda}{2})(\lambda-1)}.$}

The function
\rho_{4}
is analytic at
\nu_{0}=(D_{0},\frac{1}{2})
because
\lambda({{\nu}}_{0})=1
and

\Gamma(z)
has simple pole at
z=0
. In addition, since
\lim_{z\to 0}z\Gamma(z)=1
and
\Gamma(-\frac{1}{2})=-2\sqrt{\pi}
, we get that
\rho_{4}({{\nu}}_{0})=16\sqrt{\pi}\rho_{1}(\nu_{0})>0.
From the expressions in the statement as well, we get

T_{100}^{1}({{\nu}})=\rho_{5}({{\nu}})(D+1/2)+\rho_{6}({{\nu}})(F-1/2)

with
\rho_{5}({{\nu}})\!:=2\rho_{2}({{\nu}})\frac{\Gamma(1-\frac{1}{2\lambda})}{\Gamma(\frac{3}{2}-\frac{1}{2\lambda})}
and
\rho_{6}({{\nu}})\!:=\frac{1-F}{2}\rho_{4}({{\nu}})
, that are analytic and positive at
\nu_{0}=(D_{0},\frac{1}{2})
due to
\lambda({{\nu}}_{0})=1
. Finally a computation shows that
\rho_{5}(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})=\rho_{6}(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}).

This concludes the proof of the result.

A.3
Proof of Proposition
3.3

We will adapt the arguments in
[
23
, §3.2.1]
to take advantage of the general setting developed in
[
31
]
. To this end, as we did in the proof of the previous result, we will work in an extended parameter space
\bar{W}
to be specified. In this case the computations are a little bit more involved because we also need to straighten the separatrices of the saddle, see Figure
3
. With this aim in view we first take
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}
and consider the local change of coordinates given by

(x_{1},x_{2})=\phi_{\varepsilon}(x,y)\!:=\left(\frac{q(x)-\frac{1}{2}y^{2}}{a(p_{2}-x+\varepsilon y)^{2}},\frac{p_{2}-p_{1}}{p_{2}-x+\varepsilon y}\right),

where recall that
q(x)=a(x-p_{1})(x-p_{2})
with
a=\frac{D}{2(1-F)}>0
and
p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathbb{R},

p_{1}<p_{2}
, for all
{{\nu}}\in W.
In what follows, for the sake of shortness we set

\kappa_{1}\!:=p_{2}-p_{1}\text{ and }\kappa_{2}\!:=1/\sqrt{2a}.

One can check that the Jacobian determinant of
\phi_{\varepsilon}
vanishes at
(x,y)
if and only if
y-\varepsilon q^{\prime}(x)=0,
where

q^{\prime}(x)=2ax-a(p_{1}+p_{2})x,

and that this straight line is mapped by
\phi_{\varepsilon}
to

\mathscr{D}_{\varepsilon}(x_{1},x_{2})\!:=2a(1-x_{1}-x_{2})+a^{2}\varepsilon^{2}(4x_{1}+x_{2}^{2})=0.

We claim that
\phi_{\varepsilon}
is an analytic map from

\Omega_{\varepsilon}\!:=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:p_{2}-x+\varepsilon y>0,\,y-\varepsilon q^{\prime}(x)>0\}

to
\mathscr{U}_{\varepsilon}\!:=\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:x_{2}>0,\,\mathscr{D}_{\varepsilon}(x_{1},x_{2})>0\}
with a well defined analytic inverse given by

\psi_{\varepsilon}(x_{1},x_{2})\!:=\left(\frac{\kappa_{1}(\varepsilon\mathscr{D}_{\varepsilon}(x_{1},x_{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}-1)+(p_{2}-\varepsilon^{2}a(p_{1}+p_{2}))x_{2}}{(1-2a\varepsilon^{2})x_{2}},\frac{\kappa_{1}(\mathscr{D}_{\varepsilon}(x_{1},x_{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}+a\varepsilon(x_{2}-2))}{(1-2a\varepsilon^{2})x_{2}}\right).

Indeed, the claim follows by checking that
\phi_{\varepsilon}\circ\psi_{\varepsilon}=\text{Id}
on
\{\mathscr{D}_{\varepsilon}(x_{1},x_{2})>0,x_{2}\neq 0\}
and that
\psi_{\varepsilon}\circ\phi_{\varepsilon}=\text{Id}
on
\{\frac{y-\varepsilon q^{\prime}(x)}{p_{2}-x+\varepsilon y}>0\}.
To show the second identity we use that
(\mathscr{D}_{\varepsilon}\circ\phi_{\varepsilon})(x,y)=\left(\frac{y-\varepsilon q^{\prime}(x)}{p_{2}-x+\varepsilon y}\right)^{2}.
Consequently
(x_{1},x_{2})=\phi_{\varepsilon}(x,y)
is an analytic global change of variables from
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
to
\mathscr{U}_{\varepsilon}
for all
\varepsilon.
In what follows we require
|\varepsilon|<\frac{1}{\sqrt{2a}}
in order that the straight line

\{\varepsilon y+p_{2}-x=0\}
does not intersect the left branch of the hyperbola
\mathcal{C}=\{\frac{1}{2}y^{2}-q(x)=0\}
, see Figure
9
.

Figure 9:
Auxiliary transverse sections in the proof of Proposition
3.3
.

Next we introduce a second auxiliary parameter
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
and consider two additional transverse sections
\Sigma_{1}^{\eta}
and
\Sigma_{2}^{\eta}
laying on the straight line
\ell_{\eta}\!:=\{y=\eta(p_{2}-x)\},
see Figure
9
.
Observe that
\ell_{\eta}
intersects the right branch of the hyperbola
\mathcal{C}
at
(p_{2},0)
for all
\eta
. We require
|\eta|<\sqrt{2a}
additionally in order that
\ell_{\eta}
intersects the hyperbola at a point
(x_{\eta},y_{\eta})
in the left branch. Then we parametrize
\Sigma_{1}^{\eta}
and
\Sigma_{2}^{\eta}
, respectively, by

s\mapsto\big(x_{\eta}-s,\eta(p_{2}-x_{\eta}+s)\big)\text{ and }s\mapsto\big(-1/s,\eta(p_{2}+1/s)\big),

(31)

for
s>0
small enough. We also require
\varepsilon(\eta^{2}+2a)+2\eta>0
so that
(x_{\eta},y_{\eta})\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}.
Summing up, the admissible conditions

|\varepsilon|<\frac{1}{\sqrt{2a}},\;|\eta|<\sqrt{2a}\text{ and }\varepsilon(\eta^{2}+2a)+2\eta>0

guarantee that any solution of
X_{{\nu}}
going from
\Sigma_{1}^{\eta}
to
\Sigma_{2}^{\eta}
is inside the domain
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
of the coordinate change
(x_{1},x_{2})=\phi_{\varepsilon}(x,y)
. Thus, setting
\bar{{\nu}}\!:=({{\nu}},\varepsilon,\eta)
, we will work on the extended parameter space

\textstyle\bar{W}\!:=\big\{\bar{{\nu}}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:F+D>0,D<0,F>1,|\varepsilon|<\frac{1}{\sqrt{2a}},\;|\eta|<\sqrt{2a}\text{ and }\varepsilon(\eta^{2}+2a)+2\eta>0\big\}.

Clearly the sets
\{({{\nu}},\varepsilon,0):{{\nu}}\in W\text{ and }\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2a}})\}
and
\{({{\nu}},0,\eta):{{\nu}}\in W\text{ and }\eta\in(0,\sqrt{2a})\}
are inside
\bar{W}
, that will be crucial in the forthcoming steps.

At this point we define
\sigma_{1}(\,\cdot\,;\bar{{\nu}})
and
\sigma_{2}(\,\cdot\,;\bar{{\nu}})
to be, respectively, the composition with
\phi_{\varepsilon}
of the parametrization of
\Sigma_{1}^{\eta}
and
\Sigma_{2}^{\eta}
given in
(\ref{p2eq1})
. In its regard one can check that

\displaystyle\left.\sigma_{1}(s;\bar{{\nu}})\right|_{\varepsilon=0}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{s(1-\kappa_{2}^{2}\eta^{2})^{2}}{\kappa_{1}+s(1-\kappa_{2}^{2}\eta^{2})},\frac{\kappa_{1}(1-\kappa_{2}^{2}\eta^{2})}{\kappa_{1}+s(1-\kappa_{2}^{2}\eta^{2})}\right)

(32)

and

\displaystyle\left.\sigma_{2}(s;\bar{{\nu}})\right|_{\varepsilon=0}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1+p_{1}s}{1+p_{2}s}-\kappa_{2}^{2}\eta^{2},\frac{\kappa_{1}s}{1+p_{2}s}\right).

(33)

One can also verify that the coordinate change
(x_{1},x_{2})=\phi_{\varepsilon}(x,y)
brings the vector field
X_{{\nu}}
in
(\ref{sist_loud})
to

\bar{X}_{\bar{{\nu}}}(x_{1},x_{2})=\frac{1}{x_{2}}\big(x_{1}P_{1}(x_{1},x_{2};\bar{{\nu}})\partial_{x_{1}}+x_{2}P_{2}(x_{1},x_{2};\bar{{\nu}})\partial_{x_{2}}\big),

where
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
analytic functions on
\{(x_{1},x_{2},\bar{{\nu}})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\bar{W}:\mathscr{D}_{\varepsilon}(x_{1},x_{2})>0\}.
The hyperbolicity ratio of the saddle at the origin is

\lambda=-\frac{P_{2}(0,0)}{P_{1}(0,0)}=\frac{1}{2(F-1)}.

Moreover
P_{1}|_{\varepsilon=0}=R\bar{P}_{1}
and
P_{2}|_{\varepsilon=0}=R\bar{P}_{2}
where
R(x_{1},x_{2})=\frac{1}{\kappa_{2}}\sqrt{1-x_{1}-x_{2}}
,

\bar{P}_{1}(x_{1},x_{2})=2\kappa_{1}(F-1)+2(p_{2}-1)x_{2}\text{ and }\bar{P}_{2}(x_{1},x_{2})=-\kappa_{1}+(p_{2}-1)x_{2}.

(34)

(It will be clear in a moment the reason why it suffices to restrict to
\varepsilon=0.
) For each
\bar{{\nu}}\in\bar{W},
we define
\bar{T}(s;\bar{{\nu}})
to be the Dulac time of
\bar{X}_{\bar{{\nu}}}
between the transverse sections
\phi_{\varepsilon}(\Sigma^{\eta}_{1})
and
\phi_{\varepsilon}(\Sigma^{\eta}_{2})
parametrized by
\sigma_{1}(\,\cdot\,;\bar{{\nu}})
and
\sigma_{2}(\,\cdot\,;\bar{{\nu}})
, respectively. We point out that, by construction,
\bar{T}(s;\bar{{\nu}})
does not depend on
\varepsilon
and that, furthermore,
\left.\bar{T}(s;\bar{{\nu}})\right|_{\eta=0}=T(s;{{\nu}}).

Next we will apply Theorem
A.3
to obtain the asymptotic expansion of
\bar{T}(s;\bar{{\nu}})
at
s=0
. Note to this end that, by construction, given any
\bar{{\nu}}_{0}\in\bar{W}
there exists a relatively compact neighbourhood
\mathcal{V}_{0}
of

\big\{(x_{1},0):x_{1}\in[0,\bar{\sigma}_{21}(0;\bar{{\nu}}_{0})]\big\}\cup\big\{(0,x_{2}):x_{2}\in[0,\bar{\sigma}_{12}(0;\bar{{\nu}}_{0})]\big\}

in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and a neighbourhood
\bar{W}_{0}
of
\bar{{\nu}}_{0}
in
\bar{W}
such that
\phi_{\varepsilon}\big(\Sigma_{1}^{\eta}\cup\Sigma_{2}^{\eta}\big)\subset\mathcal{V}_{0}
for all
\bar{{\nu}}\in\bar{W}_{0}
and

\mathcal{V}_{0}\times\bar{W}_{0}\subset\{(x_{1},x_{2},\bar{{\nu}})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\bar{W}:\mathscr{D}_{\varepsilon}(x_{1},x_{2})>0\}.

Here we use (see also Figure
9
) that
\phi_{\varepsilon}
maps the straight line
\{y-\varepsilon q^{\prime}(x)=0\}
to
\{\mathscr{D}_{\varepsilon}(x_{1},x_{2})=0\}
. The above inclusion guarantees that
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are analytic on
\mathcal{V}_{0}\times\bar{W}_{0},
so that we can apply Theorem
A.3
to study the Dulac time of
\bar{X}_{\bar{{\nu}}}
for
\bar{{\nu}}\approx\bar{{\nu}}_{0}
. Accordingly, with this aim, let us fix any
\bar{{\nu}}_{0}=(D_{0},F_{0},\varepsilon_{0},\eta_{0})\in\bar{W}
with
F_{0}\in(1,\frac{3}{2})\cup\{2\}.
Observe that

\lambda(\bar{{\nu}}_{0})>2
if
F_{0}\in(1,\frac{5}{4})
,
\lambda(\bar{{\nu}}_{0})\in(1,2)
if
F_{0}\in(\frac{5}{4},\frac{3}{2})
,
\lambda(\bar{{\nu}}_{0})=2
if
F_{0}=\frac{5}{4}
and
\lambda(\bar{{\nu}}_{0})=\frac{1}{2}
if
F_{0}=2.
Then, by applying
(2),

(1)
,
(5)
and
(3)
in Theorem
A.3
, respectively, we can assert that

(a^{\prime})

\bar{T}(s;\bar{{\nu}})=\bar{T}_{00}(\bar{{\nu}})+\bar{T}_{10}(\bar{{\nu}})s+\bar{T}_{20}(\bar{{\nu}})s^{2}+\mathcal{F}_{L_{0}-\upsilon}^{\infty}(\bar{{\nu}}_{0})
if
F_{0}\in(1,\frac{5}{4})
, where
L_{0}=\min(3,\lambda(\bar{{\nu}}_{0})),

(b^{\prime})

\bar{T}(s;\bar{{\nu}})=\bar{T}_{00}(\bar{{\nu}})+\bar{T}_{10}(\bar{{\nu}})s+\bar{T}_{01}(\bar{{\nu}})s^{\lambda}+\bar{T}_{20}(\bar{{\nu}})s^{2}+\mathcal{F}^{\infty}_{L_{0}-\upsilon}(\bar{{\nu}}_{0})
if
F_{0}\in(\frac{5}{4},\frac{3}{2})
, where
L_{0}=\lambda(\bar{{\nu}}_{0})+1,

(c^{\prime})

\bar{T}(s;\bar{{\nu}})=\bar{T}_{00}(\bar{{\nu}})+\bar{T}_{10}(\bar{{\nu}})s+\bar{T}_{201}^{2}(\bar{{\nu}})s^{2}\omega_{2-\lambda}(s)+\bar{T}_{200}^{2}(\bar{{\nu}})s^{2}+\mathcal{F}_{3-\upsilon}^{\infty}(\bar{{\nu}}_{0})
if
F_{0}=\frac{5}{4}
, where

\bar{T}_{201}^{2}(\bar{{\nu}})
and
\bar{T}_{200}^{2}(\bar{{\nu}})
are smooth in a neighbourhood of

\{\bar{{\nu}}\in\bar{W}:\lambda(\bar{{\nu}})=2\}
and, moreover,

\bar{T}_{201}^{2}(\bar{{\nu}})=(2-\lambda)\bar{T}_{01}(\bar{{\nu}})\text{ and }\bar{T}_{200}^{2}(\bar{{\nu}})=\bar{T}_{20}(\bar{{\nu}})+\bar{T}_{01}(\bar{{\nu}})\text{ for $\lambda(\bar{{\nu}})\neq 2,$}

(d^{\prime})

\bar{T}(s;\bar{{\nu}})=\bar{T}_{00}(\bar{{\nu}})+\bar{T}_{01}(\bar{{\nu}})s^{\lambda}+\bar{T}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{101}(\bar{{\nu}})s\omega_{1-2\lambda}(s)+\bar{T}_{100}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\bar{{\nu}})s+\mathcal{F}_{3/2-\upsilon}^{\infty}(\bar{{\nu}}_{0})
if
F_{0}=2
,
where
\bar{T}_{101}^{1}(\bar{{\nu}})
and
\bar{T}_{100}^{1}(\bar{{\nu}})
are smooth in a neighbourhood of
\{\bar{{\nu}}\in\bar{W}:\lambda(\bar{{\nu}})=1/2\}

and, moreover,

\bar{T}_{101}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\bar{{\nu}})=(1-2\lambda)\bar{T}_{02}(\bar{{\nu}})\text{ and }\bar{T}_{100}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\bar{{\nu}})=\bar{T}_{10}(\bar{{\nu}})+\bar{T}_{02}(\bar{{\nu}})\text{ for $\lambda(\bar{{\nu}})\neq 1/2.$}

Here
\upsilon
is a small enough positive number depending on
\bar{{\nu}}_{0}.
Furthermore by applying locally Theorem
A.3
we know that the coefficients
\bar{T}_{ij}(\bar{{\nu}})
are meromorphic functions on
\bar{W}
with poles only at those
F>1
such that
\lambda(\bar{{\nu}})=\frac{1}{2(F-1)}\in D_{ij},
where
D_{00}=\emptyset
,
D_{10}=\frac{1}{\mathbb{N}}
,
D_{01}=\mathbb{N}
,
D_{20}=\frac{2}{\mathbb{N}}
and
D_{02}=\frac{\mathbb{N}}{2}
.

We claim that the coefficients
\bar{T}_{ij}(\bar{{\nu}})
do not depend on
\varepsilon.
To prove this observe that the Dulac time
\bar{T}(s;\bar{{\nu}})
does not depend on
\varepsilon
as long as
\bar{{\nu}}\in\bar{W}.
Accordingly
\partial_{\varepsilon}\bar{T}(s;\bar{{\nu}})\equiv 0
. Thus from
(b^{\prime})
we get that, for each fixed
\bar{{\nu}}_{\star}\in\bar{W}\cap\{\frac{5}{4}<F<\frac{3}{2}\}
,

\partial_{\varepsilon}\bar{T}_{00}(\bar{{\nu}}_{\star})+\partial_{\varepsilon}\bar{T}_{10}(\bar{{\nu}}_{\star})s+\partial_{\varepsilon}\bar{T}_{01}(\bar{{\nu}}_{\star})s^{\lambda(\bar{{\nu}}_{\star})}+\partial_{\varepsilon}\bar{T}_{20}(\bar{{\nu}}_{\star})s^{2}+\mbox{\rm o}(s^{L_{0}-2\upsilon})=0\text{ for all $s>0$,}

where
L_{\star}=\lambda(\bar{{\nu}}_{\star})+1
and we use that the flatness order of the remainder is preserved when derived with respect to parameters (see Definition
A.1
). Then, since
1<\lambda<2
for
F\in(\frac{5}{4},\frac{3}{2})
and we can choose
\upsilon>0
arbitrary small (depending on
\bar{{\nu}}_{\star})
, the application of Remark
A.1
shows that

\partial_{\varepsilon}\bar{T}_{00}(\bar{{\nu}}_{\star})=0\text{, }\partial_{\varepsilon}\bar{T}_{10}(\bar{{\nu}}_{\star})=0\text{, }\partial_{\varepsilon}\bar{T}_{01}(\bar{{\nu}}_{\star})=0\text{ and }\partial_{\varepsilon}\bar{T}_{20}(\bar{{\nu}}_{\star})=0.

Since
\bar{W}\cap\{\frac{5}{4}<F<\frac{3}{2}\}
is open and the coefficients are meromorphic on
\bar{W}
, by Lemma
B.1
it follows that
\partial_{\varepsilon}\bar{T}_{00}
,
\partial_{\varepsilon}\bar{T}_{10}
,
\partial_{\varepsilon}\bar{T}_{01}
and
\partial_{\varepsilon}\bar{T}_{20}
are identically zero. The claim for
\partial_{\varepsilon}\bar{T}_{02}
follows verbatim.

Thanks to the claim and the fact that
\left.\bar{T}(s;\bar{{\nu}})\right|_{\eta=0}=T(s;{{\nu}})
by construction, the assertions in
(a)
–
(d)
concerning the asymptotic expansion of
T(s;{{\nu}})
at
s=0
follow from
(a^{\prime})
–
(d^{\prime})
, respectively, by setting

T_{ij}({{\nu}})\!:=\left.\bar{T}_{ij}(\bar{{\nu}})\right|_{\eta=0}.

We proceed next with the computation of these coefficients and for this purpose the idea is that if
{{\nu}}\in W
and
\varepsilon>0
then

T_{ij}({{\nu}})=\bar{T}_{ij}({{\nu}},\varepsilon,0)=\lim_{\eta\to 0^{+}}\bar{T}_{ij}({{\nu}},\varepsilon,\eta)=\lim_{\eta\to 0^{+}}\bar{T}_{ij}({{\nu}},0,\eta),

(35)

where in the second equality we use the continuity of
\bar{T}_{ij}(\bar{{\nu}})
at any
\bar{{\nu}}_{0}\in\bar{W}
with
\lambda(\bar{{\nu}}_{0})=\frac{1}{2(F_{0}-1)}\notin D_{ij}
and in the last one the fact that
\bar{T}_{ij}(\bar{{\nu}})
does not depend on
\varepsilon
. Hence our first goal is to obtain
\bar{T}_{ij}(\bar{{\nu}})
for
\varepsilon=0
and to this end we shall apply Theorem
A.4
. (We point out that from now on all the computations are performed taking
\varepsilon=0
and
\eta>0
.) In doing so, and setting

\kappa_{0}\!:=\frac{p_{2}-1}{p_{2}-p_{1}}

for shortness, from
(\ref{def_fun})
we obtain that
L_{1}(u)=(1-\kappa_{0}u)^{2F}
,
L_{2}(u)\equiv 1
,
M_{1}(u)\equiv 0
and

\begin{array}[]{ll}A_{1}(u)=-\frac{\kappa_{2}}{\kappa_{1}}(1-\kappa_{0}u)^{-1}(1-u)^{-\frac{1}{2}}&A_{2}(u)=\frac{\kappa_{2}}{2\kappa_{1}(F-1)}(1-u)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\\[15.0pt]
B_{1}(u)=-\frac{\kappa_{2}}{2\kappa_{1}}(1-\kappa_{0}u)^{2F-1}(1-u)^{-\frac{3}{2}}&C_{1}(u)=-\frac{3\kappa_{2}}{4\kappa_{1}}(1-\kappa_{0}u)^{4F-1}(1-u)^{-\frac{5}{2}}.\\
\end{array}

(36)

From
(\ref{p2eq2})
and
(\ref{p2eq3})
, the necessary information with regard to the transverse sections is the following:

\textstyle\sigma_{120}=\sigma_{210}=1-\kappa_{2}^{2}\eta^{2},\,\;\sigma_{111}=-\sigma_{121}=\frac{(1-\kappa_{2}^{2}\eta^{2})^{2}}{\kappa_{1}}\text{ and }\sigma_{122}=-\sigma_{112}=\frac{2(1-\kappa_{2}^{2}\eta^{2})^{3}}{\kappa_{1}^{2}}.

(37)

Taking this into account we obtain that

\displaystyle T_{00}({{\nu}})

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{\eta\to 0^{+}}\bar{T}_{00}({{\nu}},0,\eta)=-\lim\limits_{\eta\to 0^{+}}(1-\kappa_{2}^{2}\eta^{2})\hat{A}_{1}(-1,1-\kappa_{2}^{2}\eta^{2})

\displaystyle\textstyle=\frac{\kappa_{2}}{\kappa_{1}}\,\mathrm{B}(1,\frac{1}{2})\,{}_{2}F_{1}(1,1;\frac{3}{2};\kappa_{0})=\frac{2\kappa_{2}}{\kappa_{1}(1-\kappa_{0})}\,{}_{2}F_{1}(1,\frac{1}{2};\frac{3}{2};\frac{\kappa_{0}}{\kappa_{0}-1})

where in the second equality we use Theorem
A.4
, in the third one we apply
(b)
in Proposition
B.3
taking

\{\alpha=-1,\gamma=1,\delta=\frac{1}{2},x=\kappa_{0}\}
and in the last one
[
1
, §15.3]
. Since
\frac{\kappa_{0}}{\kappa_{0}-1}=\frac{1-p_{2}}{1-p_{1}}
, this shows the validity of the expression for
T_{00}
given in the statement.
Similarly, from
(\ref{p2eq4})
and
(\ref{p2eq5})
again,

\displaystyle\textstyle T_{01}({{\nu}})

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{\eta\to 0^{+}}\bar{T}_{01}({{\nu}},0,\eta)

\displaystyle\textstyle=\frac{\kappa_{1}^{-\lambda}}{(1-\kappa_{0})^{2\lambda F}}\lim\limits_{\eta\to 0^{+}}\hat{A}_{2}(\lambda,1-k^{2}_{2}\eta^{2})=\frac{\kappa_{2}(1-\kappa_{0})^{-2\lambda F}}{2\kappa_{1}^{\lambda+1}(F-1)}{\mathrm{B}(-\lambda,\frac{1}{2})}.

Here the last equality follows by applying
(b)
in Proposition
B.3
taking
\{\alpha=\lambda,\delta=\frac{1}{2},x=0\}
, so that
\alpha=\lambda({{\nu}})=\frac{1}{2(F-1)}\notin\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and noting that
{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b;c;0)=1
, see
(\ref{hyper})
. Since
\rho_{1}({{\nu}})\!:=\frac{\kappa_{2}(1-\kappa_{0})^{-2\lambda F}}{2\kappa_{1}^{\lambda+1}(F-1)}
is an analytic positive function on
W
, this proves the the expression for
T_{01}
given in the statement.

Let us study next the coefficient
T_{10}
. For this purpose we apply Theorem
A.4
, which on account of
(\ref{p2eq5})
shows that if
\eta>0
and
\lambda({{\nu}})\notin D_{10}=\frac{1}{\mathbb{N}}
then

\bar{T}_{10}({{\nu}},0,\eta)=\frac{1}{\kappa_{1}}\left(\frac{-1+\kappa_{2}^{2}\eta^{2}}{\eta\,(\kappa_{1}+(p_{2}-1)(1-\kappa_{2}^{2}\eta^{2}))}-\frac{(1-\kappa_{2}^{2}\eta^{2})^{3}}{(1-\kappa_{0}(1-\kappa_{2}^{2}\eta^{2}))^{2F}}\,\hat{B}_{1}\!\left(1/\lambda-1,1-\kappa_{2}^{2}\eta^{2}\right)\right).

(38)

Following the notation in Proposition
B.3
, see
(\ref{p2eq4})
, we can write
B_{1}(1-\kappa_{2}^{2}\eta^{2})=-\frac{\kappa_{2}}{2\kappa_{1}}g(y;\delta,\gamma;x)
with
\{y=1-\kappa_{2}^{2}\eta^{2},\delta=-\frac{1}{2},\gamma=2F,x=\kappa_{0}\}
but we cannot apply it to get the limit of
\hat{B}_{1}\!\left(1/\lambda-1,1-\kappa_{2}^{2}\eta^{2}\right)
as
\eta\to 0^{+}
because the condition
\delta>0
is not satisfied. As a matter of fact this is coherent because, since the first summand in
(\ref{p2eq6})
is divergent as
\eta\to 0^{+},
it happens that
\lim_{\eta\to 0^{+}}\hat{B}_{1}\!\left(1/\lambda-1,1-\kappa_{2}^{2}\eta^{2}\right)
diverges as well. Hence the approach to compute this coefficient hast to be different. The idea is to take advantage of
[
23
, Theorem 3.6]
, which shows that if
F\in(1,\frac{3}{2})
then
T_{10}({{\nu}})=\rho_{2}({{\nu}})\bar{\rho}_{2}({{\nu}})
where

\rho_{2}({{\nu}})\!:=\frac{\kappa_{2}}{2\kappa_{1}(1-p_{1})}\text{ and }\bar{\rho}_{2}({{\nu}})\!:=-2+\int_{0}^{1}\left((1-s)^{-\frac{1}{\lambda}}(1-\bar{\kappa}s)^{1+\frac{1}{\lambda}}-1\right)s^{-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{ds}{s}

with
\bar{\kappa}\!:=\frac{1-p_{2}}{1-p_{1}}.
By applying assertion
(b)
in Theorem
A.5
taking
f(s;{{\nu}})=(1-s)^{-\frac{1}{\lambda}}(1-\bar{\kappa}s)^{1+\frac{1}{\lambda}}
,
k=1
and
\alpha=\frac{1}{2}
we can assert that
\bar{\rho}_{2}({{\nu}})=\lim_{s\to 1^{-}}\hat{f}(\frac{1}{2},s;{{\nu}})
. Observe on the other hand that, following the notation in Proposition
B.3
, we can write
f(s;{{\nu}})=g(y;\delta,\gamma;x)
with
\{y=s,\delta=1-\frac{1}{\lambda},\gamma=-1-\frac{1}{\lambda},x=\bar{\kappa}\}.
Thus, since one can verify that
\delta=1-\frac{1}{\lambda}>0
and
x=\bar{\kappa}<1
for all
{{\nu}}\in W\cap\{1<F<\frac{3}{2}\},
the application of assertion
(b)
in that result gives

\textstyle\bar{\rho}_{2}({{\nu}})=\lim_{y\to 1^{-}}\hat{f}(\frac{1}{2},y;{{\nu}})=\mathrm{B}\left(-\frac{1}{2},1-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right){}_{2}F_{1}\left(-1-\frac{1}{\lambda},-\frac{1}{2};\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\lambda};\bar{\kappa}\right).

Due to
\mathrm{B}(-\frac{1}{2},1-\frac{1}{\lambda})=\mathrm{B}(1-\frac{1}{\lambda},-\frac{1}{2})
, see
(\ref{eqB0})
,
this proves the validity of the expression for
T_{10}({{\nu}})
in the statement for all
{{\nu}}\in W\cap\{1<F<\frac{3}{2}\}.
Accordingly, since
T_{10}
is meromorphic on
W
, this is also the case of
\bar{\rho}_{2}
thanks to Lemma
B.2
, and
\rho_{2}
is analytic on
W
, the application of the real version of Lemma
B.1
implies the validity of the equality on
W
. Observe moreover that
\rho_{2}
is positive on
W.

We proceed with the computation of the coefficient
T_{20}
. In first instance, for the sake of convenience we shall work with
{{\nu}}\in W\cap\{1<F<\frac{5}{4}\},
so that
\lambda({{\nu}})>2.
Due to
M_{1}\equiv 0,
Theorem
A.4
shows that if
\lambda({{\nu}})\notin D_{20}=\frac{2}{\mathbb{N}}
then

\displaystyle\bar{T}_{20}({{\nu}},0,\eta)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sigma_{120}\sigma_{122}}{2\sigma_{120}P_{2}(0,\sigma_{120})}-\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{121}^{2}\partial_{2}P_{2}^{-1}(0,\sigma_{120})-\sigma_{121}\sigma_{111}\partial_{1}P_{2}^{-1}(0,\sigma_{120})

(39)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt-\frac{\sigma_{120}\sigma_{111}^{2}}{2L_{1}^{2}(\sigma_{120})}\hat{C}_{1}(2/\lambda-1,\sigma_{120})-\left(\frac{\sigma_{112}}{2\sigma_{111}}-\frac{\sigma_{121}}{\sigma_{120}}\left(\frac{P_{1}}{P_{2}}\right)\!(0,\sigma_{120})\right)\frac{\sigma_{120}\sigma_{111}}{L_{1}(\sigma_{120})}\hat{B}_{1}(1/\lambda-1,\sigma_{120}).

On account of
\left.P_{2}\right|_{\varepsilon=0}=R\bar{P}_{2}
,
R(0,\sigma_{120})=\frac{1}{\kappa_{2}}\left.\sqrt{1-x_{2}}\right|_{x_{2}=1-\kappa_{2}^{2}\eta^{2}}=\eta
and
\bar{P}_{2}(0,1)=p_{1}-1\neq 0
, see
(\ref{p2eq8})
and
(\ref{p2eq5})
, it follows that we can write

\displaystyle-\frac{\sigma_{120}\sigma_{122}}{2\sigma_{120}P_{2}(0,\sigma_{120})}-\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{121}^{2}\partial_{2}P_{2}^{-1}(0,\sigma_{120})-\sigma_{121}\sigma_{111}\partial_{1}P_{2}^{-1}(0,\sigma_{120})=\frac{\phi_{1}(\eta^{2})}{\eta^{3}}

(40)

and

\displaystyle-\frac{\sigma_{112}}{2\sigma_{111}}+\frac{\sigma_{121}}{\sigma_{120}}\left(\frac{P_{1}}{P_{2}}\right)\!(0,\sigma_{120})=\phi_{2}(\eta^{2}),

(41)

where here (and in what follows)
\phi_{i}(x)
stands for an analytic function at
x=0.
(In fact
\phi_{i}
depends also on
{{\nu}}
and this dependence is analytic on
W
. We omit this dependence for brevity when there is no risk of confusion.) Following this notation, from
(\ref{p2eq6})
and using that
\lim_{\eta\to 0^{+}}L_{1}(1-\kappa_{2}^{2}\eta^{2})=(1-\kappa_{0})^{2F}\neq 0,
we can assert that

\frac{\sigma_{120}\sigma_{111}}{L_{1}(\sigma_{120})}\hat{B}_{1}(1/\lambda-1,\sigma_{120})=\phi_{3}(\eta^{2})\bar{T}_{10}({{\nu}},0,\eta)+\frac{\phi_{4}(\eta^{2})}{\eta}

(42)

as long as
\lambda({{\nu}})\notin D_{10}=\frac{1}{\mathbb{N}}.
On the other hand, since
\lambda({{\nu}})>2
for all
{{\nu}}\in W\cap\{1<F<\frac{5}{4}\},
by applying assertion
(b)
in Theorem
A.5
with
k=0
and
\alpha=\frac{2}{\lambda}-1
, we get

\displaystyle\sigma_{120}^{1-\frac{2}{\lambda}}\hat{C}_{1}{(2/\lambda-1},\sigma_{120})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\sigma_{120}}C_{1}(u)u^{1-\frac{2}{\lambda}}\frac{du}{u}=\int_{0}^{1-\kappa_{2}^{2}\eta^{2}}C_{1}(u)u^{-\frac{2}{\lambda}}du

\displaystyle=-\frac{3\kappa_{2}}{4\kappa_{1}}(1-\kappa_{0})^{4F-1}\underbrace{\int_{\kappa_{2}^{2}\eta^{2}}^{1}(1-\bar{\kappa}x)^{4F-1}x^{-\frac{5}{2}}(1-x)^{-\frac{2}{\lambda}}dx}_{I},

where in the second equality we perform the change of variable
u=1-x
and use that
\frac{\kappa_{0}}{\kappa_{0}-1}=\frac{1-p_{2}}{1-p_{1}}=\bar{\kappa}
. Next we split the above integral as
I=I_{1}+I_{2}
with

I_{1}\!:=\int_{\bar{\kappa}_{2}^{2}\eta^{2}}^{1}\left((1-\bar{\kappa}x)^{4F-1}(1-x)^{-\frac{2}{\lambda}}-(1+\beta x)\right)x^{-\frac{3}{2}}\frac{dx}{x}\text{ and }I_{2}\!:=\int_{\bar{\kappa}_{2}^{2}\eta^{2}}^{1}(1+\beta x)x^{-\frac{3}{2}}\frac{dx}{x},

where we take
\beta\!:=\frac{2}{\lambda}-(4F-1)\bar{\kappa}
so that
I_{1}
converges as
\eta\to 0
. Due to

I_{2}=-\frac{2}{3}-2\beta+\frac{2}{3}\frac{1}{(\kappa_{2}\eta)^{3}}+\frac{2\beta}{\kappa_{2}\eta},
we can write
I=J+\frac{2}{3}\frac{1}{(\kappa_{2}\eta)^{3}}+\frac{2\beta}{\kappa_{2}\eta}
with

J({{\nu}},\eta)\!:=\int_{\kappa_{2}^{2}\eta^{2}}^{1}\left((1-\bar{\kappa}x)^{4F-1}(1-x)^{-\frac{2}{\lambda}}-(1+\beta x)\right)x^{-\frac{3}{2}}\frac{dx}{x}-\frac{2}{3}-2\beta.

Consequently we obtain that

\hat{C}_{1}{(2/\lambda-1},\sigma_{120})=-\frac{3\kappa_{2}}{4\kappa_{1}}\frac{(1-\kappa_{0})^{4F-1}}{(1-\kappa_{2}^{2}\eta^{2})^{1-\frac{2}{\lambda}}}\left(J({{\nu}},\eta)+\frac{2}{3(\kappa_{2}\eta)^{3}}+\frac{2\beta}{\kappa_{2}\eta}\right).

(43)

On the other hand, setting
g(x;{{\nu}})\!:=(1-\bar{\kappa}x)^{4F-1}(1-x)^{-\frac{2}{\lambda}}
we get that

\lim_{\eta\to 0}J({{\nu}},\eta)=\lim_{x\to 1^{-}}\hat{g}(3/2,x;{{\nu}})=\mathrm{B}\left(1-\frac{2}{\lambda},-\frac{3}{2}\right){}_{2}F_{1}\left(1-4F,-\frac{3}{2};-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{\lambda};\bar{\kappa}\right)=:\!J_{0}({{\nu}}).

Here the first equality follows by
(b)
in Theorem
A.5
taking
\{\alpha=\frac{3}{2},k=2\}
and the second one by
(b)
in Proposition
B.3
taking
\{\alpha=\frac{3}{2},\gamma=1-4F,\delta=1-\frac{2}{\lambda},x=\bar{\kappa}\}
and using that
\delta>0
thanks to
\lambda({{\nu}})>2
for all
{{\nu}}\in W\cap\{1<F<\frac{5}{4}\}.
That being said, substituting
(\ref{eq525})
,
(\ref{eq526})
,
(\ref{eq523})
and
(\ref{eq522})
into
(\ref{p2eq7})
and gathering next the analytic functions at
\eta=0
we obtain that

\bar{T}_{20}({{\nu}},0,\eta)=\phi_{5}({{\nu}},\eta^{2})J({{\nu}},\eta)+\phi_{6}({{\nu}},\eta^{2})\bar{T}_{10}({{\nu}},0,\eta)+\phi_{7}({{\nu}},\eta^{2})/\eta^{3},

with
\phi_{i}({{\nu}},x)
analytic at
x=0
. (Here we specify again the dependence on
{{\nu}}
for the sake of consistency in the exposition.) Consequently, from
(\ref{eq521})
,

T_{20}({{\nu}})=\lim_{\eta\to 0^{+}}\bar{T}_{20}({{\nu}},0,\eta)=\phi_{5}({{\nu}},0)J_{0}({{\nu}})+\phi_{6}({{\nu}},0)T_{10}({{\nu}}),

where we use that
\phi_{7}({{\nu}},\eta^{2})=\rm O(\eta^{4})
because the limit must be finite. One can easily check that

\rho_{3}({{\nu}})\!:=\phi_{5}({{\nu}},0)=\frac{3\kappa_{2}}{8\kappa_{1}^{3}(1-\kappa_{0})}\text{ and }\rho_{4}({{\nu}})\!:=\phi_{6}({{\nu}},0)=\frac{p_{1}-1+2F\kappa_{1}}{\kappa_{1}(p_{1}-1)}.

This proves the validity of the expression for
T_{20}({{\nu}})
for all
{{\nu}}\in W\cap\{1<F<\frac{5}{4}\}.
Similarly as before, by applying Lemma
B.1
this equality extends to
W
since
\rho_{3}
and
\rho_{4}
are analytic on
W
and, on the other hand,
J_{0}
is meromorphic on
W
by Lemma
B.2
and so are
\bar{T}_{10}
and
\bar{T}_{20}
. Finally an easy computation shows that
\rho_{3}
and
\rho_{4}
are positive on
W.

So far we have proved the validity of the expression of the coefficients
T_{ij}({{\nu}})
that we give in the first part of the statement. Moreover, since
T(s;{{\nu}})=\bar{T}(s;\bar{{\nu}})|_{\eta=0}
and
T_{ij}({{\nu}})=\bar{T}_{ij}(\bar{{\nu}})|_{\eta=0},
the assertions with regard to the asymptotic expansion of the Dulac map at
s=0
in
(a)
–
(d)
follow, respectively, from
(a^{\prime})
–
(d^{\prime})
. The only remaining point concerns the behaviour of the coefficients in each one of these cases. This is our final task, that we carry out case by case:

(a)

Let us consider any
{{\nu}}_{0}=(D_{0},F_{0})\in W\cap\{1<F<\frac{5}{4}\}
such that
T_{10}({{\nu}}_{0})=0
. We claim that then
D_{0}\in(-1,-\frac{1}{2}).
Indeed, to prove this we first use Proposition 3.11 in
[
23
]
, which shows that the set
\big\{{{\nu}}\in W:T_{10}({{\nu}})=0\text{ with }1<F<\frac{3}{2}\big\}
is the graphic of an analytic function
D=\mathcal{G}(F)
verifying
-F<\mathcal{G}(F)<-\frac{1}{2}
and
\lim_{F\to 1^{+}}\mathcal{G}(F)=-\frac{1}{2}.
Therefore it is clear that the claim will follow once we prove that
T_{10}(-1,F)\neq 0
for all
F\in(1,\frac{5}{4}).
In order to show this we note that
p_{2}|_{D=-1}=1
and, consequently,

\textstyle T_{10}(-1,F)=\left.\rho_{2}({{\nu}})\mathrm{B}\big(1-\frac{1}{\lambda},-\frac{1}{2}\big){}_{2}F_{1}\big(-1-\frac{1}{\lambda},-\frac{1}{2};\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\lambda};\frac{1-p_{2}}{1-p_{1}}\big)\right|_{D=-1}\neq 0

because
{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b;c;0)=1
by definition and, on the other hand,
\lambda({{\nu}})=\frac{1}{2(1-F)}>2
for all
F\in(1,\frac{5}{4})
and one can check that

\mathrm{B}\big(1-\frac{1}{\lambda},-\frac{1}{2}\big)=\frac{\Gamma(1-\frac{1}{\lambda})\Gamma(-\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\lambda})}\neq 0
for all
\lambda>2
. This proves the claim.

Recall at this point that

T_{20}({{\nu}})\textstyle=\rho_{3}({{\nu}})\mathrm{B}\big(1-\frac{2}{\lambda},-\frac{3}{2}\big){}_{2}F_{1}\big(-\frac{2}{\lambda}-3,-\frac{3}{2};-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{\lambda};\frac{1-p_{2}}{1-p_{1}}\big)+\rho_{4}({{\nu}})T_{10}({{\nu}}).

(44)

Accordingly, since
T_{10}({{\nu}}_{0})=0
with
{{\nu}}_{0}\in Q\!:=W\cap\{1<F<\frac{5}{4}\}\cap\{-1<D<-\frac{1}{2}\}
and
\rho_{2}
and
\rho_{3}
are positive functions, in order to prove that
T_{20}({{\nu}}_{0})\neq 0
it suffices to show that the linear combination

\textstyle\mathrm{B}\big(1-\frac{2}{\lambda},-\frac{3}{2}\big){}_{2}F_{1}\big(-\frac{2}{\lambda}-3,-\frac{3}{2};-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{\lambda};\frac{1-p_{2}}{1-p_{1}}\big)-4\mathrm{B}\big(1-\frac{1}{\lambda},-\frac{1}{2}\big){}_{2}F_{1}\big(-1-\frac{1}{\lambda},-\frac{1}{2};\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\lambda};\frac{1-p_{2}}{1-p_{1}}\big)

does not vanish on
Q
. Since one can easily verify that
\frac{2}{\lambda}\in(0,1)
and
\frac{1-p_{2}}{1-p_{1}}\in(-1,0)
for all
{{\nu}}\in Q
, this follows directly by applying Proposition
C.1
with
\{a=\frac{2}{\lambda},b=-\frac{1-p_{2}}{1-p_{1}}\}.

(b)

We already proved that
T_{01}({{\nu}})=\rho_{1}({{\nu}})\mathrm{B}\big(-\lambda,\frac{1}{2}\big)
with
\rho_{1}
an analytic positive function on
W
. The function
\mathrm{B}(-\lambda,\frac{1}{2})=\frac{\Gamma(-\lambda)\Gamma(\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-\lambda)}
vanishes only when
\frac{1}{2}-\lambda({{\nu}})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
and, for
{{\nu}}\in W\cap\{\frac{5}{4}<F<\frac{3}{2}\}
, this occurs if and only if
\lambda({{\nu}})=\frac{3}{2},
i.e.,
F=\frac{4}{3}.

Recall moreover that, by Proposition 3.11 in
[
23
]
, the set
\big\{{{\nu}}\in W:T_{10}({{\nu}})=0\text{ with }1<F<\frac{3}{2}\big\}
is the graphic of an analytic function
D=\mathcal{G}(F)
on
(1,\frac{3}{2}).
Consequently there exists a unique
{{\nu}}_{\star}=(D_{\star},\frac{4}{3})
inside

W\cap\{\frac{5}{4}<F<\frac{3}{2}\}
such that
T_{10}({{\nu}}_{\star})=0
and one can prove that
D_{\star}\in(-1.15,-1.10)
. The gradients of
T_{10}
and
T_{01}
are linearly independent at
{{\nu}}_{\star}
because
\partial_{D}T_{10}({{\nu}})\neq 0
for all
{{\nu}}\in W\cap\{1<F<\frac{3}{2}\}
by
(a)
of Lemma 3.13 in
[
23
]
and, on the other hand,
\partial_{D}T_{01}({{\nu}}_{\star})=\mathrm{B}\big(-\lambda,\frac{1}{2}\big)|_{{{\nu}}={{\nu}}_{\star}}\partial_{D}\rho_{1}({{\nu}}_{\star})=0
and
\partial_{F}T_{10}({{\nu}}_{\star})\neq 0
since the gamma function has simple poles at
\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
with non-zero residue. Finally
T_{20}({{\nu}}_{\star})<0
follows noting that, from
(\ref{p2eq9})
and
\lambda({{\nu}}_{\star})=\frac{3}{2}
, we get

\textstyle T_{20}({{\nu}}_{\star})=\rho_{3}({{\nu}}_{\star})\left.\mathrm{B}\big(-\frac{1}{3},-\frac{3}{2}\big){}_{2}F_{1}\big(-\frac{13}{3},-\frac{3}{2};-\frac{11}{6};\frac{1-p_{2}}{1-p_{1}}\big)\right|_{{{\nu}}={{\nu}}_{\star}},

which is negative because one can easily check that
D\mapsto{}_{2}F_{1}\big(-\frac{13}{3},-\frac{3}{2};-\frac{11}{6};\frac{1-p_{2}}{1-p_{1}}\big)|_{F=\frac{4}{3}}
is positive on
(-1.15,-1.10)
and
\mathrm{B}\big(-\frac{1}{3},-\frac{3}{2}\big)\approx-2.6.

(c)

Let us fix any
{{\nu}}_{0}\in W\cap\{F=\frac{5}{4}\},
so that
\lambda({{\nu}}_{0})=2.
Then, from
(c^{\prime}),

T_{201}^{2}({{\nu}})\!:=\bar{T}_{201}^{2}(\bar{{\nu}})|_{\eta=0}
and
T_{200}^{2}({{\nu}})\!:=\bar{T}_{200}^{2}(\bar{{\nu}})|_{\eta=0}
are smooth functions in a neighbourhood of
\{{{\nu}}\in W:\lambda({{\nu}})=2\}
and, in addition,

T_{201}^{2}({{\nu}})=\big(2-\lambda({{\nu}})\big)T_{01}({{\nu}})\text{ and }T_{200}^{2}({{\nu}})=T_{20}({{\nu}})+T_{01}({{\nu}})\text{ for $\lambda({{\nu}})\neq 2.$}

The functions
T_{01}({{\nu}})
and
T_{20}({{\nu}})
are meromorphic with a pole
at those
{{\nu}}
such that
\lambda({{\nu}})=2\in D_{01}\cap D_{20}
, and the pole is simple in both cases by Propositions 3.2 and 3.6 in
[
31
]
, respectively. Therefore by the Weierstrass Division Theorem (or, more directly, by
[
31
, Lemma 2.8]
) it follows that
T_{201}^{2}({{\nu}})
and
T_{200}^{2}({{\nu}})
are analytic in a neighbourhood of
W\cap\{F=\frac{5}{4}\}
.
Furthermore, by
[
23
, Lemma 3.13]
once again,
T_{10}(D,\frac{5}{4})=0
if and only if
D=-1
. Finally, since
\lambda(-1,\frac{5}{4})=2,

T_{01}({{\nu}})=\rho_{1}({{\nu}})\mathrm{B}(-\lambda,\frac{1}{2})
and
\mathrm{B}(-\lambda,\frac{1}{2})=\frac{\Gamma(-\lambda)\Gamma(\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-\lambda)}
, we have that

T_{201}^{2}(-1,5/4)=\lim_{{{\nu}}\to(-1,\frac{5}{4})}\big(2-\lambda({{\nu}})\big)T_{01}({{\nu}})=\rho_{1}(-1,5/4)\textstyle\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(-\frac{3}{2})}\lim\limits_{\lambda\to 2}(2-\lambda)\Gamma(-\lambda)\neq 0,

because the gamma function has simple poles with non-zero residues at
\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}.

(d)

Let us consider finally any
{{\nu}}_{0}\in W\cap\{F=2\},
so that
\lambda({{\nu}}_{0})=\frac{1}{2}.
Due to
T_{01}({{\nu}})=\rho_{1}({{\nu}})\mathrm{B}\big(-\lambda,\frac{1}{2}\big)
with
\rho_{1}\neq 0
,
\lambda({{\nu}})=\frac{1}{2(F-1)}
and
\mathrm{B}\big(-\lambda,\frac{1}{2}\big)=\frac{\Gamma(-\lambda)\Gamma(\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-\lambda)}
, there exists an analytic non-vanishing function
\ell_{1}
in a neighbourhood of
W\cap\{F=2\}
such that
T_{01}({{\nu}})=(F-2)\ell_{1}({{\nu}}).
On the other hand, from
(d^{\prime})
and arguing as in the previous case, the functions
T_{101}^{\frac{1}{2}}({{\nu}})\!:=\bar{T}_{101}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\bar{{\nu}})|_{\eta=0}
and
T_{100}^{\frac{1}{2}}({{\nu}})\!:=\bar{T}_{100}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\bar{{\nu}})|_{\eta=0}
are analytic in a neighbourhood of
\{{{\nu}}\in W:\lambda({{\nu}})=\frac{1}{2}\}
and

T_{101}^{\frac{1}{2}}({{\nu}})=(1-2\lambda)T_{02}({{\nu}})\text{ and }T_{100}^{\frac{1}{2}}({{\nu}})=T_{10}({{\nu}})+T_{02}({{\nu}})\text{ for $\lambda({{\nu}})\neq 1/2.$}

In particular we have that the sum of residues of
T_{10}
and
T_{02}
along
\{{{\nu}}\in W:\lambda({{\nu}})=\frac{1}{2}\}=W\cap\{F=2\}

is equal to zero,
